This is the entral aruent o Marx. We have to ove beyon tieless hoin to unerstan the raility o aitalis. This raility is ouse in risis. Marxis, as I unerstan it, is a theory o risis. Historiity ilies a notion o risis, a oent in whih the relations o the ol worl are broken an the new atterns ush throuh. Marx onnets this ortality o aital with the reurrent isrutions that are tyial o aitalist eveloent. In risis our subetive hoin (our subetive reatin o a ierent worl, our strules aainst aitalis) eets the hoeability, the raility or breakability o aitalis. Eah risis is a breakin o the relations o the ol worl an a real or otential ushin orwar o the relations o a new worl. "Think hoe, think risis" is a hallene or us to ask, "In what sense an we say that the resent risis is a breakin o the relations o the ol worl an a ushin orwar o the relations o a new worl?"

In risis, hoe eets hoeability. We shoul be anin or oy an lauhin at the oent, seein the iiulties o aital. But are we? Yes an no. There is ertainly an eleent o oyous rae, a elebration o hoe in Arentina, Greee, Turkey. Chaulin. But the ore establishe iae o risis is ust the oosite: a erio o eression. We seak o the last reat risis o aitalis as the Great Deression.

Greee is the eret exale. The ost eroious attak by aital in any so-alle avane ountry in reent years has been onronte by the ost ilitant let in Euroe with assive eonstration ater assive eonstration, with violent onrontations with the olie, a hue nuber o builins in the ity burne, the iainative reation o all sorts o raks, reiurations o another worl, an what has been the reation o the Greek an other states an inee o aital in eneral as exresse throuh the oney arkets? None, total losure, total autis, ust as i all the strules ha never taken lae, as i the rotesters were no ore than lies to be swatte away or ut in rison. An now we have, on the sie o aital, the return to the bon arket, to aitalist resetability, an on our sie, ossibly the srea o eression as the iiult onitions o lie ain hol. Where is hoe there? Where is the raility o aital? What we see here is the arroane an violene o aital.

To think hoe is to think ro the ush o the unborn worl. To think risis is to think ro the raility o the ol worl, that utri worl that has alreay one well ast its sell-by ate. But the eetin o the two oes not see to work. An yet it has to work: the ush or a new worl has to eet the raility o the ol one i revolution is to be ossible.

### V

Can Marx hel us here? Perhas not in his resentation o risis in Capital, whih akes the risis see external to us, but yes, in the substane o what he says. My unerstanin o Marx on risis is that he is sayin that aital as a historially seii oe o oination (an in istintion ro other oes o oination) suers ro a ruial illness that onstitutes its eroity, its instability, an its ortality. That illness is its own insatiability, the hroni, onvulsive, an rowin inaequay o its own oination over the worl.

All ors o oination suer ro a hroni roble: the eenene o the rulers on the rule, their eenene on bein able to ake the rule o what the rulers want the to. In aitalis, that eenene aquires a new iension: ro bein a onstant roble, it beoes a real illness. Unlike other ors o oination, aital is unable to exist on the basis o a stable relation o oination/exloitation. To reroue itsel, it ust onstantly intensiy its oination/exloitation. The at that it is not able to o so aequately onstitutes its risis.

In Capital, Marx introues this onstant rive to intensiy by sayin that value is easure by the soially neessary labor tie require to roue a ooity. This soially neessary labor tie is onstantly allin, so there is a onstant reorulation o the labor neessary to roue value, what Marx alls "abstrat labor," a onstant restruturin o the onversion o our huan ativity into labor that roues aital or suorts the roution o aital. Value, the struturin o soial relations on the basis o exhane, involves a onstantly intensiyin attak on huan ativity an on the whole attern o soial relations an relations between huans an nature o whih that ativity is art. This oes u aainst onstant resistane, resistane that an easily overlow into rebellion. Caital lees into ahinery, to relae the reratory han o labor, as Marx uts it, but this is no solution, beause aital then has to intensiy exloitation in orer to over the osts o the ahines. Caital's inability to o so suiiently leas, Marx arues, to the teneny or the rate o roit to all an to risis—both as the hroni ina equay o aital's exloitation (i.e., aital's aaity to suborinate huan ativity to its nees) an as the erioi exlosion o this inaequay as the ruture o existin soial relations an the strule by aital to restruture the aorin to its nees. Crisis as hroni inaequay eres in oern aitalis into the erioi isrution, so that the ter "the urrent risis o aitalis" oes (at least sine the i-1970s) to reer to a hroni onition untuate by intensiiations.

I o not ean that aital's ontrol over the worl is weakenin—quite the ontrary, unortunately—but that, at the sae tie as its ontrol is rowin, this ontrol is inreasinly inaequate to ensure its own reroution. Growin oination oinies with rowin inaequay o oination. This sees to e what Marx was exressin in his insistene in Capital that a rowin ass o roit oinies with a allin rate o roit. It is iortant to kee both o these trens in view: i we see only the rowin oination o aital, this an easily lea us to esair, but i we see only the rowin inaequay o that oination, that an lea us to an unustiie otiis. Hoe an essiis walk han in han: rea Bloh with one eye an Aorno with the other.

Kee the two toether, but erhas we nee to ous ore on the ina equay o aitalist oination, or we are that inaequay; we are the risis o aital. We are the risis o aital, an rou o it. It is our resistane, our rebellion, our reusal to be onverte into ahines, our lauhter, our love, our rienshi, our rive to o thins in a ierent way, to eaniate our reative aaities, our ore o roution ro the loi o aital: it is all that that stans in the way o aital's eserate rive to intensiy its oination o all huan lie.

We are the risis o aital, an what oes aital o to overoe its risis? First, it oes everythin ossible to intensiy its ontrol over us, over our aily ativity, over the worl in whih we live. But seon, it is ore to reonize that even with this intensiiation, even with all it has ahieve in the estrution o huanity an the estrution o the lanet, it is not enouh. For the last eihty years or so, but to an ever-inreasin extent, this reonition o its own inaequay has beoe a entral harateristi o the existene o aital. The reonition o the inaequay o its rule takes the or o a ae o "let's reten": "let's reten that our oination o hu an ativity is suiient to allow us to auulate ore an ore aital; let's reate a onetary reresentation that orresons to this worl that we esire; let's exan reit an kee oin that way." A ititious worl is reate that is always a bet on the uture roution o the surlus value that iht ustiy the bet, a bet on bein able to exloit ore eetively, on bein able to suborinate all huan ativity ore eetively to the loi o aital. But even with all the vitories o aital in reent years, this exetation has not been ully realize, so the ititious worl rows an rows an rows. An as it rows it beoes ore unstable, ore volatile, ore violent, ore rone to onvulsions that hit the eole o ierent arts o the lanet ore or less at rano: Greee aain, o ourse, or Sain, Irelan, Italy, Arentina, an so on an on, ore an ore. A nasty, ititious worl o ebt that has seee into all our lives, kees us awake at niht, an—absurity o absurities—now onstitutes a entral art o the exeriene o university euation in ore an ore ountries.

We are the inaequay o aital; we are its risis. Our insuborination resonates within aital as its risis. The roble is that the eho o our strenth oes bak at us as soethin alien. We o into a ave an all out, an a ultitue o voies oe bak at us: we o not know that it is our eho an are terriie an all on our knees an ry out or ery. That is what haens to us in risis: our own insuborination oes bak at us as a terriyin alien ore, an we bow own. What shoul be an exeriene o lauhter an oy turns into one o terror.

Our hoe, the hoe that rives illions o strules in all the worl, the hoe that rives the illions o weavins o another worl, eets the hoeability-raility o the worl in the risis o aital an oes not reo nize it, oes not reonize it as its own rout, as the result o the oin toether o illions o resistanes in all the worl.

What an we o to hane this, to brin about a sel-reonition? An how oul we exress this sel-reonition ratially in suh a way as to strenthen the oveent o hoe, hoe in our ability to reate a very i erent worl? I o not have the answer, but I o think it is iortant. Crisis oes at us as neessity, as a ore that ioses isiline, as a ore that akes us lon or noral relations o oination. We have to in a way o exressin it as the exression o our own strenth an huanity.

I it soeties sees that the olete isaearane o aitalis is still a lon way o, it is even learer that aital is very ar ro havin resolve its risis. This oes not ean a sooth transition—ust the oosite. Caital, i it ontinues to exist, is likely to be wrake by ever ore violent rises, ever ore eserate attets to ake real its itional oination o the worl. Our strule is to ake lear that its weakness is our strenth an to use that strenth to weave an weave an weave the ierent worls that oul yet burst throuh the isaster that is aitalis.

This is the anerous roun on whih hoe exists. That is why we nee to think hoe, think risis.

This oes not ean that we have to aet it as it is, quite the oosite: it is ruial to reeine its oe o eveloent, its oetenes, its eans, an its ossible articulations in a democratic environment. To do so, struggles for freedoms must also be given new instruments to take us forward:

- 1. Ad hoc interventions in practical affairs where rights and freedoms are undermined
- 2. Longer-term activities, such as liaising with groups of lawyers, magistrates, social workers, and prisoners, in view of developing alternative forms of systems of justice

The struggles that defend the respect of the law and the offensive struggles aimed at conquering new realms of freedom are complementary. Both are set to become at least as important as trade union and political struggles, and to influence them more and more. This is the process that is apparently unfolding in France, with the growing role played by organizations such as Amnesty International, the League of Human Rights, France Terre d'Asile, and the Cimade.

Despite the above premises, we still cannot treat the evolution of freedoms in Europe as something in itself separate from the context of international tensions and world economic crises. But as soon as I mention these two things, a new question starts humming in my ears. Should we regard these tensions and crises as *causes* of the weakening of freedoms or, inversely, as the *outcome* of the rise of conservativism and reactionarism that followed the 1960s wave of struggles for freedoms? What I'd like to demonstrate is that our analysis of the tension between East and West and the world crisis would gain considerable ground if we reconsidered them from the perspective of this question on freedoms.

I sometimes wonder whether in our societies, imprudently known as "postindustrial," these freedoms are not destined to be irreversibly eroded by some kind of global rise in the entropy of social control. But this morose sociologism earns me nothing but days of depression! On dispassionate reflection, I see no reason to blame this repression on the proliferation of the mechanisms of information and communication in the machineries of production and social life. No! What distorts everything is something else! It is not techno-scientific "progress" but the inertia of outmoded social relations: international relations between blocs and this permanent arms race that sucks the blood out of the economy and anesthetizes its spirits! So I would be inclined to say that the international tension is probably less the result of a fundamental antagonism between two superpowers—as we are led to believe—than a means for them to actually "discipline" the planet. In short, two chief gendarmes hold complementary roles, but not as in a puppet show, because here the blows really hurt! So the overall tension of the system rows an the hierarhial eleents o its ilitary, eonoi, soial, an ultural wins beoe exaerbate. U there, in the Olyus o the Gos o War, uh noise an any threats are ae—as well as, unortunately, any very anerous thins too!—so that at the botto, at all levels, the lunkies are ket silent!

In this reset, the eense o iniviual an olletive reeos never was a serious issue in the onlit-rien relations between the East an the West, an this is iniative. With rolaations an the arain o reat riniles ut asie, it beoes aarent how little this issue weihs on the iortant international "eals" (Presient Jiy Carter anae to riiule hisel beore the Aerian olitial lass by insistin ore than was ustoary on this subet!). The outoe is there; we an alreay see it! That is, a rowin sereation that enerates ethni, sexual, an ae isriination, reater reeo o ation or the ast o bosses an anaers, an ore subserviene ro the awns at the ounations o the bi aitalist ae. The eline o reeos aetin ore or less the whole worl is ainly ue to the rowth o ore onservative an untionalist onetions o the worl. We nee to realize that this reression was ae ossible only by the olitial onuntion o the Western boureoisie, "soialist" bureauraies, an the orrut "elites" o the thir worl, whih toether or a new iure o aitalis that I elsewhere eine as "Interate Worl Caitalis."

The risis an reeos—o ourse they are relate! But, even so, isn't the resentation o this roble in the ainstrea ass eia larely istorte? Is this risis weihin on our reeos, or rather, is it olletive assivity, eoralization, isorientation, an the lak o oranization o otentially innovative ores to leave the iel oen or a new "wil aitalis" to onvert roit into soially evastatin eets? On the one han, the ter crisis is artiularly ill-suite to enote the nature o the series o atastrohes that has been shakin the worl, an riarily the thir worl, or the ast ten years. On the other han, it woul be o letely illeitiate to irusribe these henoena to the eonoi shere alone. Hunres o illions o huan beins are starvin to eath, billions o iniviuals are sinkin into isery an esair year ater year, an this is resente an exlaine to us as an eonoi roble that annot be ore aste until the en o the risis! Nothin an be one about it! This risis alls ro the sky; it oes an oes, like the hail o Hurriane Hortense! Only the oens—these aous an istinuishe eonoists—oul ossibly have soethin to say about it. But i there is a lae where absurity turns into inay, this is it! Beause in the en, what nee woul there be to assoiate inustrial an eonoi restruturin—alie on a worl sale an enae in the eeest reoranization o the eans o roution an soiety—with suh a ess? We nee a 180-eree turn in the way we think throuh these robles, an urently. The olitial takes reeene over the eonoi, not the other way aroun! Even thouh uner resent irustanes it woul be iiult to assert that the olitial anuatures the risis ro srath—insoar as it roues siilar eets an atastrohi interations that eole no loner ontrol, or exale, between eonoi evastations an environental isasters, or, in another real, between the onetary syste an the oil arket—there isn't uh ore to be hel resonsible or the ost erniious soial eets. An the en o the risis, or, i you reer, o this series o isasters, either will be olitial an soial or won't haen at all, an huanity will ontinue to ake her way towar who knows what last ilosion! Where oes Euroe stan in all this? Euroe is oten hel u as a lan o reeo an ulture, so its voation ouht to be to stabilize relations between the East an the West an initiate the rootion o a new international orer between the North an the South. While it is true that its Geran sie reently starte revealin all its interests in alin thins own, we are still very ar ro an autonoous an oherent Euro ean oliy. All the ore so as Frane retreats into its traitional role o the Don Quixote o the rotetion o Western roress! In at, Euroe's reeo to at reues, like shareen, as it beoes ore aarent that Euroe is not oin to eere unsathe ro this hue attet at restruturation o worl aitalis. Euroe's eet an hans reain tie to the eonoi an onetary axioati stratey o the Unite States. More than ever, Euroe is entanle in what the tehnorats lai to be nationalist an statist "ar haiss" an all sorts o "ororatis." In orer to evelo a unitary ove ent within the eole who it is eant to unite, the Euroean Eonoi

Counity has unearthe an eeene the very hatres we thouht ha ie out or a lon tie, an to ake atters worse the whole o its Meiterranean lank slowly shits towar an intereiary kin o thir worl status.

Freeo is a riht, above all! But not a veste riht, at least. Conrete reeos kee lutuatin alon the ath o ower relations aorin to whether they are renoune or reaire. In this reset, to avoi eneralities an abstrations, it woul be better to talk about degrees of freedom or, rather, about differential coefficients of freedom. Huan reeo has never existe all in one iee. Even in the borerline ase o the solitue o ivory towers, reeo is only establishe in relation to others—startin ro the bloks o ientity interete in the sel. In ratie, reeos only unravel in relation to the rihts establishe with lose riens an neihbors, in relation to the suborination o those who are in y ower, to the eets o intiiation an inluene o the authorities that oinate e an, inally, in relation to the rules, oes, an laws o ierent ubli oains. Just as the status o ree itizen was establishe on the bakroun o eneralize slavery in anient ties, so o the reeos o Euroean white aults with a iniu inoe at their isosal in their "stanin" on the roun o the enslaveent o the thir worl toay, both internally an externally. That is to say, in Frane, or instane, the ost eleentary wish to een the rihts o iirants or rotet the riht to olitial asylu, even i evoi o out ate olitial theories or eanatin ro sile harity, oul en u takin us very ar beause it uts uner question not only the reset o oral rihts but a whole onetion o the worl, o ruial axios o sereation, rais, withrawal, ieoloy o seurity, an the ersetive o a Euroe o olie rather than a Euroe o reeos.

Reset o huan rihts in the East as in the West, in the North as in the South; eae an isaraent iose on states throuh new waves o " aiist eoralization"; the establishent, aon the wealthy thir worl ountries, o relations that share the oal o ontributin to the eveloent o huan otential: these oul be the ain international axes o a new soial ratie or the eaniation an onquest o saes o reeos. But these issues annot ee into a boy o eaninul strules unless those who wish to at on the in ratie areiate the ouble nature o the obstales that interate worl aitalis ooses to their roet—naely:

- 1. An obetive aversity that is onstantly evolvin ue to the aelerate transorations o eans o roution an soial relations
- 2. A subetive stueation an a veritable inustrial roution o iniviual an olletive subetivity, that ensures the ost ori able eiieny an obeiene

Before going any further I now wish to recall the conditions that future militant actions and machines of struggle for peace and freedom in all their forms need to be ready for. In my opinion—and I do not claim to have an exhaustive definition and a proposal that is "ready to go"—we need to draw some lessons from the auspicious period of the 1960s and the defeat that followed it. We were naïve, disorganized, indiscriminate, and well informed, sometimes sectarian and narrow-minded, but often visionaries and oriented toward the future; obviously a future that would not resemble the image of our dreams! But I am convinced that we are faced again with a set of problems of method reminiscent of the ones of the struggles and organization of those times, and some lessons can be drawn from experience, the experiences to which some people sacrificed their best years. I see these conditions as follows:

- 1. New social practices of liberation will not establish hierarchical relations between themselves; their development will answer to a principle of *transversality* that will enable them to be established by "traversing," as a "rhizome," heterogeneous social groups and interests. The pitfalls to avoid are these:
 - a. The reconstitution of "vanguard" and major state parties that dictate their law and mold their collective desires in a way that parallels—though formally antagonizes—that of a dominant system. The inefficiency and pernicious character of this kind of dispositif is no longer in need of demonstration.
 - b. The compartmentalization of militant practices and the singling out and separation between practices with political objectives of different scope, from the defense of sectarian interests to the transformation of everyday life . . . and the separation between, on the one hand, programmatic and theoretical reflection and, on the other hand, an analytics of subjectivity of groups and individuals concretely engaged in action, which is to be invented from scratch.

This character of transversality of new social practices—the refusal of authoritarian disciplines, formal hierarchies, orders of priorities decreed from above, and compulsory ideological references—should not be seen in contradiction with the obviously inevitable, necessary, and desirable establishment of *centers of decision* that use the most sophisticated technologies of communication and aim to maximum efficaciousness if necessary. The whole question here is to promote analytical collective procedures

- that allow for the dissociation of the work of decision from the imaginary investments of power; these only coincide in capitalist subjectivity because the latter lost its dimensions of singularity and converted into what might be called an Eros of equivalence (little does it matter the nature of my power, since I dispose of a certain capital of abstract power).
- 2. One of the main goals of new social practices of liberation will be the development of more than a simple protection: collective and/ or individual processes of singularization. These are meant to include everything that confers to these initiatives a character of living subjectivation and irreplaceable experience that is worth being lived, that gives meaning to life. After iron decades of Stalinism, numerous returns to power of the social democrats—with the selfsame scenario of compromise, spinelessness, impotence, and defeat—and the narrow-minded and dishonest Boy Scout attitude of small groups, militancy ended up being impregnated with a rancid smell of church that has come to arouse a legitimate movement of rejection. Only its reinvention of new themes that start from a dissident subjectivity carried out by groups-subjects will make it possible to conquer again the abandoned terrains currently left to the prefabricated subjectivities of the media and infrastructures of this new-look capitalism. And here we reiterate the need to invent a collective analytics of different forms of "engaged" subjectivities. In this respect, we do not start completely from scratch. We have much to learn from the way the Greens in Germany or Solidarnosc in Poland have successfully managed to build new forms of militant life. We also have negative and inverse examples, such as the sectarianism of the Basque military ETA or the monstrous terrorist and dogmatic deviations of the Red Brigades in Italy that have inexorably led to the decapitation of the movement of liberation that had indisputably been the richest and most promising in Europe.

I repeat: the only means to avoid this deadly calamity is to provide the means of an analytical management of the processes of singularization or the "making dissidence" of subjectivity.

3. These mutating militant machines for transversal and singularized spaces of freedom will not have any claim to durability. This way, they will come to terms with their intrinsic precariousness and the need for their continuous renewal, supported by a longlasting social movement of great scope.

This will lea the to ore new and large alliances that will ake the avoi their ost serious inantile isease: a tenaious roensity to exerienin onesel as a inority uner siee. Here it is a ase o rootin a loi o ultivalent allianes that avoi both the uliitous obinations o ower an the urist an setarian ynais o the oveents o the 1960s that le to its einitive searation ro the oulation en asse. # 1.3 The Theory of State-Capitalism

### PIIC d sCI

It was the ontention o Corae [Leon] Trotsky that the existene o stati ie roerty in Russia was suiient to haraterize it as a workers' state, re arless o the olitial reie in ower. Corae [Max] Shahtan asks: "I the workers are no loner the rulin lass an the Soviet Union no loner a workers' state, an i there is no rivate-roerty-ownin aitalist lass rulin Russia, what is the lass nature o the state an what exatly is the bureauray that oinates it?" The eterinin ator in analyzin the lass nature o a soiety is not whether the eans o roution are the rivate roerty o the aitalist lass or are state-owne, but whether the eans o roution are aital, that is, whether they are onoolize an alienate ro the iret ro uers. State aitalis, Trotsky ontene, oes not exist in Russia sine the ownershi o the eans o roution by the state ourre in history by the roletariat with the etho o soial revolution an not by the aitalist with the etho o state trustiiation. But oes the anner in whih a thin is aolishe eterine the use to whih it is ut by its usurers any ore than eah task to be aolishe eterines the rou to exeute it? "The boureois harater o a revolution," wrote Trotsky in oleiizin aainst the Menshevik thesis that sine the Russian Revolution was a boureois revolution the roletariat ouht to renoune ower in avor o the boureoisie, " oul not answer in avane the question as to whih lass woul solve the tasks o the eorati revolution." Its task was aolishe by the asses with the etho o soial revolution. However, the task o the youn roletarian rulers was reatly oliate by the bakwarness o Russia, an the treahery o the Soial-Deoray let the unaie by the worl roletariat. Finally, the Stalinist ounterrevolution ientiie itsel with the state. The anner in whih the eans o roution were onverte into state roerty i not erive the o their beoin aital.

To rove that the artiular state-onooly aitalis existin in Russia i not oe about throuh state trustiiation but by ethos o soial revolution exlains its histori oriin but oes not rove that its eonoi law o otion iers ro that analyze by Karl Marx, [Frierih] Enels, an Lenin. It is hih tie to evaluate "the eonoi law o otion o oern soiety" as it alies to the Soviet Union an not erely to retain or statiie roerty the sae "suerstitious reverene" the oortunists entertaine or the boureois state.

# dfs f h CPIIs sCI Is Ig I ssI

Beause we i not learly unerstan the lass nature o the resent Soviet state, the Soviet Union's interal artiiation in the Seon Ierialist Worl War ae as a onstrous surrise. The Re Ary arh on Polan, the blooy onquest o art o Finlan, an the eaeul onquest o the Balti states rove that the Stalinize Re Ary ha no ore onnetion with the sirit, urose, an ontent o Otober than has the Stalinist state, whose are iht it is. What an abhorrent relase ro the onquests o Otober are the Stalinist onquests!

Lon beore the outbreak o Worl War II the Russian asses bore the brunt o this "abhorrent relase." The worker ha a irst reonition o it when as a Let Oositionist he ouht the Theriorians who erive hi o his ob alon with his Counist Party ebershi ar. The li er o hoe that he ha when the Stalinist bureauray nevertheless aote the Oosition lank or inustrialization an olletivization ae as soon as he realize that the eveloent o the routive ores i not raise his stanar o livin. He learne quikly enouh that the "soialist atherlan" knew how to auulate or other uroses. He woul have elt the rin o Stakhanovis i the nae ha not been Russiie or hi but ha the oriinal For-Taylor see-u insinia. To all the ieework syste, whih is best suite to aitalist exloitation, "soialist workin nors" oes not lihten the eree o exloitation o the briklayer who has to lay sixteen thousan briks er ay, or or a tyist (i I ay be eritte a ettyboureois interest in y own trae) to tye orty-ive aes o thirty lines eah an sixty strokes in eah line er ay. Dereein "universal, reehan equal suffrage" does not make it possible for the fourteen-year-old to vote "no" to being conscripted in the labor reserves, "educated" (read: taught a trade), and at the end of the two-year training program, being put to work on state enterprises to work for four consecutive years—even if this newly educated sixteen-year-old is guaranteed "the established wage rate." It is not only that the income of the factory worker is 110 rubles a month, and that of the director 1,200 a month, but that the whole mode of production produces and reproduces the capitalist production relations. State capitalism, it is true, but capitalism nevertheless. Could we have forgotten that state property forms (and it is only form, not relation, for it is without control by the masses) are the aim of proletarian revolution only as a means to achieve the quicker the fullest development of the productive forces the better to satisfy the needs of man?

No, the existence of statified property in Russia does not make its defense imperative even were the Soviet Union attacked by other imperialist nations for purposes of abolishing statified property (which is less likely just now than the Stalinist state joining the "new order" of Hitler)—unless we are to change our policy and call for the defense of, say, France because the work of the German fascists in dividing the country is of a decidedly retrogressive character.

It is the irrationality of Shachtman's characterization of the class nature of the Soviet Union as "bureaucratic state socialism" that leads him to expound a conditional defense of the present Soviet state. It is the real economic relations behind that irrational expression that leads to no defense of the capitalist society existing in Russia.

# **1.4** Death, Freedom, and the Disintegration of Communism

## Raya Dunayevskaya

Death and starvation stalked the streets of Hungary as the rebel radio sent out its last SOS. "We are quiet. Not afraid. Send the news to the world."

The news to the world about five days of freedom revealed more than courageous fighting.

It showed that you cannot kill the idea of freedom. That idea does not float in heaven. People live by that idea.

Overnight the one-party system disintegrated and various political parties reappeared along with small newspapers and radio stations.

Peasants and soldiers united with city workers—spearheaded by the incredible youth who braved Russian tanks.

Hundreds of local and district organizations, from the Hungarian Revolutionary Youth Party to old parties, including both smallholders and social democrats, appeared.

So total was the wrath of the people against Russian Communism that the Hungarian Communist Party tried to appear under a new guise. The temporary puppet leader, János Kádár, reorganized it as the Socialist Workers Party, but no one took that seriously. Indeed it was the same old communism that, while promising withdrawal of the Russian troops and a different way of living, was conspiring to bring back the Russian tanks and troops in force.

### THEY WILL NOT DISAPPEAR

The revolutionary forces now unloosed cannot be overcome by sheer force. They may be forced underground but they will not disappear. Nor will their impact be exhausted within the national boundaries of Hungary.

Already, in Western Europe, we see the beginning of the disintegration of the mass communist parties. Ever since the end of World War II, the West European people—veering sharply against the private capitalism that they knew and hated because it had brought them two world wars in one lifetime—had turned to Russian Communism, literally by the millions.

They now see Russian Communism as but another name for state capitalism. They are tearing up their Communist Party membership cards by the thousands. The question is: Where will they go now that they see both poles of world capital—United States and Russia—striving for world domination? I will take up this question in my next column. For now, we must stress that this tearing up of the C. P. membership cards stands on a par with the Hungarian Revolution itself in showing a road to freedom out of the totalitarian stranglehold.

# **1.5** Revolution and Counterrevolution in Hungary

## Raya Dunayevskaya

"Dussian soldiers, go home!" has become the central rallying slogan of The Hungarian Revolution, which broke out on Tuesday, October 23. The student youth seem to have been the ones who sparked this revolt. But there is no doubt whatever that the overwhelming majority of the people are not merely "behind" it but are creatively and actively participating in this struggle for freedom.

If Russia puts down this revolt by its superior military might, it will learn that no counterrevolution can, for long, still the new forces of revolution that have unfolded.

### **1.6** Dialectics

## The Algebra of Revolution

## Raya Dunayevskaya come from Russia, 1917, and the ghettos of Chicago, where I first saw a black person. The reason that I'm starting that way is that I was illiterate. You're born in a border town. There's a revolution, there's a counterrevolution, there's anti-Semitism. You know nothing but experience a lot, especially if you happen to be born a revolutionary. You don't know that you're a revolutionary, but you're opposed to everything.

If the capitalists were only exploiting us, they wouldn't last a minute. It's because they have all the mass media, as well as the exploitation, all the education, everything with which to brainwash us and make us think that their ideas are our ideas—"If I only think about myself and my family, I will get somewhere" is that type of idea—that they are able to perpetuate this exploitative system.

If you live when an idea is born and a great revolution in the world is born, it doesn't make any difference where you are. THAT BECOMES THE NEXT STAGE OF THE DEVELOPMENT OF HUMANITY. You know it in your bones in something as simple as when you say, "No!" to your mama, who wants to put you in pink and the boy child in blue.

Take Rosa Parks. Do you think she thought she was starting a revolution? No, she was tired as all get-out! She had just worked a full day. She was tired and just wasn't going to get up again to move to the back of the bus to give her seat to a white man who hadn't labored as hard as she. And the black youths who were sitting there seeing this middle-aged woman being dragged off to the police station said, let's not let Rosa Parks be all alone there.

What did Rosa Parks do by that one action? She started the entire black revolution in the South! She's the one who made Rev. Martin Luther King Jr. the "leader." King would never have been leader if a movement for freedom hadn't started from below, spontaneously.

What is important is that you are so natural an opponent of this system that you will bring on the revolution. Your one action of opposition to the system makes you part of that revolutionary movement, and you did it, not because you were "unconscious"—that's what they think you were—but because you were born a revolutionary and don't like the damn system under which we live!

Take the question of male chauvinism. Suddenly just a "personal," "family" affair makes you rebel. It isn't that you made a category called "a movement from practice to theory; a movement from theory to practice." It isn't in any book you rea. The book ay have ae a eneralization about it, but it was in lie, in your lie, an beause it was also in other lives, an they too rebelle, it beae a oveent, an a book, an an oranization.

One other inient hasn't to o with woen but with blaks. I was in Paris in 1947, tryin to onvine Trotskyists they shoul believe that Russia isn't erely a "eenerate workers' state," it's a state-aitalist soiety. When I o to Lyon, Frane, where I aresse the soialist youth, soeboy is sittin in that auiene who I in't know. But now I rea all the histories an biorahies o Frantz Fanon, an not only was he there, in Lyon at the sae tie, intereste in Trotskyis, but thouh he was also inin an ainity with existentialis, the blak nature leas hi away ro it all to an ineenent ath he will later all "new huanis." These haenins aren't aients. When there are reat stirrins in lie, in revolutions-to-be, soethin ets in the air an rosses national bounaries.

The oint is: it oesn't ake any ierene whether there was an in erson relationshi. I you know the exat relationshi between jc d jc, between pp d , an on't onsier any o that as abstrat, you then realize it is abstrat only i you haven't ae the onnetion o obetive an subetive an seen how the atual subetive enuine huan new beinnins, whih then unite with the oveent ro theory, an ake u into this Absolute Iea gg.

A theory is oo or the answer o what you're oin to o this year or next year, but you nee an entire hilosohy or a vision o your ae's "breakin the barrier"—that is to say, not only overthrowin the ol but reatin the new.

# 2 | Ideology

I in all ideology men and their circumstances appear upside-down as in a camera obscura, this phenomenon arises just as much rom their historical lie-process as the inversion o objects on the retina does rom their physical lie-process.

## —Karl Marx, The German Ideology

People with advantages are loath to believe that they just happen to be people with advantages. hey come readily to deine themselves as inherently worthy o what they possess; they come to believe themselves "naturally" elite; and, in act, to imagine their possessions and their privileges as natural extensions o their own elite selves. In this sense, the idea o the elite as composed o men and women having a iner moral character is an ideology o the elite as a privileged ruling stratum, and this is true whether the ideology is elite-made or made up or it by others.

## —C. Wright Mills, The Power Elite

# 2.1 Socialism or Barbarism

## C C

I n most countries the working class is organized in gigantic trade unions and political parties, numbering tens o millions o members. But these unions and parties are every day more openly and more cynically playing the role o direct agents o the ruling class and o the capitalist state, or o the bureaucratic capitalism that reigns in Russia.

Only a ew minute organizations seem to have survived the general shipwreck, organizations such as the "Fourth International," the Anarchist Federations, and a ew sel-described "ultraletist" groups (Bordigists, Spartacists, Council Communists). hese organizations are very weak, not only because o their numbers (numerical strength by itsel is never a criterion), but above all because o their political and ideological bankruptcy. Relics o the past rather than harbingers o the uture, they have proved themselves utterly incapable o understanding the undamental social transormations o the twentieth century and even less capable o developing a positive orientation toward them.

In some countries, the Anarchist Federations still enjoy the support o a number o workers with a healthy class instinct—but those workers are very backward politically, and the anarchists keep them that way. he anarchists' constant reusal to venture beyond the sterile slogan "No Politics," or to take theory seriously, contributes to the conusion. his makes anarchism one more blind alley or workers to get lost in.

Meanwhile, various "ultraletist" groups cultivate their pet sectarian deviations, some o them (like the Bordigists) even going so ar as to blame the proletariat or their own stagnation and impotence, others (like the Council Communists) living happily in the past and seeking therein their recipes or the "socialist" kitchens o the uture.

Within the ramework o a world system based on exploitation, new economic orms and new types o exploitation have appeared. While maintaining the most undamental eatures o capitalism these new orms dier signiicantly rom traditional capitalism in that they have superseded and broken radically with such traditional capitalist orms as the private ownership o the means o production. hese new economic orms even supericially resemble some o the objectives the workers' movement had set itsel, objectives such as the statiication or nationalization o the means o production and exchange, economic planning and the coordination o production on an international scale.

he bureaucracy was the social expression o these new economic orms. As traditional orms o property and the bourgeoisie o the classical period are pushed aside by state property and by the bureaucracy, the main con lict within society gradually ceases to be the old one between the owners o wealth and those without property and is replaced by the conlict between directors and executants in the process o production. In act, the bureaucracy justiies its own existence (and can be explained in objective terms) only insoar as it plays a role deemed essential to the "management" o the productive activities o society and, thereby, o all other orms o activity.

his has taken place without there resulting or the toiling masses anything other than a more intense, better coordinated, and, in a word, rationalized exploitation. he objective outcome o this evolution has been a more eicient and more systematic organization or exploiting and enslaving the proletariat.

he beneiciary o this exploitation o the proletariat is an enormous and monstrous bureaucracy (consisting o the bureaucrats in the political and economic apparatus, o technicians and intellectuals, o leaders o the "Communist" party and o the trade unions, and o the top military and police personnel). Economic "planning" proceeds in the interests o the bureaucracy and aects all areas o production.

hus there evolved a system that cynically calls itsel "socialist" yet where side by side with the appalling poverty o the working masses can be seen the lie o luxury led by about 10 or 15 percent o the population who make up the exploiting bureaucracy. his is a system where millions o people are held in concentration and orced labor camps, where the state police (o which the Gestapo was but an imitation) exercises total terror, where "elections" and other "democratic" procedures would be deemed sinister arces were they not the tragic expressions o the terrorization, the brutalization, and the degradation o man under the most overwhelming dictatorship alive today.

How did things reach this pass? How did the power established by the irst victorious proletarian revolution transorm itsel into the most eective instrument or exploiting and oppressing the masses? And how did the parties o the hird International, created to abolish exploitation and to instaurate on earth the power o workers and peasants, become the instruments o a new social ormation with interests as radically opposed to those o the proletariat as had been those o the traditional bourgeoisie itsel? hese are the questions that all advanced workers will anxiously ask themselves, once they have understood that to see anything "socialist" in Russia is to calumniate the very word socialism.

he October Revolution succumbed to the bureaucratic counterrevolution under the combined pressures o external and internal orces, o objective and subjective actors. hey all boil down to the ollowing idea: Between the second and third decade o this century neither the world economy nor the working class was as yet quite ripe or the total abolition o exploitation. A revolution, even a victorious one, would be overthrown i it remained isolated in a single country. It would either be overthrown rom outside, through civil war and the armed intervention o other capitalist countries, or it would degenerate rom within, through a change in the nature o the regime to which it had given birth.

A undamental question thereore has to be answered on the morrow o every successul revolution. Who will be the master o society once it is purged o the capitalists and their tools? he structure o the new regime, its political orm, the relationship between the working class and its own leadership, the management o production, the type o system prevailing in the actories—all these are but particular aspects o this general problem.

Should a hird World War lead to the uniication o the world system o exploitation, the civilization and social lie o humanity would be threatened with total collapse. he unlimited totalitarian domination o a single group o exploiters (whether Yankee monopolists or Russian bureaucrats) would give them ree rein to plunder the earth. he all in the productivity o labor under such a regime o ever-increasing exploitation and the complete transormation o its dominant stratum into a parasitic caste no longer having any need to develop the orces o production would lead to a massive regression in social conditions and to a prolonged setback in the development o human consciousness.

# 2.2 Ideology Materialized

## G D

Sel-consciousness exists in itsel and or itsel only insoar as it exists in and or another sel-consciousness; that is, it exists only by being recognized.

## —. W. F. egel, The Phenomenology of Spirit

### 212

Ideology is the intellectual basis o class societies within the conlictual course o history. Ideological expressions have never been pure ictions; they represent a distorted consciousness o realities, and as such they have been real actors that have in turn produced real distorting eects. his interconnection is intensiied with the advent o the spectacle—the materialization o ideology brought about by the concrete success o an autonomized system o economic production—which virtually identiies social reality with an ideology that has remolded all reality in its own image.

### 213

Once ideology—the abstract will to universality and the illusion associated with that will—is legitimized by the universal abstraction and the eective dictatorship o illusion that prevail in modern society, it is no longer a voluntaristic struggle o the ragmentary but its triumph. At that point, ideological pretensions take on a sort o lat, positivistic precision: they no longer represent historical choices; they are assertions o undeniable acts. In such a context, the particular names o ideologies tend to disappear. he speciically ideological orms o system-supporting labor are reduced to an "epistemological base" that is itsel presumed to be beyond ideology. Materialized ideology has no name, just as it has no ormulatable historical agenda. Which is another way o saying that the history o different ideologies is over.

### 214

Ideology, whose whole internal logic led toward what [Karl] Mannheim calls "total ideology" —the despotism o a ragment imposing itsel as pseudoknowledge o a rozen totality, as a totalitarian worldview—has reached its culmination in the immobilized spectacle o nonhistory. Its culmination is also its dissolution into society as a whole. When that society itsel is concretely dissolved, ideology—the final irrationality standing in the way o historical lie—must also disappear.

### 215

he spectacle is the epitome o ideology because in its plenitude it exposes and maniests the essence o all ideological systems: the impoverishment, enslavement, and negation o real lie. he spectacle is the material "expression o the separation and estrangement between man and man." he "new power o deception" concentrated in it is based on the production system in which "as the quantity o objects increases, so does the realm o alien powers to which man is subjected." his is the supreme stage o an expansion that has turned need against lie. "he need or money is thus the true need produced by the modern economic system, and it is the only need which the latter produces." Hegel's characterization o money as "the lie o what is dead, moving within itsel" has now been extended by the spectacle to all social lie.

### 216

In contrast to the project outlined in the "heses on Feuerbach" (the realization o philosophy in a praxis transcending the opposition between idealism and materialism), the spectacle preserves the ideological eatures o both materialism and idealism, imposing them in the pseudo-concreteness o its universe. he contemplative aspect o the old materialism, which conceives the world as representation and not as activity—and which ultimately idealizes matter—is ulilled in the spectacle, where concrete things are automatic masters o social lie. Conversely, the dreamed activity o idealism is also ulilled in the spectacle, through the technical mediation o signs and signals—which ultimately materialize an abstract ideal.

### 217

he parallel between ideology and schizophrenia demonstrated in [Joseph] Gabel's False Consciousness should be considered in the context o this economic materialization o ideology. Society has become what ideology already was. he racturing o practice and the antidialectical alse consciousness that results rom that racturing are imposed at every moment o everyday lie subjected to the spectacle—a subjection that systematically destroys the "aculty o encounter" and replaces it with a social hallucination: a alse consciousness o encounter, an "illusion o encounter." In a society where no one can any longer be recognized by others, each individual becomes incapable o recognizing his own reality. Ideology is at home; separation has built its own world.

### 218

"In clinical accounts o schizophrenia," says Gabel, "the deterioration o the dialectic o totality (with dissociation as its extreme orm) and the deterioration o the dialectic o becoming (with catatonia as its extreme orm) seem closely interrelated." Imprisoned in a lattened universe bounded by the screen o the spectacle, behind which his own lie has been exiled, the spectator's consciousness no longer knows anyone but the fictitious interlocutors who subject him to a one-way monologue about their commodities and the politics o their commodities. he spectacle as a whole is his "mirror sign," presenting illusory escapes rom a universal autism.

### 219

he spectacle, which obliterates the boundaries between sel and world by crushing the sel besieged by the presence/absence o the world, also obliterates the boundaries between true and alse by repressing all directly lived truth beneath the real presence o alsehood maintained by the organization o appearances. Individuals who passively accept their subjection to an alien everyday reality are thus driven toward a madness that reacts to that ate by resorting to illusory magical techniques. he essence o this pseudo-response to an unanswerable communication is the acceptance and consumption o commodities. he consumer's compulsion to imitate is a truly inantile need, conditioned by all the aspects o his undamental dispossession. As Gabel puts it in describing a quite dierent level o pathology, "he abnormal need or representation here makes up or a torturing eeling o being on the edge o existence."

### 220

In contrast to the logic o alse consciousness, which cannot truly know itsel, the search or critical truth about the spectacle must also be a true critique. It must struggle in practice among the irreconcilable enemies o the spectacle and admit that it is nothing without them. By rushing into sordid reormist compromises or pseudo-revolutionary collective actions, those driven by an abstract desire or immediate eectiveness are in reality obeying the ruling laws o thought, adopting a perspective that can see nothing but the latest news. In this way delirium reappears within the camp that claims to be opposing it. A critique seeking to go beyond the spectacle must know how to wait.

### 221

he sel-emancipation o our time is an emancipation rom the material bases o inverted truth. his "historic mission o establishing truth in the world" can be carried out neither by the isolated individual nor by atomized and manipulated masses but only and always by the class that is able to dissolve all classes by reducing all power to the de-alienating orm o realized democracy—to councils in which practical theory veriies itsel and surveys its own actions. Only there are individuals "directly linked to world history"—there where dialogue has armed itsel to impose its own conditions.

## "SCInIFIC BSIS" oF "CoMMon SnS"

A "social scientist" is someone who is paid to deend this society's myths. His deense mechanism, in its simplest ormulation, runs approximately as ollows: he begins by assuming that the society o his time and place is the only possible orm o society; he then concludes that some other orm o society is impossible. Unortunately, the "social scientist" rarely admits his assumptions; he usually claims that he doesn't make any assumptions. And it can't be said that he's lying outright: he usually takes his assumptions so much or granted that he doesn't even know he's making them.

he "social scientist" claims to be empirical and objective; he claims to make no value judgments. Yet by reducing the person to the bundle o tastes, desires, and preerences to which he's restricted in capitalist society, the "objective scientist" makes the bizarre claim that this bundle is what the worker is; and he makes the antastic value judgment that the worker cannot be other than what he is in capitalist society. According to the "laws o human behavior" o this "science," the solidarity o students with workers, the occupation o actories by workers, the desire o workers to run their own production, distribution, and coordination, are all impossible. Why? Because these things are impossible in capitalist society, and or these "scientists" who make no value judgments, existing societies are the only possible societies, and the corporate-military society is the best o all possible societies.

o the American social scientist, "human nature" is what people do in corporate-military America: a ew make decisions, and the rest ollow orders; some think, and others do; some buy other people's labor, and the rest sell their own labor; a ew invest, and the rest are consumers; some are sadists, and others masochists; some have a desire to kill and others to die. he "scientist" passes all this o as "exchange," as "reciprocity," as a "division o labor" in which people are divided along with tasks. o the "social scientist" this is all so natural that he thinks he makes no value judgments when he takes it all or granted. Corporations and the military even give him grants to show that it's always been this way: grants to demonstrate that this "human nature" is lodged in the beginning o history and in the depths o the unconscious. (American psychologists—especially "behaviorists"—make the ambiguous "contribution" o demonstrating that animals also have a "human nature": the psychologists drive rats mad in a situation similar to a war, which the psychologists themselves helped plan, and then they show that rats, too, have a desire to kill, that they have masochist tendencies.)

In terms o what the American "social scientist" takes or granted, when students and workers in France started to ight to do away with "reciprocity," "exchange," and the division o labor, they were ighting not against the capitalist police but against "human nature." And since this is obviously impossible, the events that took place in May 1968 did not take place.

o keep its relative privileges, each group tries to keep the groups below rom shaking the structure.

hus in times o "peace" the system is largely sel-policed: the colonized repress the colonized, blacks repress blacks, whites repress each other, the blacks, and the colonized. hus the working population represses itsel, "law and order" is maintained, and the ruling class is saved rom urther outlays on the repressive apparatus.

# 2.4 Radical Learning through Neoliberal Crisis

## S R

Education is liberation, removal o all weeds, rubble, and vermin that seek to harm the plant's delicate shoots, a radiance o light and warmth, the loving rush o rain alling at night.

## —Friedrich nietzsche, "Schopenhauer as Educator"

## SMMy

Liberalism reconciles capitalism and democracy in the humanist cultivation o moral and sel-improving individuals. Markets and elections require identical capacities: discerning intellect, empathic recognition, agonistic contention, ethical invigilation, and desire to contribute individual to collective progress. Liberal discourse integrates diverse opinions by nurturing private skills and public duties, personal dreams and civic ainities. Liberal education supports this ideological repression o capitalist-democratic tensions; its neurotic pedagogical subjects instantiate the rickety bad-aith antasy o the liberal-democratic peace.

Neoliberalism radicalizes capitalism by shedding this integument o humanist pastoralism with its political critique o liberal consolation. Neoliberalism disburdens liberalism o its obsolete palliatives, replacing its docile, disciplined, civic-minded citizens with entrepreneurial sel-investors eager to sacriice everything or market society. Capitalism is wrested rom antiquated humanitarian etters o welare economics and inantilizing therapies. Neoliberal education enorces this ideological repression o communal mediation; its psychotic subjects instantiate the bad-aith antasy o turning social aggression into capitalist holism.

Neoliberalism's opponents ight antihumanist hypercapitalism with anticapitalist hyperhumanism. I urge instead a dialectical inversion o neoliberal discourse that reuses the alse comorts o bourgeois democracy. aking U. S. higher education as a neoliberal exemplar, I envisage an audacious pedagogy that reclaims the generalized physical courage, personal sacriice, unconscious desires, and "motivating" terror o precarious lie to transcend the repressive antasies o both liberalism and neoliberalism.

## IBISM

It is oten said that European philosophy is ounded on the death sentence o a teacher, Socrates, convicted o corrupting youth and ignoring gods. A harsh sentence or a teacher who "never promised to teach . . . anything," a knower who knew only that "I do not think I know what I do not know." "Corrupting youth" meant insulating their innocence rom artiice and conceit— indeed rom corruption.

Liberalism draws on blank slates, guided by a similar "romantic notion o original innocence [and] childhood as a period o wisdom, purity, and creativity, a stage . . . children should be let to enjoy rather than be disciplined out o." Humanist pedagogy rejected the "rote memorization, relentless drills, endless repetition, daily analysis o texts, elaborate exercises in imitation and rhetorical variation, all backed up by the threat o violence," which were characteristic in Shakespeare's day. Children and students would embody the liberal order that educated them: hopeul, curious, playul, restless, excited, and brimming with sel-discovery.

But anxiety lurks in the "innocence [and] childhood [o our] democratic ideal." Innocence orges liberal morality, and its etishized violation justiies it. We seek out harrowing stories of abused children to affirm our primal innocence. "Stolen youth" consecrates this purity: a boy's "happy, carefree years of childhood . . . drawn to a close" by war; 6 a girl left "no longer a child" by occupation;7 a kid aged to death by poverty.8 But it also sublimates our anxiety that we are *not* innocent. Liberal humanists blame the outside world for our fallen selves but also our inner selves for our fallen world. These ambivalent selves converge in the conflicted pedagogy of liberal "freedom" that tames children and capitalists alike.

In The Bell Jar Sylvia Plath sneers, "I was taking one of those honors programs that teach you to think independently."9 One thinks for others and only then for oneself. 10 Liberalism first must tame the "wild child" released by self-exploration. "These 'vagabonds of the intellect" loosed by humanism's "vast operation of de-consecration" must be contained. 11 Novels about college12 assured us that narcissism would replace the "inner-direction implanted early in life by the elders." 13 But education theorists like Ernest Becker lamented the "tragic paradox [of] education and the state": "liberal education . . . train[ed] youth to exercise their own judgement, seek their own solutions, criticize and master the world on their own term. [But then recoiled] that they [might] criticize . . . anything."14 Liberal pedagogy inevitably echoed Immanuel Kant's injunction to "argue as much as you like and about whatever you like, but obey!" "Intellectual freedom" requires limits on "civil freedom," the theory went, just as democracy should limit capitalism.

Education served the utilitarian needs of the "civilizing process" but especially of capitalism. Democratic pedagogy was "to provide the modern state with enlightened citizens and to train an efficient work force." Liberal schooling also ensured wealthy classes a "rate of return on educational capital." But capitalist-democratic cultivation of individuality and wage labor were unstable. 19 This tension recalls Karl Marx's chiasmus: "In bourgeois society capital is independent and has individuality, while the living person is dependent and has no individuality."20 Exposing this ideological perversion, Theodor Adorno would stress that the "precarious and irrational self-preservation of society is falsified and turned into an achievement of its immanent justice or 'rationality."21 Humanist capitalism exemplifies this process.

Liberal pedagogy supports the democratic claim to promote civil society to "restrict the private abuse of public institutions" and "prevent aristocratic tendencies."22 Children must become little liberals prepared for enlightened civic "participation." 23 Humanism advances the "consciously socialized interest," in John Dewey's phrase, against "customs operating under the control of a superior class."24 Even playgrounds were "arenas for democratic advancement [of] children rich and poor, immigrant and native-born."25 The liberal student is, in short, a welter of capitalist-democratic subjectivities. She works hard; reads and votes critically; becomes a CEO or protests one; joins or resists a war—all within the humanist-pedagogic ethos. She knows that rights entail duties, privilege requires civics, and social reciprocity reconciles entrepreneur and elector in a stable capitalist polity in which "the ree assent o the members is the only source o political authority." Liberalism:

- nurtures authentic selhood
- develops personal character
- assimilates individual desires
- integrates idiosyncratic creativity
- cultivates particular talents
- aligns unique pursuits with general ethics
- inculcates "agency" discourse
- disciplines excess energies
- sublimates disruptive desires/aects
- moralizes communal sentiment
- instrumentalizes reined talents
- establishes logocentric valuation
- internalizes implicit social codes
- consolidates ideological conormity
- diuses public obligation
- antasizes sel/society reconciliation

## noIBISM

Neoliberalism decimates humanist solace in a capitalism without c onsolation, apology, or guilt, populated by sel-investors without nagging superegos or nodding therapists. Neoliberalism is terrorism, instilling ear o abjection under market austerity. Neoliberalism is to liberalism as Leninism is to Marxism, reviving a gradualist theory by attacking its etters. Immanent in the liberalism it condemns, neoliberalism commodiies education to promote paranoid aggression. hrough its academic outposts, neoliberalism imposes an "inverted totalitarian" kingdom o means.

Neoliberalism has been a pedagogical calamity. First, increasing poverty, inequity, and inancial burdens devastate students, teachers, and campuses. Meretricious spending on vanity projects and iscal austerity combine to deund public schools, triggering social migration; prioritize athletic over academic programs; encourage elite colleges to exclude poorer applicants; increase dropout rates through debt peonage; and incur living-wage campaigns by sta. Corporate higher education attacks aculty as well by intensiying battles over intellectual property, productivity assessments, cutthroat careerism, precarious employment, and poverty-wage adjunct assignments. Second, neoliberal inancial strangulation subsists on a steady discursive diet o meritocracy, lean production, business modeling, test-prep preoccupation, and "learning outcome" yardsticks. In eect bourgeois pedagogy celebrates SEM-centric science envy, denigrates literature and the humanities, hinders open inquiry and innovative research, harms teaching and undermines learning, and erodes academic reedom.

Many misconstrue neoliberal harshness as capitalism on steroids: a degree change in bourgeois economics. Neoliberalism advances not by reinventing capitalism but by removing its social emollients. Seeing neoliberalism as extreme capitalism romanticizes its earlier incarnations and eclipses resistance to them. Neoliberalism assails dissent rom maximal capitalist exploitation. As Samir Amin says, "he weakening o the working class and historic let stance . . . encourages capital to take a so-called neoliberal hard line." he supposed indices o neoliberalism have been here all along: disposable people, reserve workers, class conlict, systemic precariousness, personal responsibility, and capital-subservient pedagogy. Liberalism reers not to the lack o bourgeois rigors but o their public amelioration. Its neoliberal successor puriies capitalism by destroying humane restraint along with its antasy o harmonious capitalist lie.

"he resurgence o liberalism in the orm o neoliberalism" is a normative correction; it rewires the democratic asylum, not the capitalist lunatic. First, "neoliberalism [constitutes] a rationality" o "eective abandonment." We live or die by our own exertion. Second, neoliberalism ully subsumes rather than just commodiies. hings and people aren't potentially but necessarily reduced to exchange values and punished or nonmarket pursuits. hird, capitalist markets are organic systems requiring the sacriice o personal security and communal reciprocity. Neoliberalism replaces repressive ideology with sacriicial theology, recalling Søren Kierkegaard's denial o a "guarantee that our sacriice will be rewarded, that it will restore Meaning to our lie—we have to make a leap o aith which, to an external observer cannot but look like an act o madness." Wendell Berry calls it "sentimental capitalism," in which "everything small, local, private, personal, natural, good, and beautiul must be sacriiced in the interest o the 'ree market' and the great corporations, which will bring . . . security and happiness . . . in, o course, the uture."

For neoliberals capitalism is the condition o reedom, supreme over individuals. hus Ludwig von Mises could call "the capitalist system o production . . . an economic democracy in which every penny gives a right to vote." Neoliberal governance secures property rights, enorces law, and directs markets. o Friedrich von Hayek, "[liberalism's] deect was not that it adhered too stubbornly to principles, but rather that it lacked principles sufficiently definite to provide clear guidance." Markets need muscles—the state must compel capitalism, not just clear a space for it. Neoliberalism modifies laissez-faire economics much as state-led growth revised "free trade." In its "move toward more state by less government," in Michel Foucault's phrase, neoliberals consolidate a "neoclassical synthesis [that uses] macroeconomic policy [to] stabilize aggregate demand, utilizing tools of fiscal and monetary policy." Neoliberalism replaces:

- authenticity with flexibility
- personal with corporate character
- · assimilated with deregulated desires
- idiosyncrasy with brand name
- particular with substitutable talents
- · ethical with contractual obligation
- · agency with autonomy
- · disciplined with released excess energies
- sublimated with expulsive fantasies
- neurotic with psychotic subjectivity
- · communitarian sentiment with enclave mentality
- · mediated with immediate gratification
- logocentric valuation with identitarian/psychic injury45
- implicit social codes with explicit social symbolism
- ideological conformity with postideological candor
- public safety with private security
- · reconciliation of self/society with antinomy

### POSTNEOLIBERAL PERSON(A)S

But tho' education be disclaim'd by philosophy, as a fallacious ground of assent to any opinion, it prevails nevertheless in the world, and in this cause why all systems are apt to be rejected at first as new and unusual.

## —David Hume, A Treatise of Human Nature

Rejections of neoliberalism tend toward nostalgia. If neoliberalism is "faltering" under "structural strains . . . it [can] no longer . . . handle,"46 perhaps we should relitigate social democracy. Maybe neoliberal commodification should inspire resistance by "cosmopolitan education" or "slow scholarship."47 Because humanism limited capitalist excess, it seems natural to think a "hyperhumanism" can constrain neoliberal "hypercapitalism." But this just the old liberal fantasy recycled. Instead, let's go *through* neoliberalism, explode its cracks, and reweaponize its tools. As Richard Gilman-Opalsky proclaims, crisis desires a "revolt [that] embodies and relects the transormative aspirations o everyday people." On and o campus we must repoliticize pedagogy, revising our scholarship while turning "traditional disciplinary boundaries" into "sites/cites o contestation [in] orging . . . alternatives as academics, grassroots organizers, and activists."

Recall that neoliberal bad aith exposes liberal bad aith with unusual candor about capitalism:

- 1. Capitalism cannot be palliated by humanism.
- 2. Capitalism cannot be softened by ideology.
- 3. Capitalism is an unmediated physical test.
- 4. Capitalist innovation violates tradition.
- 5. Capitalist sacriices or sel equal sacriices or capital.
- 6. he capitalist invests in a substitutable sel.
- 7. Capitalist risk is universal.
- 8. Capitalist precariousness prompts ierce resistance.

hese neoliberal commitments provide the consciousness to reject liberal democracy and the subjectivity to reject neoliberalism. We can dialectically invert and sublate neoliberal candor. Capitalism is inescapably brutal, says the neoliberal (1–3), so approach it radically and absolutely; yes, we reject it absolutely and radically. Capitalism rewards startling inventions by sacriicial members o a universal community who iercely resist precariousness, says the neoliberal (4–8); yes, therein lies the communist revolt! May the perverse play o the psychical-physical person(a) inspire the uncaptured pedagogy o the uprising.

In Otto de Kat's novella The Figure in the Distance, the narrator recalls his high school perormance in Antigone: "Lie would never again have the same vastness as it had on those bare boards. . . . From that moment on, everything seemed less important. . . . He had participated in a drama o love and ate and death." he child transorms himsel through an act, a role, a part. An epigraph to obias Wol's This Boy's Life is a similar comment by Oscar Wilde: "he irst duty in lie is to assume a pose. What the second is, no one has yet discovered." he boy Wol, playing with his gun, wonders what an approaching nun "would . . . make o me. . . . I didn't understand it mysel. Being so close to so much robust identity made me eel the poverty o my own, the ludicrous aspect o my costume and props." Imagining himsel a sniper, the happy child is suddenly shattered, sees himsel through another as a boy. Similarly, contemplating the ducks in Catcher in the Rye, ageless nature beyond him, Holden Caulield undergoes "a conscious and voluntary surrender that is prelude to Enlightenment." How does the ecstasy o role play bring us the joy o our higher true sel?

"Persona originally means 'mask' and it is through the mask that the individual acquires a role and a social identity," Giorgio Agamben says, describing a patrician's insignia. Persona becomes "the 'personality' that deines the place o the individual in the dramas and rituals o social lie. Eventually persona came to signiy the juridical capacity and political dignity o the ree man." "he struggle or recognition is," he stresses, "a struggle or a mask but this mask coincides with the 'personality' that society recognizes in every individual." Hence "the moral person constitutes himsel . . . through, at once, an adhesion to and a distancing rom, the social mask." We have here a reversal o our liberal-therapeutic holistic sel. he mask as person(a) allows us to play not on our surace but beneath it, secure, unseen. hink o the director Jacques Copeau's rustration with an actor who "could experience but not express an emotion," the demand that he "struggle with his own blood." Having per ected the person(a) o his character he must imbue it with his own person: he traverses the irreducibly liminal space o the sel/person(a). his person(a) turns the interchangeable neoliberal subject, ever malleable to market direction, into a masked ugitive, revealed by will and choice alone. eachers and students should cultivate this person(a) as both intensiication and protection o creative upheaval beyond liberal conormity and neoliberal promiscuity.

Rainer Maria Rilke's letters (1903–1904) to Franz Kappus, a soldier seeking advice on his poetry, oer cryptic counsel. Rilke begins his mentorship by denying its possibility: "You ask whether your verses are any good. You ask me. You have asked others beore this. . . . I beg you to stop doing that sort o thing." His gentle pedagogy is strictly staged. First, achieve "vast, inner solitude": "walk inside yoursel and meet no one or hours," gazing "upon it all as a child would." Only in radical solitude do we ind our person. Second, engage "your innermost eeling, in your quietest hour" so "the natural growth o your inner lie will eventually guide you." hird, perceive the relationship to creativity; "ask yoursel in the most silent hour o your night: must I write?" his stage also occasions your "search into the depths o hings," to "ind out i this way o perceiving the world arises rom a necessity o your being." Fourth, Rilke says one must accept that "in important matters we are unspeakably alone"—the condition o natality.

Most striking is his insistence that poetic words seek what eludes words. "Most experiences are unsayable," he readily announces. You must "let each impression and each embryo o a eeling come to completion, entirely in itsel, in the dark, in the unsayable, the unconscious." What language desires, he ruminates, "is so very delicate, is almost unsayable." When Gilles Deleuze oddly reers to "Rilke reusing to give any advice to a young poet," he may mean his initial warning was sincere: poetic guidance itsel may be unsayable. But Rilke submits to the wordless unconscious, the process o becoming his inner experience. At this moment, one is not liberal subject o a sel but posthumanist object o a lie. In Deleuze's words:

It's a strange business, speaking or yoursel, in your own name, because it doesn't at all come with seeing yoursel as an ego or a person or a subject. Individuals ind a real name or themselves, rather, only through the harshest exercise in depersonalization, by opening themselves up to the multiplicities everywhere within them, to the intensities running through them.

Or in Guy Debord's exhortation, "In a dérive one or more persons during a certain period drop their relations, their work and leisure activities, and all their other usual motives or movement and action, and let themselves be drawn by the attractions o the terrain and the encounters they end there." When we inally recognize our persona as our misrecognized person, as the stage o our liberal subjectivity, we may experience ourselves genuinely as objects o objects, causes o causes, at home in our bodily becoming. his artistic-philosophical urge tracks to "Montaigne's method," that "peculiar orm o his Essays" in which "he ollows his own inner rhythm, which, though constantly induced and maintained by things, is not bound to them, but reely skips rom one to another." Michel de Montaigne's ounding humanist rejection o sel-mastering piety may ound a posthumanist rejection o neoliberalism's equally sel-mutilating theology.

## ICIC o

A pedagogical revolt through neoliberal inhumanity welcomes to our campuses—those porous enclosures o sel-serious cerebration—the means o their remaking: laughter, love, play, anxiety, acedia, tricksters, satire, aggression, ressentiment, communism—above all a idelity to what eludes language. I a radical pedagogy is a chimera, we ought to imagine it or that very reason. We need irst, not last, to crack open the walls o the universities, classrooms, all plodding disciplinary routines now rendered reactionary. Should we disrupt our classes and ideologies with the aective silences and perceptive secrets o ugitive person(a)s, the threat posed to neoliberalism will ward o liberal retrenchment as well.

In his memoir Paul Feyerabend says, "I think many young people . . . see the world in a special way, and yet the slightest pressure can make them see it dierently. A good teacher respects this instability." And nurtures it in the revolutionary spirit o all "young people." He then remembers "Oswald homas, a well-known igure in Viennese adult education. Once a month, homas assembled about two thousand people in a large meadow outside Vienna, turned o the streetlights, and explained the constellations. He told scientiic details about the stars and somewhat less scientiic stories about the legendary animals, gods, humans, that populated the sky."
