In th twntith cntury th blck mn on his hom trritory is oblivious of th momnt whn his infriority is dtrmind by th Othr. Nturlly, w hv hd th opportunity to discuss th blck problm with frinds nd, lss oftn, with Africn Amricns. Togthr w proclimd loud nd clr th qulity of mn in th world. In th Antills thr is lso tht minor tnsion btwn th cliqus of whit Crols, multtos, nd blcks. But w wr contnt to intllctuliz ths diffrncs. In fct, thr ws nothing drmtic bout thm. And thn . . .

And thn w wr givn th occsion to confront th whit gz. An unusul wight dscndd on us. Th rl world robbd us of our shr. In th whit world, th mn of color ncountrs difficultis in lborting his body schm. Th img of on's body is solly ngting. It's n img in th third prson. All round th body rigns n tmosphr of crtin uncrtinty. I know tht if I wnt to smok, I shll hv to strtch out my right rm nd grb th pck of cigrtts lying t th othr nd of th tbl. As for th mtchs, thy r in th lft drwr, nd I shll hv to mov bck littl. And I mk ll ths movs, not out of hbit, but by implicit knowldg. A slow construction of my slf s body in sptil nd tmporl world—such sms to b th schm. It is not imposd on m; it is rthr dfinitiv structuring of my slf nd th world—dfinitiv bcus it crts gnuin dilctic btwn my body nd th world.

For some years now, certain laboratories have been researching for a "denegrification" serum. In all seriousness they have been rinsing out their test tubes and adjusting their scales and have begun research on how the wretched black man could whiten himself and thus rid himself of the burden of this bodily curse. Beneath the body schema I had created a historical-racial schema. The data I used were provided not by "remnants of feelings and notions of the tactile, vestibular, kinesthetic, or visual nature" but by the Other, the white man, who had woven me out of a thousand details, anecdotes, and stories. I thought I was being asked to construct a physiological self, to balance space and localize sensations, when all the time they were clamoring for more

"Look! A Negro!" It was a passing sting. I attempted a smile.

"Look! A Negro!" Absolutely. I was beginning to enjoy myself.

"Look! A Negro!" The circle was gradually getting smaller. I was really enjoying myself.

"Maman, look, a Negro; I'm scared!" Scared! Scared! Now they were beginning to be scared of me. I wanted to kill myself laughing, but laughter had become out of the question.

I couldn't take it any longer, for I already knew there were legends, stories, history, and especially the historicity that [Karl] Jaspers had taught me. As a result, the body schema, attacked in several places, collapsed, giving way to an epidermal racial schema. In the train, it was a question of being aware of my body, no longer in the third person but in triple. In the train, instead of one seat, they left me two or three. I was no longer enjoying myself. I was unable to discover the feverish coordinates of the world. I existed in triple: I was taking up room. I approached the Other, and the Other, evasive, hostile, but not opaque, transparent and absent, vanished. Nausea.

I was responsible not only for my body but also for my race and my ancestors. I cast an objective gaze over myself, discovered my blackness, my ethnic features; deafened by cannibalism, backwardness, fetishism, racial stigmas, slave traders, and above all, yes, above all, the grinning *Y a bon Banania*.

Disoriented, incapable of confronting the Other, the white man, who had no scruples about imprisoning me, I transported myself on that particular day far, very far, from my self, and gave myself up as an object. What did this mean to me? Peeling, stripping my skin, causing a hemorrhage that left congealed black blood all over my body. Yet this reconsideration of myself, this thematization, was not my idea. I wanted quite simply to be a man among men. I would have liked to enter our world young and sleek, a world we could build together.

I refused, however, any affective tetanization. I wanted to be a man, and nothing but a man. There were some who wanted to equate me with my ncstors, nslvd nd lynchd: I dcidd tht I would ccpt this. I considrd this intrnl kinship from th univrsl lvl of th intllct—I ws th grndson of slvs th sm wy Prsidnt [Albrt Frnçois] Lbrun ws th grndson of psnts who hd bn xploitd nd workd to th bon.

Th lrt ws soon ovr, in fct.

In th Unitd Stts, blcks r sgrgtd. In South Amric, thy r whippd in th strts, nd blck strikrs r gunnd down. In Wst Afric, th blck mn is bst of burdn. And just bsid m thr is this studnt collgu of min from Algri who tlls m, "As long s th Arb is trtd lik mn, lik on of us, thr will b no vibl nswr."

"You s, my dr fllow, color prjudic is totlly forign to m." "But do com in, old chp, you won't find ny color prjudic hr." "Quit so, th blck is just s much mn s w r." "It's not bcus h's blck tht h's lss intllignt thn w r." "I hd Sngls collgu in th rgimnt, vry smrt guy."

Whr do I fit in? Or, if you lik, whr should I stick myslf?

## "Mrtinicn, ntiv from on of our 'old' colonis."

Whr should I hid?

## "Look, Ngro! Maman, Ngro!"

"Ssh! You'll mk him ngry. Don't py ttntion to him, monsiur; h dosn't rliz you'r just s civilizd s w r."

My body ws rturnd to m sprd-gld, disjointd, rdon, drpd in mourning on this whit wintr's dy. Th Ngro is n niml, th Ngro is bd, th Ngro is wickd, th Ngro is ugly; look, Ngro; th Ngro is trmbling, th Ngro is trmbling bcus h's cold, th smll boy is trmbling bcus h's frid of th Ngro, th Ngro is trmbling with cold, th cold tht chills th bons, th lovly littl boy is trmbling bcus h thinks th Ngro is trmbling with rg, th littl whit boy runs to his mothr's rms: "Maman, th Ngro's going to t m."

Th whit mn is ll round m; up bov th sky is tring t its nvl; th rth crunchs undr my ft nd sings whit, whit. All this whitnss bums m to cindr.

I sit down nxt to th fir nd discovr my livry for th first tim. It is in fct ugly. I won't go on bcus who cn tll m wht buty is?

Whr should I put myslf from now on? I cn fl tht fmilir rush of blood surg up from th numrous disprsions of my bing. I m bout to los my tmpr. Th fir hd did long tim go, nd onc gin th Ngro is trmbling.

## "Look how hndsom tht Ngro is."

## "Th hndsom Ngro sys, 'Fuck you,' mdm."

Hr fc colord with shm. At lst I ws frd from my rumintion. I rlizd two things t onc: I hd idntifid th nmy nd crtd scndl. Ovrjoyd. W could now hv som fun.

Th bttlfild hd bn drwn up; I could ntr th lists.

I don't bliv it! Whrs I ws prprd to forgt, to forgiv, nd to lov, my mssg ws flung bck t m lik slp in th fc. Th whit world, th only dcnt on, ws prvnting m from prticipting. It dmndd tht mn bhv lik mn. It dmndd of m tht I bhv lik blck mn—or t lst lik Ngro. I hild th world, nd th world mputtd my nthusism. I ws xpctd to sty in lin nd mk myslf scrc.

I'll show thm! Thy cn't sy I didn't wrn thm. Slvry? No longr subjct of discussion, just bd mmory. My so-clld infriority? A hox tht it would b bttr to lugh bout. I ws prprd to forgt vrything, providd th world intgrtd m. My incisors wr rdy to go into ction. I could fl thm, shrp. And thn . . .

I don't bliv it! Whrs I hd vry rson to vnt my htrd nd lothing, thy wr rjcting m? Whrs I ws th on thy should hv bggd nd implord, I ws dnid th slightst rcognition? I md up my mind, sinc it ws impossibl to rid myslf of n innate complex, to ssrt myslf s . Sinc th Othr ws rluctnt to rcogniz m, thr ws only on nswr: to mk myslf known.

In Anti-Semite and Jew [Jn-Pul] Srtr writs: "Thy [th Jws] hv llowd thmslvs to b poisond by th strotyp tht othrs hv of thm, nd thy liv in fr tht thir cts will corrspond to this strotyp. . . . W my sy tht thir conduct is prptully ovrdtrmind from th insid."

Th Jwishnss of th Jw, howvr, cn go unnoticd. H is not int grlly wht h is. W cn but hop nd wit. His cts nd bhvior r th dtrmining fctor. H is whit mn, nd prt from som dbtbl f turs, h cn pss undtctd. H blongs to th rc tht hs nvr prcticd cnniblism. Wht strng id, to t on's fthr! Srvs thm right; thy shouldn't b blck. Of cours th Jws hv bn tormntd—wht m I sying? Thy hv bn huntd, xtrmintd, nd crmtd, but ths r just minor pisods in th fmily history. Th Jw is not likd s soon s h hs bn dtctd. But with m things tk on new fc. I'm not givn scond chnc. I m ovrdtrmind from th outsid. I m slv not to th "id" othrs hv of m, but to my pprnc. I rriv slowly in th world; suddn mrgncs r no longr my hbit. I crwl long. Th whit gz, th only vlid on, is lrdy disscting m. I m fixed. Onc thir microtoms r shrpnd, th whits objctivly cut sctions of my rlity. I hv bn btryd. I sns, I s in this whit gz tht it's th rrivl not of nw mn, but of nw typ of mn, nw spcis. A Ngro, in fct!

I slip into cornrs, my long ntnn ncountring th vrious xioms on th surfc of things: th Ngro's cloths smll of Ngro; th Ngro hs whit tth; th Ngro hs big ft; th Ngro hs brod chst. I slip into cornrs; I kp silnt; ll I wnt is to b nonymous, to b forgottn. Look, I'll gr to vrything, on condition I go unnoticd!

"Hy, I'd lik you to mt my blck frind. . . . Aim Csir, blck agrégé from th Sorbonn. . . . Mrin Andrson, th grtst blck singr. . . . Dr. Cobb, who discovrd whit blood clls, is blck. . . . Hy, sy hllo to my frind from Mrtiniqu (b crful, h's vry touchy)."

Shm. Shm nd slf-contmpt. Nus. Whn thy lik m, thy tll m my color hs nothing to do with it. Whn thy ht m, thy dd tht it's not bcus of my color. Eithr wy, I m prisonr of th vicious circl.

I turn wy from ths prophts of doom nd cling to my brothrs, N gros lik myslf. To my horror, thy rjct m. Thy r lmost whit. And thn thy'll probbly mrry whit womn nd hv slightly brown childrn. Who knows, grdully, prhps . . .

I ws drming.

"You must undrstnd tht I m on of Lyon's biggst fns of blck popl."

Th proof ws thr, implcbl. My blcknss ws thr, dns nd undnibl. And it tormntd m, pursud m, md m unsy, nd xspr td m.

Ngros r svgs, morons, nd illitrts. But I knw prsonlly tht in my cs ths ssrtions wr wrong. Thr ws this myth of th Ngro tht hd to b dstroyd t ll costs. W wr no longr living in n g whn popl mrvld t blck prist. W hd doctors, tchrs, nd sttsmn. Oky, but thr ws lwys somthing unusul bout thm. "W hv Sn gls history tchr. H's vry intllignt. . . . Our physicin's blck. H's vry gntl." Hr ws th Ngro tchr, th Ngro physicin; s for m, I ws bcoming nrvous wrck, shking t th slightst lrt. I knw for instnc tht if th physicin md on fls mov, it ws ovr for him nd for ll thos who cm ftr him. Wht, in fct, could on xpct from Ngro physicin? As long s vrything ws going smoothly, h ws prisd to th hvns; but wtch out—thr ws no room whtsovr for ny mistk. Th blck physicin will nvr know how clos h is to bing discrditd. I rpt, I ws wlld in: nithr my rfind mnnrs nor my litrry knowldg nor my undrstnding of th quntum thory could find fvor.

Th Jw nd I: not stisfid with rcilizing myslf, by hppy strok of ft, I ws turning mor humn. I ws drwing closr to th Jw, my brothr in misfortun.

Disgrcful!

At first glnc it might sm strng tht th ttitud of th nti-Smit cn b qutd with tht of th ngrophob. It ws my philosophy tchr from th Antills who rmindd m on dy: "Whn you hr somon insulting th Jws, py ttntion; h is tlking bout you." And I blivd t th tim h ws univrslly right, mning tht I ws rsponsibl in my body nd soul for th ft rsrvd for my brothr. Sinc thn, I hv undrstood tht wht h mnt quit simply ws tht th nti-Smit is invitbly ngrophob.

"You hv com too lt, much too lt. Thr will lwys b world whit world—btwn you nd us: tht impossibility on ithr sid to oblitr t th pst onc nd for ll." Undrstndbly, confrontd with this ffctiv nkylosis of th whit mn, I finlly md up my mind to shout my blcknss. Grdully, putting out psudopodi in ll dirctions, I scrtd rc.

I put th whit mn bck in his plc; mboldnd, I jostld him nd hurld in his fc: ccommodt m s I m; I'm not ccommodting nyon. I snickrd to my hrt's dlight. Th whit mn ws visibly growling. His rction ws long tim coming. I hd won. I ws ovrjoyd.

"Ly sid your history, your rsrch into th pst, nd try to gt in stp with our rhythm. In socity such s ours, industrilizd to th xtrm, domintd by scinc, thr is no longr room for your snsitivity. You hv to b tough to b bl to liv. It is no longr nough to ply bll with th world; you hv to mstr it with intgrls nd toms. Of cours, thy will tll m, from tim to tim whn w r tird of ll tht concrt, we will turn to you s our childrn, our nïv, ingnuous, nd spontnous childrn. W will turn to you s th childhood of th world. You r so uthntic in your lif, so plyful. Lt us forgt for fw momnts our forml, polit civiliztion nd bnd down ovr thos hds, thos dorbl xprssiv fcs. In sns, you rconcil us with ourslvs."

So thy wr countring my irrtionlity with rtionlity, my rtionlity with th "tru rtionlity." I couldn't hop to win. I tstd my hrdity. I did complt chckup of my sicknss. I wntd to b typiclly blck—tht ws out of th qustion. I wntd to b whit—tht ws jok. And whn I trid to clim my ngritud intllctully s concpt, thy sntchd it wy from m.

From tim to tim you fl lik giving up. Exprssing th rl is n rduous job. But whn you tk it into your hd to xprss xistnc, you will vry likly ncountr nothing but th nonxistnt. Wht is crtin is tht t th vry momnt whn I ndvord to grsp my bing, Srtr, who rmins "th Othr," by nming m shttrd my lst illusion. I, in proxysm of xprinc nd rg, ws procliming this; h rmindd m tht my ngritud ws nothing but wk stg. Truthfully, I'm tlling you, I no longr flt th crss of th ground. Without blck pst, without blck futur, it ws impossible for me to live my blackness. Not yet white, no longer completely black, I was damned. Jean-Paul Sartre forgets that the black man suffers in his body quite differently from the white man.3

Between the white man and me there is irremediably a relationship of transcendence. But we have forgotten my constancy in love. I define myself as absolutely and sustainedly open-minded. And I take this negritude and with tears in my eyes I piece together the mechanism. That which had been shattered is rebuilt and constructed by the intuitive lianas of my hands. My shout rings out more violently: I am a nigger, I am a nigger, I am a nigger.

A feeling of inferiority? No, a feeling of not existing. Sin is black as virtue is white. All those white men, fingering their guns, can't be wrong. I am guilty. I don't know what of, but I know I'm a wretch.

Though Sartre's speculations on the existence of "the Other" remain correct (insofar as, we may recall, *Being and Nothingness* describes an alienated consciousness), their application to a black consciousness proves fallacious because the white man is not only "the Other" but also the master, whether real or imaginary.

# 4.2 The Negro's Fight

Negroes, We Can Depend Only on Ourselves!

## C. L. R. James

The worst traitors and enemies are always the traitors and enemies inside the camp. Today at the head of the traitors is Philip Randolph with his two assistants, Walter White of the NAACP and Frank Crosswaith of the Socialist Party. But Randolph and White are the chiefs. They are responsible for the calling off of the March on Washington. They did [Franklin] Roosevelt's dirty work. They did what Roosevelt could not have done himself. Randolph bears the responsibility, but White of the NAACP is smeared all over with evidence of his partnership in the crime. Let us therefore have a few words bout Whit. H is big shot in th NAACP nd for yrs w hv pointd out tht th ldrship of th NAACP is rottn, tht though it my invstigt lynchings, tc., nd crry on crtin mount of public gittion, yt it is not n orgniztion which dpnds on th ction of th msss but bgs for fvors nd crris on intrigus with Roosvlt nd Elnor Roosvlt. It is fr mor concrnd with its bck-stirs connctions with th Whit Hous thn with mobilizing mss ction. It will go littl wy with th msss of Ngros whn th msss mov, but s soon s th mss movmnt bgins to look s if it mns businss, th NAACP bgins to think of wht Roosvlt will sy. And in ny srious clsh btwn th msss of Ngro popl nd th govrnmnt, th NAACP is with th govrnmnt. Now ll through th ngotitions Wltr Whit ws Rndolph's chif frind nd dvisr.

Yt Rndolph ws th mn in front. Why? Chifly bcus Rndolph hd grt rputtion s lbor ldr. Rndolph hd orgnizd th Pullmn portrs, th bst lbor orgniztion tht Ngros hv tody. Th struggls of Ngros r first nd formost lbor struggls, prticulrly th struggl for jobs in th wr industris. So vrybody lookd to Rndolph.

## WH H Rd H vN

Why did thy strt th movmnt t ll? Th Ngros wr clmoring for ction. All ovr th country thr ws th usul unrst nd disstisfction, but this tim linkd with th dsir for ction. Whit nd Rndolph sw tht if thy did not tk th ldrship of th movmnt, th Stlinists, using thir stoog orgniztion, th Ntionl Ngro Congrss, would tk it ovr. At tht tim (not tody ny longr) th Stlinists wr clling th wr n imprilist wr nd wr doing ll thy could to confus nd mbrrss th Roosvlt govrnmnt. So tht if thy lt th Stlinists gt wy with th ldrship, Whit nd Rndolph would b discrditd, mss Ngro movmnt would gt undr wy nd lv thm bhind; nd Ngros would b undr Stlinist influnc. Whit nd Rndolph thrfor cm out s ldrs.

Roosvlt, howvr, didn't wnt ny Mrch on Wshington. Wht th ruling clss hts bov ll is indpndnt mss ction. Abov ll, th workrs must lwys b told wht to do. Thy must nvr do nything thmslvs. And prticulrly Ngros. This mrch would hv mnt trmndous wkning of Ngros ll ovr th country. It would hv shown th hypocrisy of th Amricn cpitlist clss, prtnding tht it ws fighting for dmocrcy brod, whn it ws stmping s usul on th dmocrtic rights of th poor t hom, [so] Roosvlt st out to brk th mrch.

On Jun 10 Elnor Roosvlt wrot lttr to Rndolph. Sh clld th mrch "vry grv mistk." Following this, Elnor Roosvlt, [Fiorllo] LGurdi, Rndolph, nd Whit hd confrnc t City Hll in Nw York. Whit nd Rndolph wr now in mss.

[Hnry] Stimson, Scrtry of Wr, nd [Frnk] Knox, Scrtry of th Nvy, snt tlgrm to Rndolph sking him to com to Wshington for confrnc. Thr ws lot of going nd coming nd in th nd Roosvlt issud his xcutiv ordr, which dos not mn on dmn thing bcus thr r no pnltis to b imposd on thos who continu to discrimint ginst Ngros. But Whit nd Rndolph hv don thir work. Thy hv killd th grtst indpndnt ction th Ngros hv undrtkn for gnrtion.

## R N FNHd

Th story isn't finishd yt, howvr, nd it isn't going to b finishd for long tim. Hr r two nw chptrs, vry short but vry significnt: Rndolph got on th rdio fw dys go nd sid tht soon th prsidnt would issu nothr xcutiv ordr bolishing discrimintion in th Army nd Nvy for ll tim. This cn mn only on thing. Sombody hs promisd Rndolph job in govrnmnt, for no mn in his snss would tlk such nonsns, xcpt h ws doing it for purpos.

At th sm tim Glnn L. Mrtin, whos irpln plnt t Middl Rivr, Mrylnd, hs hlf billion dollrs' worth of wr contrcts, ws skd wht his [rspons] would b if th prsidnt trid to nforc th xcutiv ordr. H rplid: "Immdit stoppg of work." In othr words, "To hll with th prsidnt."

Ngros, tht is wht w hv to dl with. Ngro-hting cpitlists, sly politicins lik Roosvlt, nd trchrous stoogs lik Whit nd Rndolph. W must orgniz ourslvs, with our own lctd committs nd dpnd only on ourslvs.

# 4.3 Harlem Negroes Protest Jim Crow Discrimination C. L. R. J

"Shame has come to our city and sorrow to the large number of our fellow citizens, decent, law-abiding citizens, who live in the Harlem section."

Thus the first citizen of New York describes the demonstration in Harlem of the Negro people which has resulted in half a dozen deaths, scores of wounded, and hundreds of arrests.

So that, according to Mayor La Guardia, when the Negro people demonstrate, shame comes to the citizens. Shame did come to La Guardia himself when he insulted the Negro people by signing the contract for the Metropolitan Insurance Company housing project which expressly stipulated the exclusion of Negroes.

Shame does not come to the decent, law-abiding citizens in the White House in Washington and the decent, law-abiding citizens in Congress who have insulted the Negro people by segregating them in the federal government, by segregating them in the Army, in the Navy, in the Air Force, and in women's auxiliaries.

Shame does not come to Secretary of War Stimson and Secretary of the Navy Knox when the men they have inducted into the Army and Navy are shot down by military police and Southern civilians, are Jim Crowed and ill-treated on their way to the camps, are segregated in the camps themselves, persecuted, maltreated, and lynched without any protection from the government.

We have not seen shame in the industrialists and men of business who in the very City of New York will not employ Negroes, and only when they are compelled to and have no other means of evasion, grudgingly give them work in industry, that same industry which is supposed to be doing all that is possible to win the great "war for democracy."

All ths things cn b don by th "dcnt, lw-biding citizns" who mrly continu th thr-hundrd-yr-old prscution of th Ngro popl which hs lwys chrctrizd Amricn cpitlist socity.

But whn th Ngros in Hrlm bcom xsprtd to th utmost limit by th combind prscution nd hypocrisy of thir lords nd mstrs, nd dcid tht thy will show thir rsntmnt in th only wy tht sms possibl to thm, thn is th tim whn LGurdi gos to th microphon nd informs th public tht this indd is shm.

This, nd not th prscution, is th scndl. This, nd not th hypocrisy, is th disgrc. Not thos who insult th Ngro popl, not thos who insult th intllignc of th Ngros by th prptul bwling nd ylling to thm bout th "wr for dmocrcy."

No! According to LGurdi, th dmonstrtion ginst ths things, tht is th shm.

## WH KNd F dNRN W ?

Th myor himslf hs informd th public tht th uphvl in Hrlm ws dmonstrtion. A dmonstrtion ginst wht? Sinc whn is it shmful to dmonstrt ginst lynch lw, sgrgtion, discrimintion, nd hypocrisy in high plcs, msqurding bfor th popl s "wr for dmocrcy"? In this truly shmful hypocrisy, LGurdi is only on of mny. All th prss, ll th worthy citizns, not only in Nw York, but in Dtroit, in Mobil, in Bumont, nd in Sn Frncisco, ll gt togthr nd in on loud, clr, nd mournful voic shk thir hds nd sy to th protsting Ngro popl, "Wht shm!"

Th popl in Hrlm r xsprtd byond ndurnc by th situ tion of th Ngro popl in th Unitd Stts s whol nd th continuous contrdiction btwn bing prscutd by dmocrcy nd thn bing told tht thy must di for tht dmocrcy.

### GLOBAL COMMONS, WORLD BANK COMMONS

First, since at least the early 1990s, the language of the commons has been appropriated by the World Bank and the United Nations, and put at the service of privatization. Under the guise of protecting biodiversity and conserving "global commons," the bank has turned rain forests into ecological reserves, has expelled the populations that for centuries had drawn their sustenance from them, while making them available to people who do not need them but can pay for them, for instance, through ecotourism. On its side, the United Nations, in the name again of preserving the common heritage of mankind, has revised the international law governing access to the oceans, in ways enabling governments to consolidate the use of seawaters in fewer hands.

The World Bank and the United Nations are not alone in their adaptation of the idea of the commons to market interests. Responding to different motivations, a revalorization of the commons has become trendy among mainstream economists and capitalist planners, witness the growing academic literature on the subject and its cognates: "social capital," "gift economies," "altruism." Witness also the official recognition of this trend through the conferral of the Nobel Prize for Economics in 2009 to the leading voice in this field, the political scientist Elinor Ostrom.7

Development planners and policy makers have discovered that, under proper conditions, a collective management of natural resources can be more efficient and less conflictual than privatization, and commons can very well be made to produce for the market. They have also recognized that, carried to the extreme, the commodification of social relations has self-defeating consequences. The extension of the commodity-form to every corner of the social factory, which neoliberalism has promoted, is an ideal limit for capitalist ideologues, but it is a project not only unrealizable but undesirable from the viewpoint of the long-term reproduction of the capitalist system. Capitalist accumulation is structurally dependent on the free appropriation of immense areas of labor and resources that must appear as externalities to the market, like the unpaid domestic work that women have provided, on which employers have relied for the reproduction of the workforce.

Not accidentally, then, long before the Wall Street "meltdown," a variety of economists and social theorists warned that the marketization of all spheres of life is detrimental to the market's well-functioning, for markets too—the argument goes—depend on the existence of nonmonetary relations like confidence, trust, and gift-giving. In brief, capital is learning about the virtues of the "common good." In its July 31, 2008, issue, even the London *Economist*, the organ of capitalist free-market economics for more than 150 years, cautiously joined the chorus. "The economics of the new commons," the journal wrote, "is still in its infncy. It is too soon to b confidnt bout its hypothss. But it my yt prov usful wy of thinking bout problms, such s mnging th intrnt, intllctul proprty or intrntionl pollution, on which policymkrs nd ll th hlp thy cn gt." W must b vry crful, thn, not to crft th discours on th commons in such wy s to llow crisis-riddn cpitlist clss to rviv itslf, posturing, for instnc, s th gurdin of th plnt.

Pus.

When you design an event/campaign/et cetera based on the work of queer Black women, don't invite them to participate in shaping it but ask them to provide materials and ideas for next steps for said event; that is racism in practice. Perhaps if we were the charismatic Black men many are rallying around these days, it would have been a different story, but being queer Black women in this society (and apparently within these movements) tends to equal invisibility and nonrelevancy.

And for thos who w hv not yt hd th opportunity to ngg with round th dpttions of th Blck Livs Mttr cll, pls considr th following points.

# 5 | Critical Pedagogy

Because the society we live in is devoted to acquiring property and making a profit, most people see the having mode as the most natural mode of existence. . . . Our education generally tries to train people to have knowledge as a possession, by and large commensurate with the amount of property or social prestige they are likely to have in later life. The minimum they receive is the amount they will need in order to function properly in their work.

-Erich Fromm, To Have or To Be?

To educate the masses politically does not mean, cannot mean, making a political speech. What it means is to try, relentlessly and passionately, to teach the masses that everything depends on them . . . that there is no famous man who will take the responsibility for everything, but that the demiurge is the people themselves and the magic hands are finally only the hands of the people. . . . A government or a party gets the people it deserves, and sooner or later a people gets the government it deserves.

-Frantz Fanon, The Wretched of the Earth

# **5.1** Beyond Dystopian Visions in the Age of Neoliberal Violence

## HENRY A. GIROUX

George Orwell's nightmarish vision of a totalitarian society casts a dark shadow over the United States. We live at a time in which institutions that were meant to limit human suffering and misfortune and protect the public from the excesses of the market have been either weakened or abolished. The consequences of this speak to a different experience of total terror in the twenty-first century. The basic elements can be seen clearly in the ongoing and ruthless assault on the social state, unions, higher education, workers, students, poor minority youth, and any vestige of the social contract. Free market policies, values, and practices with their emphasis on the privatization of public wealth, the elimination of social protections, and the deregulation of economic activity now shape practically every commanding political and economic institution in both countries. We don't see the work camps or death camps that characterize the catastrophes o micentury totalitarian regimes. But as a generation o black youth can attest, you on't hae to be in jail to eel imprisone, especially when it is increasingly iicult to take control o one's lie an means in a meaningul way.

We lie at a time when politics is nation-base an power is global. Global markets now trump the national, renering the political culture an institutions o moernity obsolete. The inancial elite now loat beyon national borers an no longer care about the welare state, the common goo, or or that matter any institution not suborinate to the ictates o inance capitalism. Hence, the ruling elites make no concessions in their pursuits o power an proits. The social contract o the past, especially in the Unite States, is now on lie support as social proisions are cut, pensions are ecimate, an the certainty o a once-secure job isappears. Many neoliberal societies are now goerne by politicians an inancial elites who no longer beliee in social inestments an are more than willing to conemn young people an others—oten paralyze by the precariousness an instability that haunts their lies an uture—to a saage orm o casino capitalism.

The mantras o eregulation, priatization, commoiication, an the unimpee low o capital now rie politics an concentrate power in the hans o the 1 percent. Class warare has merge with neoconseratie polices to engage in permanent warare both abroa an at home. There are no sae spaces ree rom the reach o capital an the tentacles o the sureillance an punishing state. The basic imperaties o casino capitalism—extening rom eliminating corporate taxes an shiting wealth rom the public to the priate sector to ismantling corporate regulations an insisting that markets shoul goern all o social lie—hae become the new common sense. Any iable notion o the social, soliarity, an share emocratic alues are now iewe as a pathology, replace by a surial-o-the-ittest ethic, the celebration o sel-interest, an a notion o the goo lie entirely tie to a api consumerist ethic.

With the return o the new Gile Age, not only are emocratic institutions, alues, an social protections at risk in many countries, but the ciic, peagogical, an ormatie cultures that make them central to emocratic lie are in anger o isappearing altogether. Poerty, joblessness, low-wage work, an the threat o state-sanctione iolence prouce among many populations the ongoing ear o a lie o perpetual misery an an ongoing struggle simply to surie. Insecurity couple with a climate o ear an sureillance ampens issent an promotes an ethical tranquilization e aily by the mobilization o moral panics, whether they reerence the iolence o lone omestic terrorists, ISIS thugs blowing up malls, immigrants swarming across borers, or gay people seeking marriage certiicates.

Underlying the rise of the authoritarian state and the forces that hide in the shadows is a politics indebted to promoting historical and social amnesia. The new authoritarianism is strongly indebted to what Orwell once called a "protective stupidity" that negates political life and divests language of its critical content.3 Neoliberal authoritarianism has changed the language of politics and everyday life through a malicious public pedagogy that turns reason on its head and normalizes a culture of fear, war, surveillance, and exploitation. That is, the heavy hand of Orwellian control is evident in those dominant cultural apparatuses that extend from schools to print, audio, and screen cultures, which now serve as disimagination machines attacking any critical notion of politics that makes a claim to be educative in its attempts to enable the conditions for changing "the ways in which people might think critically."4

Higher education represents one area where neoliberalism wages war on any field of study that might encourage students to think critically. One egregious example was on full display in North Carolina, where Republican Party members who control the Board of Governors decimated higher education in that state by voting to cut forty-six degree programs. One member defended such cuts with the comment: "We're capitalists, and we have to look at what the demand is, and we have to respond to the demand."5 This is more than an example of crude economic instrumentalism, it is also a recipe for instituting an academic culture of thoughtlessness and a kind of stupidity receptive to what Hannah Arendt once called totalitarianism. In Wisconsin, Governor Scott Walker has eliminated tenure at Wisconsin's public universities and eviscerated any vestige of shared governance. 6 He also cut \$200 million from the state higher education budget, which is not surprising given his hatred of public education.

Both of these examples point to a new breed of politician waging war on higher education, critical pedagogy, the public good, and any viable notion of the social state. Like many of their politically extremist colleagues, they reflect an era that exhibits zero tolerance for economic and racial justice and "infinite tolerance for the crimes of bankers and government embezzlers which affect the lives of millions." Under such conditions, material violence is now matched by symbolic violence, as made evident by the proliferation of images, institutions, and narratives that legitimate not only the manufactured ignorance of consumer culture and its corollary worship of wealth and celebrity but also what might be called an expanding politics of disposability.

Rendered redundant as a result of the collapse of the welfare state, a pervasive racism, a growing disparity in income and wealth, and a take-noprisoners market-driven ideology, an increasing number of individuals and groups—especially young people, low-income groups, and minorities of class an color—are being emonize, criminalize, or simply abanone by irtue o their inability to participate in rituals o consumption ue to low-paying jobs, poor health, or pressing amily nees. Du Bois, become problem people rather than people who ace problems.

Alreay isenranchise by irtue o their age, young people are uner assault in ways that are entirely new because they now ace a worl that is ar more precarious than at any other time in recent history. Increasing numbers o youth suer mental anguish an oert istress een, perhaps especially, among the college boun, ebt-rien, an unemploye whose numbers are growing exponentially. Many reports claim that "young Americans are suering rom rising leels o anxiety, stress, epression an een suicie." For example, "one out o eery ie young people an one out o eery our college stuents . . . suers rom some orm o iagnosable mental illness."

The politics o isposability with its expaning machineries o ciic an social eath, terminal exclusion, an zones o abanonment represents a angerous historical moment an must be aresse within the context o a market-rien society that is rewriting the meaning o common sense, agency, esire, an politics itsel. Ater the 2008 recession, the capitalist ream machine is back with huge proits or hege un managers, major players in the inancial serice inustries, an the enizens o the ultra rich. In these new lanscapes o wealth, exclusion, an rau, the commaning institutions o casino capitalism promote a winner-take-all ethos an aggressiely unermine a more egalitarian istribution o wealth ia corporate taxation. In aition, the inancial elite eun crucial social serices such as oo stamp programs or poor chilren an attack labor unions, gay rights, an women's reprouctie rights while waging a counterreolution against the principles o social citizenship an emocracy. In this instance, the war on the poor, women, black youth, immigrants, an labor is part o the war on emocracy an signiies a new thrust towar what might be calle the authoritarian rule o corporate soereignty an goernance.

Politics an power are now on the sie o legally protecte lawlessness, as is eient in the state's enless iolations o ciil liberties, reeom o speech, an many constitutional rights, mostly one in the name o national security. Lawlessness wraps itsel in goernment ictates, as is eient in such policies as the Patriot Act, the National Deense Authorization Act, the Military Commissions Act, an a host o other legal illegalities. These woul inclue the right o the presient "to orer the assassination o any citizen whom he consiers allie with terrorists," to use secret eience to etain iniiuals ineinitely, to eelop a massie sureillance apparatus to monitor eery auio an electronic communication use by citizens who hae not committe a crime, to employ state torture against those consiere enemy combatants, an to block the courts rom prosecuting oicials who commit such heinous crimes. In reaing Orwell's ystopia, what becomes clear is that his nightmarish uture has become our present, an there is more uner assault than simply the iniiual's right to priacy.

Power in its most oppressie orms is eploye not only by arious repressie goernment policies an intelligence agencies but also through a preatory an market-rien culture that turns iolence into entertainment, oreign aggression into ieo games, an omestic iolence into a goosestepping celebration o masculinity an the ma alues o unbrile militarism. At the same time, the increasing circulation o public narraties an public isplays o cruelty an moral inierence continue to maim an su ocate the exercise o reason an social responsibility. What we hae been witnessing in the Unite States since the 1980s an the Reagan-Thatcher isaowal o all things social is a kin o harening o the culture marke by an increasing inierence to matters o empathy an an erasure o ethical consierations.

Eience o such cruelty is eerywhere. The kis woul actually o work, they woul hae cash, they woul hae prie in the school, they' begin the process o rising." Neoliberalism has prouce a broa lanscape o cruelty, precarity, an isposability. Another instance o the culture o cruelty can be seen in the high-octane an unethical grammars o iolence that now oer the only currency with any enuring alue or meiating relationships, aressing problems, an oering instant pleasure in the larger culture. Neeless to say, extreme iolence is more than a spectacle or upping the pleasure quotient o those isengage rom politics; it is also part o a punishing machine that spens more on putting poor minorities in jail than eucating them. In the Unite States, local police orces, in particular, hae been outitte with ull riot gear, submachine guns, armore ehicles, an other lethal weapons importe rom the battleiels o Iraq an Aghanistan, reinorcing their mission to assume battle-reay behaior. The police in too many cities hae been transorme into soliers just as ialogue an community policing hae been replace by military-style practices that are way out o proportion to the crimes the police are traine to aress. For instance, the Economist reporte that "SWAT teams were eploye about 3,000 times in 1980 but are now use aroun 50,000 times a year. Some cities use them or routine patrols in high-crime areas. Baltimore an Dallas hae use them to break up poker games." Such egregious uses o police time an taxpayer ollars woul appear iiotic i they weren't so saage.

In the aent o the recent isplays o police orce in Ferguson, Missouri, an Baltimore, Marylan, it is not surprising that the impact o the rapi militarization o local police on poor black communities is nothing short o terriying an yet eeply symptomatic o the iolence that takes place in authoritarian societies. For instance, Michelle Alexaner exposes the racist nature of the punishing state by pointing out that "there are more African American adults under correctional control today—in prison or jail, on probation or parole—than were enslaved in 1850, a decade before the Civil War began." When young black boys and girls see people in their neighborhood killed by the police for making eye contact, holding a toy gun, walking in a stairway, or selling cigarettes while "the financial elite go free for a bookmaking operation that almost brought the country to economic ruin," not only do the police lose their legitimacy, but so do established norms of lawfulness and modes of governance. 21

In terms reminiscent of Orwell, morality loses its emancipatory possibilities and degenerates into a pathology in which individual misery is denounced as a moral failing. Under the neo-Darwinian ethos of survival of the fittest, the ultimate form of entertainment becomes the pain and humiliation of others, especially those considered disposable and powerless, who are no longer objects of compassion but of ridicule and amusement. They populate the stories we are now hearing from U. S. politicians who disdain the poor as moochers who don't need social assistance but stronger morals. Jeb Bush echoes this argument in his claim that if he were elected president, he wouldn't be giving black people "free stuff,"22 as if black Americans are on welfare because they are lazy and are "plagued by pathological dependence." These narratives can also be heard from conservative pundits such as New York Times columnist David Brooks, who insists that poverty is a matter of the poor lacking virtue, middle-class norms, and decent moral codes.24 For Brooks, the problems of the poor and disadvantaged can be solved "through moral education and selfreliance . . . high-quality relationships and strong familial ties."25

In this discourse, soaring inequality in wealth and income, high levels of unemployment, stagnant economic growth, and low wages for millions of working Americans are willfully covered over and covered up. What Brooks, Bush, and other conservatives consistently obfuscate is the racist nature of the drug war, police violence, the stranglehold of the criminal justice system on poor black communities, the egregious effect of "racially skewed patterns of mass incarceration," mass unemployment for underserved youth, and poor-quality education in low income-neighborhoods. 26 Paul Krugman gets it right in rebutting the argument that all the poor need are the virtues of middle-class morality and a good dose of resilience. 27 He counters, "The poor don't need lectures on morality, they need more resources—which we can afford to provide—and better economic opportunities, which we can also afford to provide through everything from training and subsidies to higher minimum wages."

As the claims and promises of a neoliberal utopia have been transformed into an Orwellian nightmare, the United States continues to succumb to the pathology o inancial speculation, political corruption, the reistribution o wealth upwar into the hans o the 1 percent, the rise o the sureillance state, an the use o the criminal justice system as a way o ealing with social problems. At the same time, Orwell's ark antasy o an authoritarian uture continues without enough massie opposition. Stuents, low-income whites, an poor minority youth are expose to a low-intensity war in which they are hel hostage to a uture o low expectations, police iolence, an atomizing consumer culture, a growing anti-intellectualism an religious unamentalism in American society, corporate an goernment moes o sureillance, an the buren o extreme ebt.

No emocracy can surie the kin o inequality in which "the 400 richest people . . . hae as much wealth as 154 million Americans combine, that's 50 percent o the entire country [while] the top economic 1 percent o the U. S. population now has a recor 40 percent o all wealth an more wealth than 90 percent o the population combine." On a global scale, accoring to a stuy by antipoerty charity Oxam, it reports that it expects "the wealthiest 1% to own more than 50% o the worl's wealth by 2016." Within such iniquitous conitions o power, access, an wealth, a society cannot oster a sense o organize responsibility unamental to a emocracy. Instea, it encourages a sense o organize irresponsibility—a practice that unerlies the economic Darwinism an ciic corruption at the heart o a ebase politics.

What role might eucation an critical peagogy hae in a society in which the social has been iniiualize, emotional lie collapses into the therapeutic, an eucation is relegate to either a priate aair or a kin o algorithmic moe o regulation in which eerything is reuce to a esire measureable outcome? Feeback loops now replace politics an the concept o reolution is eine through the culture o measurement an eiciency. In a culture rowning in a new loe aair with empiricism an ata collecting, that which is not measurable—such as compassion, ision, the imagination, care or the other, an a passion or justice—withers. In its place emerges what Francisco Goya calle in one o his engraings, The Sleep of Reason Produces Monster. Goya's title is richly suggestie, particularly about the role o eucation an peagogy in compelling stuents to be able to recognize, as my colleague Dai Clark points out, "that an inattentieness to the neer-ening task o critique brees horrors: the ailures o conscience, the wars against thought, an the lirtations with irrationality that lie at the heart o the triumph o eeryay aggression, the withering o political lie, an the withrawal into priate obsessions."

The tentacles o power (that are clumsily tucke behin the acuous claims to emocratic goernance) maniest themseles in the rise o a punishing state an a totalitarian paranoia in which eeryone is consiere a potential terrorist or criminal. How else o we explain the increasing criminalization o social problems such as homelessness an the ailure o the poor to pay court costs, to say nothing o arresting stuents or triial inractions such as ooling on a esk or throwing peanuts at a bus, all o which can lan the most ulnerable in jail? In act, I hae long argue that there is a har an sot war being wage against young people. The har war is taking place in many schools, which now resemble prisons in light o their lockown proceures, zero tolerance policies, metal etectors, an the increasing presence o police in the schools.

The sot war is the war o consumerism an inance. Partnere with a massie aertising machinery an ariety o corporate institutions, the sot war targets all youth by treating them as yet another "market" to be commoiie an exploite, while attempting to create a new generation o hyperconsumers. In the Unite States the aerage stuent grauates with a loan ebt o \$27,000. Debt bonage is the ultimate isciplinary technique o casino capitalism to rob stuents o the time to think, issuae them rom entering public serice, an reinorce the ebase assumption that they shoul simply be eicient cogs in a consumer economy.

I neoliberal authoritarianism is to be challenge an oercome, it is crucial that intellectuals, unions, workers, young people, an arious social moements unite to reclaim emocracy as a central element in ashioning a raical imagination. Such action necessitates interrogating an rupturing the material an symbolic orces that hie behin a countereit claim to participatory emocracy. This requires rescuing the promises o a raical emocracy that can proie a liing wage, quality healthcare or all, public works, an massie inestments in eucation, chil care, an housing or the poor, along with a range o other crucial social proisions that can make a ierence between liing an ying or those who hae been relegate to the ranks o the isposable.

The growing global threat o neoliberal authoritarianism signals both a crisis o politics an a crisis o belies, alues, an iniiual an social agency. One inication o such a crisis is the act that the economic calamity o 2008 has not been matche by a shit in ieas about the nature o inance capital an its eastating eects on American society. Banks got baile out, an eeryay Americans who lost their houses bore the brunt o the crisis. Politics is an imminently eucatie task, an it is only through such recognition that initial steps can be taken to challenge the powerul ieological an aectie spaces through which market unamentalism prouces the esires, ientities, an alues that bin people to its orms o preatory go ernance. But the question remains regaring how a public largely inierent to politics an oten paralyze by the nee to surie, an caught in a crippling cynicism, can be moe rom "an inuce state o stupiity" to a political ormation willing to engage in arious moes o resistance extening rom "mass protests to prolonge ciil isobeience." This terriying intellectual an moral paralysis must be oset by the eelopment o alternatie public spheres in which eucators, artists, workers, young people, an others can change the terms o the ebate in American culture an politics. Ieas matter, but they wither without institutional inrastructures in which they can be nourishe, ebate, an acte on. Any iable struggle against casino capitalism must ocus on orms o omination that pose a threat to public spheres, such as public an higher eucation an the new meia, that are essential to eeloping the critical ormatie cultures, ientities, an esires that nourish moes o engage thinking necessary or the prouction o critically engage citizens.

I such a politics is to make any ierence, it must be worlly; that is, it must incorporate a critical isposition that both aresses social problems an tackles the conitions necessary or moes o emocratic political exchange that enable new orms o agency, power, an collectie struggle. Until politics can be mae meaningul in orer to be mae critical an transormatie, there will be no signiicant opposition to casino capitalism.

I want to conclue by pointing to a ew initiaties, though incomplete, that might mount a challenge to the current oppressie historical conjuncture in which many Americans now in themseles. In oing so, I want to a ress what I hae attempte to map as a crisis o memory, agency, an eucation an reclaim what I call a peagogy o eucate hope that is central to any iable notion o change that I am suggesting.

First, there is a nee or what can be calle a reial o the raical imagination an the eense o the public goo, especially higher eucation, in orer to reclaim its egalitarian an emocratic impulses. This call woul be part o a larger project "to reinent emocracy in the wake o the eience that, at the national leel, there is no emocracy—i by 'emocracy' we mean eectie popular participation in the crucial ecisions aecting the community." One step in this irection woul be or young people, intellectuals, scholars, an others to go on the oensie against a conseratie-le campaign "to en higher eucation's emocratizing inluence on the nation." Higher eucation shoul be harnesse neither to the emans o the warare state nor to the instrumental nees o corporations. Clearly, in any emocratic society, eucation shoul be iewe as a right, not an entitlement.

Politically, this suggests eining higher eucation as a emocratic public sphere an rejecting the notion that the culture o eucation is synonymous with the culture o business. Peagogically, this points to moes o teaching an learning capable o proucing an inorme public, enacting an sustaining a culture o questioning, an enabling a critical ormatie culture that aances at least in the schools what Kristen Case calls "moments o classroom grace." Peagogies o classroom grace shoul proie the conitions or stuents an others to relect critically on commonsense unerstanings o the worl, an begin to question, howeer troubling, their sense o agency, their relationship to others, an their relationships to the larger worl. This can be linke to broaer peagogical imperaties that ask why we hae wars, massie inequality, a sureillance state, the commoiication o eerything, an the collapse o the public into the priate. This is not merely a methoical consieration but also a moral an political practice because it presupposes the creation o critically engage stuents who can imagine a uture in which justice, equality, reeom, an emocracy matter. In this instance, the classroom shoul be a space o grace—a place to think critically, ask troubling questions, an take risks, een though that may mean transgressing establishe norms an bureaucratic proceures.

Secon, young people an progressies nee to eelop a comprehensie eucational program that woul inclue a range o peagogical initiaties rom eeloping a national online news channel to creating alternatie schools or young people in the manner o the ierse emocratically inspire schools such as Highlaner uner Miles Horton, the Workers College in New York, an a host o other alternatie eucational institutions. Such a peagogical task woul enable a sustaine critique o the transormation o a market economy into a market society along with a clear analysis o the amage it has cause both at home an abroa. What is crucial to recognize here is that it is not enough to teach stuents to be able to interrogate an critically screen culture an other orms o aural, ieo, an isual orms o representations. They must also learn how to be cultural proucers. This suggests eeloping alternatie public spheres such as online journals, teleision shows, newspapers, zines, an any other platorm in which alternatie positions can be eelope. In aition, such tasks can be one by mobilizing the technological resources an platorms they alreay hae. It also means working with one oot in existing cultural apparatuses in orer to promote alternatie ieas an iews that woul challenge the aectie an ieological spaces prouce by the inancial elite who control the commaning institutions o public peagogy in North America.

Thir, acaemics, artists, community actiists, young people, an parents must engage in an ongoing struggle or the right o stuents to be gien a ormiable an critical eucation not ominate by corporate alues an or young people to hae a say in the shaping o their eucation an what it means to expan an eepen the practice o reeom an emocracy. Young people hae been let out o the iscourse o emocracy. They are the new isposables who lack jobs, a ecent eucation, hope, an any semblance o a uture better than the one their parents inherite. Facing what Richar Sennett calls the "spectre o uselessness," they are a reminer o how inance capital has abanone any iable ision o the uture, incluing one that woul support uture generations. This is a moe o politics an capital that eats its own chilren an throws their ate to the agaries o the market. The ecology o inance capital only beliees in short-term inestments because they proie quick returns. Uner such circumstances, young people who nee long-term inestments are consiere a liability. I any society is in part juge by how it iews an treats its chilren, the Unite States by all accounts is truly ailing in a colossal way.

Fourth, casino capitalism is so wiesprea that progressies nee to e elop a comprehensie ision o politics that "oes not rely on single issues." It is only through an unerstaning o the wier relations an connections o power that young people an others can oercome uninorme practice, isolate struggles, an moes o singular politics that become insular an sel-sabotaging. In short, moing beyon this single-issue orientation means eeloping moes o analyses that connect the ots historically an relationally. It also means eeloping a more comprehensie ision o politics an change. The key here is the notion o translation; that is, the nee to translate priate troubles into broaer public issues an unerstan how systemic moes o analyses can be helpul in connecting a range o issues so as to be able to buil a unite ront in the call or a raical emocracy.

This is a particularly important goal gien that the ragmentation o the Let has been partly responsible or its inability to eelop a wie political an ieological umbrella to aress a range o problems: extreme poerty; the assault on the enironment; the emergence o the permanent warare state; the rollback o oting rights; the assault on public serants, women's rights, an social proisions; an other issues that eroe the possibilities or a raical emocracy. The ominating mechanisms o casino capitalism in both their symbolic an material registers reach eeply into eery aspect o American society. Any successul moement or the eense o public goos an emocracy itsel will hae to struggle against this new moe o authoritarianism rather than isolating an attacking speciic elements o its antiemocratic ethos.

One important eelopment is that black youth, among other concerne young Americans, are currently making real stries in moing beyon sporaic protests, short-lie emonstrations, an noniolent street actions in the hopes o builing sustaine political moements. Groups such as Black Lies Matter, Black Youth Project, We Charge Genocie, Dream Deeners, an others represent a new an growing political orce that is not only connecting police iolence to larger structures o militarism throughout society but also reclaiming public memory by articulating a irect link "between the establishment o proessional police systems in the Unite States [an] the patrolling systems that maintaine the business o human bonage in chattel slaery."

Fith, another serious challenge acing aocates o a new truly emocratic social orer is the task o eeloping a iscourse o both critique an possibility or what I hae calle a iscourse o eucate hope. Critique is important an is crucial to break the hol o commonsense assumptions that legitimate a wie range o injustices. The language o critique is also crucial or making isible the workings o unequal power an the necessity o holing authority accountable. But critique is not enough, an without a iscourse o hope, it can lea to a paralyzing espair or, een worse, a crippling cynicism. Hope speaks to imagining a lie beyon capitalism an combines a realistic sense o limits with a loty ision o emaning the impossible. As Ernst Bloch once insiste, reason, justice, an change cannot blossom without hope because eucate hope taps into our eepest experiences an longing or a lie o ignity with others, a lie in which it becomes possible to imagine a uture that oes not mimic the present. I am reerring not to a romanticize an empty notion o hope but to a notion o inorme hope that faces the concrete obstacles and realities of domination but continues the ongoing task of "holding the present open and thus unfinished."43

The discourse of possibility not only looks for productive solutions; it also is crucial in defending public spheres in which civic values, public scholarship, and social engagement allow for a more imaginative grasp of a future that takes seriously the demands of justice, equity, and civic courage. Democracy should encourage, even require, a way of thinking critically about education, one that connects equity to excellence, learning to ethics, and agency to the imperatives of social responsibility and the public good. Casino capitalism is a toxin that has created a predatory class of unethical zombies who are producing dead zones of the imagination that even Orwell could not have envisioned—all the while waging a fierce fight against the possibilities of a democratic future. The time has come to develop a political language in which civic values, social responsibility, and the institutions that support them become central to invigorating and fortifying a new era of civic imagination, a renewed sense of social agency, and an impassioned international social movement with a vision, organization, and set of strategies to challenge the neoliberal nightmare engulfing the planet. These may be dark times, as Hannah Arendt once warned, 44 but they don't have to be, and that raises serious questions about what educators, artists, youth, intellectuals, and others are going to do within the current historical climate to make sure that they do not succumb to the authoritarian forces circling American society, waiting for the resistance to stop and for the lights to go out. History is open, and as Howard Zinn once insisted, hope is the willingness "to hold out, even in times of pessimism, the possibility of surprise."45

# **5.2** Chapman Democracy Activist Offers a Radical Critique of Capitalism

## Interview with Peter Mc Laren

## **JONATHAN WINSLOW**

Peter Mc Laren is the codirector of Chapman University's Paulo Freire Democratic Project and the university's international ambassador for global ethics and social justice. He also happens to be one of the world's foremost thinkers on critical pedagogy. Critical pedagogy is a cross-section between philosophy and education that works to equip students to identify oppression in the world and deal with it in a constructive manner. Mc Laren has written and edited nearly fifty books in his field.

Once an elementary and middle school teacher in Canada, Mc Laren taught at a comfortable school in a comfortable neighborhood until one day he had a troubling thought: social class is one of the biggest predictors of success, and it was entirely likely that those students could succeed not because of their teachers—but, perhaps, even in spite of them. Mc Laren decided to go somewhere where he could have a more profound effect—the inner city.

While others may have been content with helping at least one of their students, Mc Laren set higher standards—he wanted to make a difference for everyone. He found a bond with his students that his colleagues didn't seem to have. At one point, he cleared everything out of his classroom and filled it with drums instead. Rather than teaching the course material, he drummed and connected with his students. Test scores actually went up.

This was the beginning of Mc Laren's path of social justice. Today, he encourages critical analysis, especially of capitalism. Inspired by his mentor Paulo Freire, one of the fathers of critical pedagogy, Mc Laren strives to equip students with the means to make sense of their experiences and look critically at the world, possibly opening the door to a better system.

Mc Laren's latest book, Pedagogy of Insurrection, was publishe in 2015. In this book, Mc Laren raws on his backgroun as a eout Catholic, looking at the teachings o Jesus as they relate to capitalism. In what he expects to be a controersial moe, he argues that Jesus was eectiely preaching communism, an that his message o loe might open the way to a better ethical approach to social justice in moern times.

We sat own with Mc Laren to gain some insight on his theoretical work.

### Q. Just for starters, how exactly would you describe critical pedagogy?

. I think that most people mistake critical peagogy with critical thinking. It's part o it, but you can't reuce critical peagogy to critical thinking.

Critical peagogy has an ethical ounation. You can't moe rom act to alue; there's no logical sequence that will take you rom a critique o capitalism to say that capitalism, thereore, is a ba thing. That requires an ethical jugment. For me, critical peagogy is a philosophy o praxis. Praxis is an interesting term—it's the ialectical relationship between theory an practice.

### Q. What are some of the key ideas you discuss in your books and talks?

. Praxis relates to changing the worl. For me, in changing the worl, I name what the problem is. For me, that problem is capitalism—the prouction o alue. Now, alue an wealth are ery ierent. Value is monetize wealth.

In my work in Latin America an with poor communities all aroun the worl, I'e come to the realization that critical peagogy requires an ethical commitment. That ethical commitment is to eliminate unnecessary, neeless suering. That's a moral posture that one takes. Liberation theology calls this a preerential option or the poor. I go arther than that; I say it's not a preerential option but an obligation.

Critical peagogy is mobilize towar ining a more humane alternatie to capitalism. Some critics will say that capitalism isn't perect, but it's the best system we hae so we can just try to make it better. It's the nature o capitalism itsel—the alienation o human labor, the exploitation o human labor through proit making—that makes it impossible to reorm.

Now, I'm not suggesting that we gie any creibility to Soiet communism or Eastern Bloc police states; they were horrible regimes that misunerstoo, manipulate, an misuse the writings o Marx. So where o we look? Well, we can look at the Spanish Ciil War, the Paris Commune an we can look at inigenous communities throughout Latin America like the Zapatistas. My emphasis is on encouraging teachers to be public intellectuals—to assume that role, not o being a clerk o the empire. Not to be a unctionary o the school boar but to become transormatie, critical, reolutionary thinkers who take a public stan on the issue o social justice. This notion o proessors an teachers assuming a political neutrality is a alsehoo because i you on't speak out, that's take a political position itsel.

# Q. Critical pedagogy is meant to instill change, but from where does that change come?

. I take a ialectical approach. It's not simply reolution or reorm; it's both. Dialectics is about meiation; meiation is not about juxtaposition. It's not "either or"; it's "both an."

There's an abstract utopia an a concrete utopia. Abstract utopias are when someboy has some iea in their min o what a perect worl woul look like, an it's sometimes ery isconnecte rom what's actually happening on the groun at the time. Concrete utopias are relate to the problems people are acing in the here an now. You o what you can within the system an hae a larger, broaer ision o where you want to go. Change begins with a critical consciousness but also with a moral an ethical commitment. Join a church group, a community group, a group connecte with public libraries, the uniersity or a high school. Get inole; make a commitment to people an change.

Critical consciousness isn't a preconition; it's an outcome o that engagement. Otherwise, you coul always say you're not reay. There's always another book or philosopher to rea, an it elays you rom taking action. Begin with the action, an use the theory to reine your thoughts about that engagement.

# Q. In your latest book, you look critically at capitalism through an interesting lens—the teachings of Jesus. How do you connect these two?

. In examining the gospels an statements by Jesus, it became ery clear to me that, while Jesus was not against absolute wealth, he was against relatie wealth or what we call "ierentiating wealth." That is to say, you cannot be rich while someone else is poor. It became ery clear through his sayings, or what hae been attribute to him, it's irreutable that a rich person cannot get into heaen. The kingom o Go is really a kingom in which ierentiating wealth oes not exist.

We coul make an argument that Jesus was teaching communism. Not the communism that came as a specter, the totalitarian ictatorships we saw with Stalin—I woul not want to een them an woul neer call those regimes "communist." I mean the notion o sharing resources. The early Christian communities came together to share what they ha. "It's easier or a camel to pass through the eye o a neele than or a rich man to get into the kingom o Go." Those statements are not about greey people; they're statements about society. Toay, how we organize our means o prouction oeretermines the opportunities that some people hae oer others.

Capitalism structurally instantiates inequality. Jesus equate loe with the struggle or equality. Loe an justice are the two main messages o Jesus. I you rea the scriptures, Jesus preache a socialist gospel. To see socialism among right-wing eangelical Christians as a kin o eil, it's absolutely absur. That's largely a position I think stems out o ignorance about what socialism is an what communism was in the writings o Marx.

I'm trying to begin a ebate because I eel that ebate is ery important. I'm beginning to inorm critical peagogy's ethics o social justice by rawing on the teachings o Jesus.

# Q. Historically, many thinkers with dreams of changing the world become disillusioned as they grow older. You're now approaching your seventies, and your passion is burning brighter than ever. What keeps you going?

. I think it's a certain sense o being blesse. My lie's been an uphill struggle. Being on this path isn't easy. I on't know i you'e rea on the Internet about the "Dirty Thirty," but I was hit by the Right in 2006. I was labele by a right-wing group with a lot o money behin it as one o the most angerous proessors at UCLA. This group was oering stuents \$100 to secretly auiotape my classes an \$50 or notes. The only group that stoo up an eene me at the time was the Chaistas in Venezuela. Taking the social justice route can make you a target or right-wing critics who eel you're against the unamental principles o the country.

It's always been a struggle, but I'e elt I'e been blesse in my lie with the opportunity to meet people rom all oer the worl. I'e oun a loe an a sense o soliarity, a sense o reciprocal relationship base on trust an a share commitment to make the worl a better place. The groups I'e met an the suering I'e seen hae humble me because it makes my suering an personal struggles pale in comparison. I'e met some pretty courageous people that hae inspire me, that hae a commitment an energy that ar excees my own. Meeting my wie, Angie, has been a blessing, an so has haing an opportunity to be at an institution like Chapman, working with stuents an being in a position to learn.

I'm not optimistic about the uture, but I'm hopeul. I on't mean hope in the acile sense; I mean hope as a struggle—as a orm o taking on yoursel the buren o history an knowing that buren is being share by others.

Knowing you're not alone and that you're driven by a love of humanity. You can't be a revolutionary without loving people. I'm a people person, I guess. Some people call me naïve, but I haven't given up on humanity yet.
