# **5.3** Neoliberal Globalization and Resistance in Education

## The Challenge of Revolutionary Critical Pedagogy

## Constantine Skordoulis

In this chapter, I describe the effects of neoliberal globalization on education and the response of revolutionary critical pedagogy. In my analysis, the terms *globalization* and *neoliberalism* are interconnected and considered as pivotal in a course of commodification of education—namely, the subordination of education to the rules of the capitalist market.

In my view, the term *globalization* is nothing more than an ideological construction that is an attempt to give a neutral description of a fundamental feature of capitalism that has already been described by E. Mandel in his *Late Capitalism*1—that is, the internationalization of capital and of the exploitative relations under the capitalism of multinationals.

Globalization is accompanied by neoliberal policy, in effect creating conditions of class war, aiming to increase the profitability of capital at the expense of the oppressed classes dismantling labor relations, halting and reversing the gains of the working class worldwide.

Neoliberal policy is accompanied by an unprecedented ideological offensive at the level of theory. Typical of this is the use of seemingly neutral terms that indicate deep reactionary meanings. The aim is the imposition of the ideological hegemony of bourgeois policy and the theoretical disarmament of the exploited classes but most importantly is the development of a consensus system inclusive of the agents of social resistance.

I believe it is extremely important to highlight and thoroughly analyze the neoliberal policy enforcement mechanism in education in national educational systems. This role has been undertaken by the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) through the Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA). An important part of this ar-

ticle iscusses how PISA homogenizes the eucation systems o the OECD country members.

I consier it important that in aition to the analysis, we shoul also outline an alternatie proposal, not only in terms o an oerall alternatie economic policy to neoliberalism but particularly at the leel o eucation. Toay, the only coherent proposal against the eects o globalization on eucation comes rom the proponents o reolutionary critical peagogy.

In this chapter, I outline the prospects o resistance that the anticapitalist/ socialist teachers' moement inspire by the theoretical accomplishments o reolutionary critical eucation.

### NElb El pl Cy N EduCN

The main objectie o neoliberal policy in eucation is the transormation o eucation to another serices-proiing sector, ultimately a source or increasing the proitability o capital.

The attempte restructuring o labor relations, o the health an eucation sectors that we witness worlwie, aims to estroy the pillars o the welare state as expresse by the right to work, eucation, an health an to replace it with a state that is nothing more than a regulator or the proitability o capital at the expense o the weaker social classes.

Against the neoliberal an neoconseratie restructuring o eucation, we must propose a ierent policy, to set up a ramework o positions aiming to reerse the neoliberal armageon in eucation. In this irection the answer to the question o the role o eucation uner capitalism has always been o paramount importance.

The theoretical legacy o classical Marxism guies our position that society shapes eucation in accorance to the nees o the social classes that hol power. From the stanpoint o the ruling class, the main role o eucation is to reprouce the ominant ieology. Here is part o the iew o eucation as ieological apparatus o the state (ISA). The reprouction o the ominant ieology inoles the istortion o reality, preenting the eelopment o critical knowlege by stuents an the critical unerstaning o the reality o the natural an social worls aroun them.

But another reprouction process takes place simultaneously. The "eucation inustry" is a signiicant state apparatus in the reprouction an replication o the capitalist social orm necessary or the continuation o "surplus alue" extraction an economic inequality, so a process o repro uction o the existing exploitatie social relations through the enhancement o the contraiction between manual an intellectual labor takes place.

Nowadays neoliberalism has entered a new stage. The educational policy of neoliberalism aims to proletarianize intellectual labor by creating conditions by which education will no longer be a springboard for social advancement but a passport for the creation of an army of flexible employees. This is achieved through the following:

- 1. the social devaluation of the teaching profession
- the intervention in the curriculum such that skills come into the foreground and scientific knowledge (understanding by the methods of science) is secondary

In accordance with the argument developed here, G. Rikowski has suggested that the capitalist state needs to control the social production of labor power for two reasons:

- 1. to try to ensure that the social production of labor power—equipping students with skills and competences—occurs
- 2. to try to ensure that modes of pedagogy that are antithetical to labor power production do not and cannot exist. The capitalist state will not tolerate any forms of pedagogy that attempt to educate students regarding their real predicament—to create an awareness of themselves as future labor powers and to underpin this awareness with critical insight that seeks to undermine the smooth running of the social production of labor power4

This policy, despite neoliberal manifestations for "freedom of choice," entails a strict control of the curriculum for teacher education, of schooling, and of educational research.

My longtime friend and comrade Dave Hill has argued that neoliberal policy in education compresses and represses critical space in education, seeking to neutralize and destroy potential pockets of resistance to global corporate expansion and neoliberal capital questioning the role of the school in maintaining and deepening social stratification, which is what neoliberalism pursues.5

### Knowledge Society

Reading the various official documents of the World Bank, the OECD, and the European Union (EU), we see that they start with the premise that we live in a "knowledge society." This premise is in fact the preamble to their education policy proposals.

What actually is the "knowlege society"? In essence it is the integration o knowlege in the prouction process as a irect prouctie orce, as a key actor in the proitability o capital. This new prouction ormat aims to recoer the permanent an structural crisis o the capitalist system, which tries to oercome the crisis through continuous technological reolutions, using knowlege as a irect prouctie orce.

As mentione beore, the key eature o late capitalism is the proletarianization o intellectual labor. This means that the intellectual worker, such as a teacher, in the new context, enters the same leel o exploitation as the manual worker. The egree o exploitation o intellectual-mental labor is at the same leel with manual labor. In this phase o capitalism, the egree o exploitation o eucational work is the same as the egree o exploitation o the worker in the actory. This is an essential eature o neoliberal capitalism an the way it is manieste in eucation.

### Skills, Not Knowledge

The irst thing someone can see in the plans an programs or the "new school" o neoliberalism is the question o eelopment o stuents' skills. The school curriculum shoul eelop skills such as critical thinking, problem soling, an ecision making. This separation between knowlege an skills an the primacy o skills ersus knowlege was characteristic o the eucational reorm implemente by Margaret Thatcher in Englan in 1979. The irst thing Thatcher i when her goernment esigne the new curricula was to publish an eucational maniesto title "Skills, Not Knowlege." This slogan constitutes the heart o the neoliberal attack on eucation, an the transormation o the curriculum is centere precisely at this point: the egraation o knowlege an the orientation towar skills in iew o eucating an ajustable lexible worker who will not hae a permanent job an be prepare to change his or her job ie to ten times uring his or her working lie. I so, it oes not make sense to hae concrete, soli knowlege, but the worker shoul eelop speciic skills, so as to be aaptable to changing working enironment uring his or her lie. This package is also associate with lielong learning (LLL). Lielong learning means that eucation nees to gie knowlege that constitutes not the ientity o the knowing subject but speciic skills instea, planning a lexible worker, springy to the working status an lexible in aapting to new working conitions through retraining.

At the same time, the aim is to inuse in the school curriculum an uni ersity eucation terms that may seem neutral but hae a serious ieological loa, to which, unortunately, we hae not gien the importance they esere. That is, there are seemingly neutral terms in eucational policy ocuments such as quality an innovation. In act, terms such as these, when introuce in the schooling process, aim to integrate stuents an teachers in the logic o prouction o "innoatie" ieas een at the curriculum leel. An what begins at the school leel will be later transorme through organize research into new knowlege that will be the locomotie to pull capitalism out o its crisis.

# pS S N NSuEN F E ECd pl Cy N EduCN

A basic prerequisite or the success o neoliberal policy in eucation at the global leel is the existence o a regulatory mechanism that will goern the application o neoliberal eucational policy worlwie. This role has been unertaken by the OECD, an the tool use is PISA, a amous program that began in 2000, is applie eery three years in all OECD countries, an ealuates stuents' skills in language, mathematics, an natural sciences.

The OECD policy ollows two main axes: homogenization o eucational systems an promotion o competition among the participating countries. In the context o this policy, the question ceases to be about eectie eucation, ocusing instea on the position a country achiees in the ranking list o the eucation systems o OECD countries.

The logic o the OECD strategy supports an increase competitieness combine with a proceure or establishing common criteria between the eucational systems o countries, as this woul be proitable or the capitalist market. In essence, the mobility in the labor market that shapes glob alize capitalism requires homogenize qualiications an skills or the workers o all countries. The aim is to stanarize the eucation systems o OECD countries. To achiee this objectie, PISA ollows the same metho an proceure in all countries employing the same criteria without taking into account the national speciicities o eucational systems an the cultural speciicities o each country. This raises a question o justice, not only or the aliity an reliability o the results but also or the stuents teste. How air is it or stuents to hae to eal with issues o speciic orm an content that may neer hae been taught?

The questions aske in the PISA test are not base on the objecties an content o the curricula o the participating countries, because the OECD oes not inten to ealuate the eucation system o each country. "It uses eucational ealuation as a technical homogenization o eucational systems an curricula through competition."

Countries participating in the program must align with PISA requirements, because their aim is to achiee the best position in the ranking lists o eucation systems the OECD publishes. Within this ramework, eucation systems an national curricula are graually homogenize. These proceures o not stimulate iscussion on improing eucational systems in each country, since the interests o national eucational policies are monopolize by the nee to improe their image in the OECD list.

PISA exerts great inluence on public opinion. Wheneer the results o the program are announce, goernments aopt an apologetic tone, newspapers in material to ill multiple columns, an teachers are puzzle an seek explanations or what is happening to their country's eucational system.

A typical example o the inluence o the PISA is the U. S. case. The program No Chil Let Behin echoe the irst results o PISA. Among other things, primary an seconary schools were put uner a permanent ealuation process to ecie on their inancing. "The orces that etermine the latest eucational policies o the action 'No Chil Let Behin' is neoliberal economic policies that push schools to perorm like multinationals."

Fortunately, PISA has raise serious criticism an objections about its nature an its reliability, also among nonraical eucators, or a number o reasons:

- The topics chosen o not ealuate the crucial aspects o school an eucation but the speciic aspects o neoliberal eucational policy that it seeks to impose on the countries inole.
- PISA is conucte by priate companies seeking a share in the growing market o tests an sureys.
- The interpretation o the PISA results are within a neoliberal conception o eucation an thus are necessarily partisan.

The logic o the OECD strategy supports competition combine with a system o common neoliberal stanars or eucational systems o the participating countries in the interest o the global capitalist market.

The OECD eucation policy makers an the goernment oicials o ierent countries show a great interest in the results o PISA an the comparatie inicators publishe because they beliee that there is a irect correlation between the eucational achieements o a country an its economic eelopment. The OECD publication Education at a Glance states that "stu ents who emonstrate high leels o perormance are more likely to become prouctie workers when they leae the eucation system." Still, "In a worl increasingly ominate by technology, knowlege an skills o stuents are central to the ability to compete in a global market."

Howeer, the existence o a relationship between a country's perormance in eucation an economic growth in capitalist terms is not at all obious. In arious stuies publishe to correlate statistically the results o stuent perormance in international competitions like the Trens in International Mathematics an Science Stuy (TIMSS) tests with the economic eelopment inicators o the country, research inings o not support this.

K. Robinson compare the results o the countries in the TIMSS tests with macroeconomic ariables such as per capita gross national prouct. He oun an extremely weak relationship between them, which means that school perormance is not irectly relecte in the economy. G. S. Drori takes a similar position using the inings o the TIMSS tests to question the relationship between eucation an economic eelopment. Drori suggests that it is important to take into account intermeiate actors such as the nature o the national eucation system, eucational inrastructure, ierent stuents' attitues towar eucation, an ierences in motiation o learning. An M. Uljens states explicitly that "the PISA-ealuations operate in orer to promote the neoliberal interests o OECD. This is consiere important as it appears oten to be orgotten that OECD is the organization behin an running the PISA project." Inee, in the neoliberal moel, politics, economy, an eucation are consiere mutually epenent. "This has create a issonance in the 'school-state-market triangle' (eucation, politics, economics) which is clearest isible in an through the contemporary iscussions on the crisis o citizenship an citizenship eucation."

Een nonraical eucation theorists beliee that PISA ocuses on uni ariate comparatie lists sering political rather than peagogical interests. Critics hae argue that "a scientiic approach woul contain peagogical interpretation o how an why some countries lack certain skills an some other countries outweigh."

The ranking lists o the countries in the arious iels are easily rea able by the general public, but nonexperts cannot interpret an properly use the ata. Because PISA ocuses on creating ranking lists o the participating countries an oes not explain the ierences between them, the interpretations are gien by the countries, their ministries, an their mass meia.

The classiication o countries into lists nurtures competitieness an a ors the growing homogenization through a sel-correcting process. So there is a policy ramework within which we hae to answer, an our answer has to be in terms o social class.

### E p Edggy F ESSNCE

The classic book that highlights the position o Marxist critical eucators on globalization in eucation is Peter Mc Laren an Ramin Farahmanpur's Teaching against Global Capitalism and the New Imperialism: A Critical Pedagogy.17 The authors' main goal is to strongly criticize economic globalization, especially its impact: global capitalism, postmodernity, cultural hybridization, and the "new imperialism." This critique is grounded in Marxist theory and as an alternative form of education offers what is called *revolutionary* critical pedagogy.

For Mc Laren and Farahmandpur, globalization is a misleading and euphemistic term that conceals the "ugly" face: imperialism and mainly cultural imperialism, the project of Western societies' unilateral supremacy and world domination, exploitation of labor, support for state terrorism, the militarization of space, the homogenization of media, and finally a capitalism that converts the natural environment into shopping center chains, aiming at short-term profits at the expense of ecological health and human dignity.

For the authors, postmodernist theories tacitly adopt the market economy, with a focus on cultural discourse and "hypersubjectivity," eventually falling victim to identity politics that characterize a "rough form of multiculturalism" and collapse in a liberal form.18

Specifically, they assert their position that in any form of education, "the Marxist analysis should serve as an axiomatic tool for contesting current social relations linked to the globalization of capital and the neoliberal educational policies that follow in its wake." However, they admit that "the Marxist theory is a social analysis system on human subjects and runs the little things of everyday life, so it should constantly be reviewed."20

To achieve education that resists this new imperialism, for Mc Laren and Farahmandpur the priority is a Freireian, socialist pedagogy of the working class, which is also referred to as revolutionary multiculturalism, revolutionary practice active citizenship, or revolutionary critical pedagogy. We need revolutionary critical pedagogy, they say, because what we now call critical pedagogy has to do more than to critique the daily commodity logic of capital. It has to build a new vision of society, freed from the law of value.

To promote a revolutionary act, critical pedagogy must be able to support the cultural struggle of the workers and to coordinate such struggles as part of a cross-border association of social movements, which in turn organize and support the working classes and the marginalized cultural workers in their efforts to conduct new international anticapitalist struggles on the path to socialism.21 Mc Laren and Farahmandpur summarize these pedagogical positions with the observation that "we need nothing less than a social revolution."22

There are, of course, Marxists who partly disagree with these positions.23 They say it is not clear, within the theoretical framework of revolutionary critical pedagogy, how it addresses the issue of diversity exemplified as racial or class or gender or sexuality discrimination, among others.

Eentually, howeer, Marxist eucators seem to agree that a global anticapitalist eucation is possible, but to achiee it, we nee to ormulate a new, completely ierent raical conception o what is boundary, what is educational, an what is globalization. Such an unerstaning must be groune on Marxism an the class struggle an must always aim at social reolution.

For Marxist eucators, the axes or a reolutionary policy in eucation in the context o globalization are structure as ollows: critique o the existing system, alternatie eucational objecties, an training or changing the worl.

Raical eucators argue that we hae to put the classroom an the school at the center o a complex network o learning an help to create a new popular culture on eucation. This means that stuents shoul be proie with alternatie interpretations o how an why things happen as they happen.

In the ialectical iew o eucation, critical thinking is key, but critical thinking nees imagination, through which stuents an practicing teachers enision a new social reality. Raical eucators ealing with how to acquire critical knowlege an unerstaning ocus on eucation or all citizens, as members o a airer society. Uner this concept, there is no istinction between the eucation o chilren an that o aults (as oppose to the concept o chilhoo in bourgeois peagogues); instea, a more long-term training strategy is require, a new eucation in general, characterize by some major policy issues.

The main objecties o such a training strategy woul be proiing public eucation beore an uring work-lie sering all citizens throughout their lies, promoting a emocratically controlle an accountable eucation at all leels, an applying principles o equality an noniscrimination in all segments o its operation. Also, any iscrimination in proiing eucation or training to any group o people in terms o age, race, gener, social class, sexual orientation, or physical an mental ability or isability shoul be eclare illegal.

We consier that the ollowing proposal summarizes a Marxist-oriente uniersal eucation taking up successully both the issues o sustainability as well as that o social justice:

Eery person an eery social group will be experiencing eucation as something that contributes to his/her own progress, but at the same time, eucation shoul ensure that at least a part o the actiities o lie are planne to ensure the uture o the planet we inherite. . . . [D]emocracy is possible i there is a ree public ebate relate to the options aailable on how to manage our social an economic system. . . . All societies are struggling or the same issues. Toay we can eliminate poerty . . . oerall. We can approach the uture inesting in a massie campaign encouraging eeryone to participate in eery stage o his/her lie in eucation an training. An eucation that increases the prouctie, social, cultural an enironmental eelopment in ways that we hae not yet begun to enision een."

### CNCluSN

I think, i we are to ormulate a concrete proposal, we shoul not well only on the theoretical achieements o reolutionary critical peagogy as it eole into the secon ecae o 2000. We shoul be more speciic an examine the proposals mae so ar in terms o the content o the curricula by the so-calle societies in transition.

I reer here to speciic examples. A ew years ago, Venezuela implemente eucational reorm uner Cesar Cháez. For this issue, there was intense ebate in the circles o the Latin American Let, because it became clear that the reorm in Venezuela starte rom the top own, unlike the Zapatista moel, which was launche in Chiapas sering irectly the nees o the community. This is a big ebate that has been an continues to be important as to what we want an how we want to change eucation.

O course, the international experience in this kin o experiment is extremely rich—or example, the experiments o the Soiet Polytechnic School an Ault an Workers Eucation in Re Vienna, in the perio 1919–1934, which hae not receie the research attention they esere, an a number o other eorts, such as the campaign or literacy in Nicaragua, which many o us watche too closely.

In general, I think that at the leel o eeloping an analysis o the ominant neoliberal project in eucation, our contributions are soli, but at the leel o eeloping an alternatie curriculum, a teaching metho in the classroom, we hae to go a ew steps urther. In the current conitions o global, unregulate market ominance, this requires a eepening o our Marxist approach in close connection with the social moements o resistance.

# 5.4 Transformative Education, Critical Education, Marxist Education

Possibilities and Alternatives to the Restructuring of Education in Global Neoliberal Times

## DAVE HILL

Teachers, both university/higher education teachers and schoolteachers, I are among the most highly organized and unionized groups of workers, and sometimes the most militant. I have written elsewhere of the various arenas in which Marxist and critical educators can be, are, and should be active—within the limits of individual capabilities and strengths, of course. These arenas are (1) within the classroom; (2) within the wider school community/organization, such as the staffroom and the union branch; (3) within the local community/town/city—for example, in tenants' or benefits' or antiracist or antiausterity or other local community organizations and movements—and within town- or citywide political parties, social movements, and trade unions; and (4) at national levels within such movements, parties, and organizations.

I point to these arenas for transformative political social and educational activism, since education—whether transformative or reproductive, whether revolutionary, reformist, conservative, or reactionary—takes place without/ outside formal schooling and education systems as well as within.

### RESISTANCE, CRITICAL AND MARXIST EDUCATION, CRITICAL AND MARXIST EDUCATORS

Critique, dissent, and transformation are not easy. Critical Marxist educators engage in critique that frames educational experiences within the conditions of capitalism and its current neoliberal and neoconservative form. And, I would add, they and we should also do so, recognizing its increasingly repressive neoconservative form, whether couched in terms of religion (Hindutva, fundamentalist Christianity, Islam, Buddhism, or mainstream Christianity), fascist/Nazi thuggery, or increased repressive tactics and weaponry used by the police. The political context in different states from India to Turkey, from the United States to the United Kingdom, from Ukraine to Greece, shows the different faces of and interrelationships between calls to religion, armed racist/neo-Nazi thugs and murderers, and chemically treated water cannons used by the police. And, as ideological state apparatuses, schools and universities have a repressive function with repressive surveillance, punishment, and new public managerialist mechanisms an measures to issuae, an punish, "eep issent" an "eep critique."

Howeer, either quietly or openly, in schools, colleges, an uniersities, many raical an Marxist critical eucators try to aect our aspects o learning an teaching, asking questions about (at least) our aspects. These questions are common to many types o raical eucators, not simply Marxists. In the ollowing sections I iscuss what is specifically Marxist about these our questions.

### p Edggy

Some critical eucators question the teacher-centere pedagogy, the pattern o teaching an learning relationships an interaction, what Paulo Freire terme the banking model o eucation. Instea, using Freirean perspecties an praxis, they try to use emocratic participatie peagogy that can break own, to some extent, patterns o omination an submission an listens to chilren's, stuents', an local communities' oices. This is no uncritical, postmoernist, or liberal, uncritical acceptance o polyocality. As Peter Mc Laren in his postmoern phase put it, attempting to eelop a resistance or Marxist postmoernism accepting metanarraties o class, capital, or the labor-capital relation (an attempt that he subsequently recognize as unattainable) "always totalizes."

Critical Marxist eucators also attempt to use ierent types o peagogy in teaching to engage in nonhierarchical, emocratic, participatie teaching an research. Such approaches are roote in social constructiist Vygotskyan unerstanings o learning an are also aime both at proucing co-learning, by teachers as well as stuents, an at oertly welcoming an aluing more cultures than are commonly alue in a transmission moe o teaching. Le Vygotsky, as a Marxist, was inspire by Marx's ialectic in that it rejects top own an bottom-up accounts o the learning process; these uniirectional moels originate in class-base societal relations, which Marxists reject.

O course, critiques o oerominant teacher-centere peagogy are not restricte to Marxist eucators. They are also mae by liberal-progressie, chil/stuent-centere eucators; anarchist eucators; an some conseratie eucators concerne about teaching eectieness an preparation or the workplace. An, ollowing Antonio Gramsci, Marxist teachers, by irtue o their role in teaching, shoul maintain an authoritatie stance where appropriate.

But critical eucation is about ar more than peagogy. Inee, it takes place outsie schools an uniersities as well as insie, as the rise o alternatives to the English university indicates. There is educational resistance not only within but also outside the state-controlled education structures. Marxist teachers, cultural workers, and intellectuals, are active within teach-ins at the Occupy occupations, their tent cities, the free university movement, and through oppositional media and cultural workers, as well as within trade union and student groups and political parties.

### **CURRICULUM**

A second question Marxist and other critical educators can and should ask is about the *curriculum*—who selected the content and how rigid is it? Even where the curriculum is very tightly controlled, even where it is very rigidly prescribed, there are, as Gramsci taught us, always little spaces for us to infiltrate, to use, to colonize. These spaces—sometimes broad, sometimes narrow—exist in schools, prisons, youth clubs, universities, and vocational colleges and in tent cities, teach-ins and teach-outs, emergent alternatives, and various social media and even, occasionally, in official media.

Marxist educators, indeed critical educators in general, can, with students, look at the curriculum and ask, "Who do you think wrote this?" "Who do you think decided on including this in the curriculum?" "What do you/ we think should be in the curriculum that is currently absent?" "Why do you think it is absent?" "Who do you think benefits and who loses from this curriculum?" "What is the ideology behind this book/task/lesson/curriculum piece?" These questions can be asked with ten-year-olds, sixteen-year-olds, forty-year-olds, or seventy-year-olds.

However limited the spaces are within a school, university, or educational site or within a curriculum, we can always find ways to question and encourage students to do this as well so that they are, in effect, developing an awareness of what can be called ideology critique.3 And then we can suggest and seek from students, an alternative, perhaps even if only for five minutes in a lesson or session. We can question existing versions of history. We can ask, "Is there a different version or view of the past, the present, or the future?" So, looking at the work of Marxist and communist teachers and critical educators, we can affect the content of curriculum, or, if that is impossible, we can seek to develop ideology critique, an understanding of the capital-labor relation, of capitalism and its relationship to education systems, of ideological and repressive state apparatuses, and of how schools and universities are shaped and controlled to produce politically and ideologically quiescent and hierarchically organized and rewarded labor power. Where Marxist educators and revolutionary critical educators4 differ from more social democratic and liberal critical eucators is in the emphasis place on resistance an socialist transormation. Social emocratic an liberal eucators rarely suggest, teach about, or proclaim the nee or an anticapitalist reolution, the nee to replace capitalist economic an social relations with socialist ones.

### gNN F SudENS

A thir question in eucation that critical an Marxist eucators can an shoul ask is about organization of the students. How shoul chilren o i erent social classes, geners, ethnic backgrouns, an sexual orientations be organize within classrooms, schools an uniersities, an national eucation systems? Are some groups, such as girls, ethnic minorities, the working class, or the poorer sections o the working class, systematically labele, segregate, iie, emeane?

In some countries, irtually all chilren go to the same type o school. But chilren ten to go to schools where their own class preominates. An this can be a problem. Some socialist municipalities ("local eucation authorities") in Englan in the 1960s an 1970s, such as Sheiel an the ILEA (Inner Lonon Eucation Authority, where I taught in a comprehensie seconary [high] school in the late 1960s), use measures such as "zoning" (rawing catchment areas or schools that eliberately inclue poor, aerage-income, an high-income urban areas) or taking a set proportion o stuents rom what were calle "ability" bans (in reality, "attainment" bans). But there is a clash o principles here, with no set Marxist solution—the clash between the eliberate mixing o attainment/ability bans or groups o stuents an a eliberate mixing o ierent social strata on the one han, an a ierent principle o neighborhoo schooling on the other.

In consiering how stuents shoul be groupe between schools an within them, there is also a question o how the eucation system inculcates a ierentiate sense o class awareness in working-, mile-, an ruling-class stuents. An it tries to keep the working class as a working class that is obeient, subserient, iniiualistic, an intereste in only themseles, not in collectiity or community. Marxist an other egalitarian eucators clearly preer an work or what in Britain is calle "comprehensie" schools, an in Inia, or example, is calle "the common school." But then, een where this happens (as in Finlan, where there are only a hanul o priate schools, where stuents up to age sixteen are taught in common/comprehensie schools in "mixe ability" classes), there are internal inormal mechanisms, the hien curriculum o ierential (race, genere, an sexually oriente) expectations an responses to ierent cultural capitals.

A ourth question that Marxist an other critical eucators ask is about ownership and control of schools (an, inee, ocational colleges an uniersities). Who shoul own, control, an goern schools an urther eucation at ocational colleges an uniersities? O course we cannot change the law at a stroke, but we can lea a moement so that at some stage—in two years, ten years, twenty years—the ownership an goernance o schools can be change, mae emocratic, secular, an egalitarian, instea o, as in some countries, being owne an goerne by a religious state, transnational corporations, religious organizations themseles, or-proit priate companies, companies that are theoretically not-or-proit but rewar hansomely their executies an their riens, or rich businessmen or women.

Marxist eucators (an others, o course) beliee that schools, colleges, an uniersities shoul be run emocratically, with eucators, stuents, an electe representaties o local communities haing powers in an oer those institutions, within a secular, emocratic national ramework. Explicit in this is the assertion that eucation is a public goo an a public right that shoul not be istorte an corrupte by priate ownership—there shoul be no priate schools, colleges, or uniersities.

A question relate to who shoul own an control schools is that o how shoul they be manage—to put it cruely, shoul the management style be emocratic, participatie, an collegiate, or shoul it be authoritarian, top-own control? The global moe to priatize state eucation (which has gone a long way in the Unite States an Englan) is a orm o control an management o the workorce—o teachers, lecturers, school support, an aministratie sta—known as new public managerialism—importing the huge ierentials o pay, perks, an power typical o the priate sector into the public sector, into eucation an other public serices such as health an welare an social serices.

### xS EduCS

What is speciically Marxist about these our questions is that while Marxists work or an willingly embrace reorms such as those that are implicit in the preceing criticisms, we are committe to three orms o analysis an action that social emocrats, raical liberals, raical emocrats, non-Marxist eminists, non-Marxist antiracists, an non-Marxist queer actiists are not.

What eines Marxists is first our belie that reorms are not sustainable uner capitalism an that thereore what is neee is a reolution to replace the capitalist economic system with its capitalist economic relations o pro uction an its capitalist social relations o prouction—the ownership by capitalists o the wealth an the power in society. Reolutions can be iolent (ruling classes o not oten gie up their power peaceully), or they might be through the ballot box or a combination o the two. The ballot box alone cannot bring about reolution because state institutions in capitalism are not emocratic. A Congress or parliament or presient or prime minister has limite power oer these institutions. An electe socialist goernment woul not be able to bring about much change that went against the interests o the capitalist class because the military, juiciary, police, an corporate hierarchy are not emocratic. They use state iolence to stop it. This is the critique, or example, mae by the Greek reolutionary Marxist coalition Antarsya o the raical Let Syriza coalition, which can be characterize as let social emocratic, which is, by current projections (summer 2014), likely to be electe into goernment. But or reolutionary Marxists, a socialist reolution is necessary, so that there comes into power (not just into government) an egalitarian, socialist economic, political, an eucation system.

The second ierence between Marxist an non-Marxist raicals is that in orer to replace capitalism, Marxists hae to organize. Thus a Marxist's uty is to be an actiist, within the limits o his or her ability an competing emans, an to recognize that political organization, program eelopment, an political interention are necessary. Reolutions o not all o trees, like apples. They hae to be ought or an eene.

The third point is the salience of class as compare with other orms o structural oppression an iscrimination an inequality. Marxists, Marxist eminists, Marxist antiracists, an Marxist queer theorists stan together with social moements an ciil rights campaigners in opposing racism, sexism, homophobia, an other orms o iscrimination. But Marxists go urther than criticizing (an acting against) social iscrimination an oppression into economic rights—an urther than that, into the recognition that ull economic rights cannot be achiee uner a capitalist economic system but only uner a socialist or communist system.

Furthermore, Marxists unerstan that only the organize working class (black-white, male-emale, straight-LGBT, Dalit an all other castes) can organize to replace the capitalist system. These are the points o ierence between Marxists an other raical liberals an letists.

### SClS NFES F EduCN

In this section I itemize twenty-one policy aspects an proposals roote in the analysis I hae set out. Many o these are supporte by a ariety o social emocratic, liberal, an social justice ieologies, teachers, an citizens. But together they oer a sustaine challenge to neoliberal capitalism. The extent to which they may oer a unamental challenge to capitalism itsel as oppose to the current neoliberal-neoconseratie phase o capitalism (what Trotsky terme a "transitional program") is a matter or ebate.

But irst, I wish to acknowlege that to propose eucational theories, we must always recognize the restrictions place on us uring that process. As Stephen Toulmin puts it, "Until the basic empirical or experimental acts are establishe, we are not in a position to eelop theoretical explanations. Till then the pursuit o theoretical generalizations is premature." It is ruitul, ollowing such analysis, to engage at a normatie leel o what shoul happen in schools an in an eucation system. This is not inepenent o what oes happen. The normatie section that ollows has been precee by a actual analysis o the history, structure, content, unctioning, an eatures o compulsory state schooling an eucation systems.

Here I present a program or maniesto or eucation, or iscussion.

- 1. Cut class sizes to a maximum of twenty students in both primary and secondary schools. In Englan, or example, many classes in primary (elementary) schools hae more than thirty pupils—some o the largest in the rich worl much larger than in priate schools, or example. Accoring to OECD research, Englan is twenty-thir out o thirty eelope countries in terms o large class size. Other countries such as Finlan hae a maximum class size o twenty. Finlan is wiely seen as proiing an extremely high quality o eucation. In countries with a lower GDP, o course, class sizes o ity to a hunre are not uncommon.
- 2. Abolish league tables and most externally set assessment tasks. Some external testing is necessary, but the types o regular tests o actual knowlege typical o the Unite States an Englan (terme "high-stakes testing") oten restrict teaching to "teaching to the test," relegates nonactual learning to the sielines, an results in unue stress.
- 3. Restore local democratic control of state schools that have been handed over to private corporations, charities, and individuals to run, and establish local democratic control of such schools. In Englan an Wales such schools are known as academies; in the as the Unite States they are calle charter schools. In a ition, Englan has state-une free schools run by speciic groups, oten sectional groups o parents. Schools shoul be run by the emocratically electe local councils/municipalities an operate uner national pay an conitions agreements or those who work within the institutions. Rich businesspeople, religious organizations, or "eucorporations"/priate companies running a chain or bran o school shoul not be hane control o state-une schools such as acaemies or charter schools. Also, such prepriatizing pet projects o goernments shoul not be more aantageously une than the rest o the state schools. The ae inestment that goernments put in to prime their aore experiments shoul be kept an enhance, but it shoul be istribute between all schools. Our schools an the chilren in them are not or sale! Schools shoul not, through uneen uning or ierent types o school (e.g., acaemies), be set up or success at the expense o others being set up (an unerune) or relatie ailure.

- 4. Form a fully comprehensive secondary school system (known in India as a common school) so that each school has a broa mix o social class, abilities, an attainment leels.
- 5. Get private profiteering out of our schools. Eucation serices that hae been priatize an hie o an ha their contracts gien to priate proiteers shoul be brought back into public control. These inclue Englan's Oice or Stanars in Eucation, Chilren's Serices an Skills (Oste), the boy that runs stuent loans, school meals, cleaning, an caretaking. This is so that proper pay an conitions can be restore to workers whose jobs hae been contracte out, an so that school an uniersity workers who work uner contract can eel that they are part o the school, college, or uni ersity community. Stuents an school workers who are protesting urther priatization shoul be supporte.
- 6. Integrate existing private schools into the state education system so that the beneits o the priate school system are share among all stuents. All schools shoul be place uner emocratic, locally electe, local council control. Priate schools shoul be prohibite. Religious groups o any religion shoul not be allowe to control an run schools. Say no to big business/ priate capital running our schools an chilren.
- 7. "Faith schools" and organized religion should be removed from schools. I Christians, Jews, Muslims, Hinus, Zoroastrians, Buhists, or members o any religion wish to teach religion, they shoul o it on their own time, at their own places o worship (Saturay/Sunay schools), or in their own supplementary or complementary schools. Ethics, spirituality, an worl religions can be taught, but there shoul be no inoctrination or brainwashing. A critical approach shoul be taken towar religion, recognizing its social an personal unctions but also its political unctions.

- 8. Ensure a good, local school for every child. Surplus space means lower class sizes an increase community use o school acilities.
- 9. Make available free, nutritious, balanced school meals for every child to combat poor iets, obesity, an, or some chilren, actual hunger.
- 10. Restore free adult education classes in pastime an leisure stuies as well as in ocational training/stuies.
- 11. Restore or establish free, state-funded residential centers and youth centers/youth clubs or schoolchilren so they can wien their experiences o lie in sae circumstances an enhance their eucation beyon the conines o the home or city.
- 12. Free up curricula that are overprescriptive so there can be more creati ity an cross-subject/isciplinary work.
- 13. Revise inspectorial and surveillance systems such as (in England) Ofsted. The results o school inspection systems such as Oste in Englan are to penalize een the best schools (outstaning in eery aspect other than in SAT attainments) in the poorest areas an to strike ear into teachers. Howeer, some external superision an inspection is necessary in extreme cases—or example, to make sure there are no "ghost schools"—that is, to make sure that schools actually exist—an to keep some oersight o school perormance across a range o criteria, a range wier than attainment in tests.
- 14. Encourage critical thinking across the curriculum. Teach chilren not what to think, but how to think. Teach about Marxist analysis an the classexploitatie nature o capitalism. Such critical thinking shoul inclue how to think critically about the meia an politicians an also about Marxist analysis. Critical analysis shoul be sel-critical.
- 15. Teach in schools ecological literacy and a readiness to act for environmental justice as well as economic and social justice. Encourage chilren to reach or the stars an to work or a society that lets that happen—a airer society with more equal chances, pay packets, an power—an teach them about enironmental an sustainability issues.
- 16. Ensure that schools are antiracist, antisexist, and antihomophobic making sure schools encourage equality an welcome ierent home an group cultures. As part o this, antibullying practices in eery school must be ully implemente to combat bullying o all sorts, incluing racism, sexism, homophobia, an bullying base on isabilities, caste, an socioeconomic class. Antibullying policies shoul also be part o the ormal curriculum.

- 17. Implement an honest sex education curriculum in schools that teaches chilren not just when to say no but also when to say yes. This program shoul ocus on posities, pleasure, an personal worth, not on stigmatizing sex an sexuality.
- 18. Ensure proper recognition of all school workers and no compulsory redundancies. For teachers, secretarial an support sta, teaching assistants, oo serice assistants, an caretaking sta, there shoul be regular emocratic workplace orums in eery school. Regaring jobs, there shoul be no compulsory reunancies—any restructuring shoul be conitional on agreement with the trae unions.
- 19. Set up of school councils that include students to encourage emocratic unerstaning, citizenship, social responsibility, an a welcoming an aluing o stuent oice.
- 20. Broaden teacher education and training to counter the negatie eects o the technicization an etheorizing o teacher training (which was the result in Englan o the 1992/1993 Conseratie reorganization o what was then calle teacher education—subsequently retitle teacher training). Bring back the stuy an awareness o the social an political an psychological contexts o teaching, incluing an unerstaning o an commitment to challenge an oerturn racism, sexism, homophobia, an other orms o iscrimination, such as against working-class pupils.
- 21. Establish a fully funded, publicly owned, and democratic education system from preschool to university. Eucation is a right, not a commoity to be bought an sol. So there shoul be no ees, as in Scaninaia, Cuba, Venezuela, an Boliia, where eucation up to PhD leel is ree. Say no to uniersity or urther eucation/ocational training ees. An bring in a liing grant or stuents rom less well-o backgrouns.

### EduCE, gE, gNE: xS NlySS

We Marxists seek to sere an aance the interests o the working class. We, as teachers, as eucators, are working class, too; we sell our labor power to capitalists an to the apparatuses o the capitalist state, such as schools an uniersities. We hae to consistently an courageously challenge the ominant ieology, the hegemony o the ruling class, the bourgeoisie, the capitalist class. We are in a battle or ominance o our ieas; there are "culture wars" between ierent ways o looking at/interpreting the worl. We hae to contest the currently hegemonic control o ieas by the capitalist state, schools, meia, an their allies in the religions.

But the situation we ace is not just an ieological war: it is also an economic class war, in which the social an economic conitions an well-being o the working class are threatene an unermine by the ruling class an its capitalist state. Dai Blacker goes een urther an argues that contemporary an uture capitalist onslaughts will result in eaths or "superluous" workers an sections o the nonworking inustrial resere army (or example, the thirteen thousan extra eaths o elerly people in the winter months in the Unite Kingom ue to lack o aorable heating). I we sit an o nothing, i their ieas are not conteste, then capitalism will continue to rule, to emean, to iie, to impoerish us an the planet.

At certain times an locations in history, the ierence between the con itions o workers' existence an what the meia an the clergy say becomes so stark that workers' subjectie consciousness changes. This is particularly likely when workers with more aance reolutionary consciousness bring about a wiesprea an more eenly istribute consciousness among the class as a whole.

In some countries, or in schools an uniersities ace by commoiication an managerialism an prepriatization, the gap between the "o icial" ieology that "we are all in this together" an "there is no alternatie" (to austerity) becomes so large that the ruling party, the ruling capitalist class, an capitalism itsel lose legitimacy. An so, as in Greece, Portugal, Spain, Turkey, Brazil, Britain, an Inia, we Marxists are necessary in lea ing changes in class consciousness an organizing or the replacement o capitalism.

### pg

In 1938, in "The Transitional Program," Trotsky aresse the types o programs moing the iscussion beyon the minimum program (minimum acceptable reorms, such as those to protect an improe existing rights an entitlements, such as rights at work an social an political rights) an the maximum program (socialist reolution, with the type o society ultimately enisage by Marx, a socialist noncapitalist/postcapitalist society) that were aance by late-nineteenth an early-twentieth-century social emocrats an by communists o the thir international an articulate a new type o program: the transitional program. Trotsky, with a istinct resonance to toay's struggles, wrote:

The strategic task o the next perio—prereolutionary perio o agitation, propagana an organization—consists in oercoming the contraiction between the maturity o the objectie reolutionary conitions an the immaturity o the proletariat an its anguar the conusion an isappointment o the oler generation, the inexperience o the younger generation. It is necessary to help the masses in the process o the aily struggle to in the brige between present eman an the socialist program o the reolution. This brige shoul inclue a system o transitional emans, stemming rom to ay's conitions an rom toay's consciousness o wie layers o the working class an unalterably leaing to one inal conclusion: the conquest o power by the proletariat.

Classical Social Democracy, unctioning in an epoch o progressie capitalism, iie its program into two parts inepenent o each other: the minimum program which limite itsel to reorms within the ramework o bourgeois society, an the maximum program which promise substitution o socialism or capitalism in the ineinite uture. Between the minimum an the maximum program no brige existe. An inee Social Democracy has no nee o such a brige, since the wor socialism is use only or holiay speechiying. The Comintern has set out to ollow the path o Social Democracy in an epoch o ecaying capitalism: when, in general, there can be no iscussion o systematic social reorms an the raising o the masses' liing stanars; when eery serious eman o the proletariat an een eery serious eman o the petty bourgeoisie ineitably reaches beyon the limits o capitalist property relations an o the bourgeois state.

## Trotsky continue:

Uner the menace o its own isintegration, the proletariat cannot permit the transormation o an increasing section o the workers into chronically unemploye paupers, liing o the slops o a crumbling society. The right to employment is the only serious right let to the worker in a society base upon exploitation. This right toay is let to the worker in a society base upon exploitation. This right toay is being shorn rom him at eery step. Against unemployment, "structural" as well as "conjunctural," the time is ripe to aance along with the slogan o public works, the slogan o a sliing scale o working hours. Trae unions an other mass organizations shoul bin the workers an the unemploye together in the soliarity o mutual responsibility. On this basis all the work on han woul then be i ie among all existing workers in accorance with how the extent o the working week is eine. The aerage wage o eery worker remains the same as it was uner the ol working week. Wages, uner a strictly guarantee minimum, woul ollow the moement o prices. It is impossible to accept any other program or the present catastrophic perio. . . .

The question is not one o a "normal" collision between opposing material interests. The question is one o guaring the proletariat rom ecay, emoralization an ruin. The question is one o lie or eath o the only creatie an progressie class, an by that token o the uture o mankin. I capitalism is incapable o satisying the emans ineitably arising rom the calamities generate by itsel, then let it perish. "Realizability" or "unrealizability" is in the gien instance a question o the relationship o orces, which can be ecie only by the struggle. By means o this struggle, no matter what immeiate practical successes may be, the workers will best come to unerstan the necessity o liquiating capitalist slaery.

### CNCluSN

The "ecay, emoralization an ruin" Trotsky speaks o are or millions o workers—incluing the mile class—an eeryay reality in the current era o capitalism, neoliberal capitalism, or immiseration capitalism. This immiseration is apparent through both rich an poor worls. The organization an characteristics o the resistance to the epreations is a matter or strategic an tactical consierations, relating to the current balance (strength, organization, [is]unity) o class orces in local an national contexts. What is clear, though, is that or Marxist actiists an eucators, it is not enough just to reorm capitalism—welcome though reorms such as the minimum program are—or to actiely campaign or an protect such reorms. Rather, our task is to replace capitalism with emocratic Marxism. As teachers, as eucators, as cultural workers, as actiists, as intellectuals, we hae a role to play. We must play it.
