# 6 Capitalist Culture and Cultural Production

It will take decades to overcome the infectious, paralyzing influence of bourgeois culture upon the proletariat in the old capitalist countries.

-Anton Pannekoek, World Revolution and Communist Tactics

It's true that it's within the realm of cultural politics that young people tend to work through political issues, which I think is good, although it's not going to solve the problems.

-Angela Y. Davis, PBS interview, 1997

# 6.1 The Revolution of Everyday Life

## RAOUL VANEIGEM

The distance between those who possess the most and those who possess a small (if ever-increasing) amount has not shrunk; but the intermediate levels have multiplied and so to speak brought the two extremes, rulers and ruled, closer to the same mediocrity. To be rich nowadays means to possess a large number of impoverished objects.

Consumer goods tend to lose all use-value. Their nature is to be consumable at all costs, as witness the recent American fad for the "nothing box"—an object with no conceivable utility. And as General [Dwight David] Eisenhower explained in all candor, the present economic system can be rescued only by turning human beings into consumers, conflating them with the largest possible number of consumable values—which is to say nonvalues, empty, fictitious, abstract values. After being "the most precious kind of capital," in [Josef] Stalin's happy phrase, human beings must now become the most highly prized of consumer goods. Stereotyped images—movie star, poor person, communist, murderer-out-of-love, law-abiding citizen, rebel, bourgeois—are about to replace humanity with a punch-card system of categories arranged in accordance with an irrefutable robotic logic. Already the idea of "teenager" tends to identify buyers and what they buy, reducing their real variety to a still varied but circumscribed range of commodities (records, guitars, Levi's, etc.). You are no longer as old as you eel or as old as you look but as "old" as what you buy. The time o production, o "time is money," is giving way to the time o consumption (in both the igurative and the material senses o the word), a time measured in terms o products bought, worn out, and thrown away—the time o that premature old age which is the eternal youth o trees and stones.

The theory o pauperization is strikingly conirmed today—not, as Marx expected, in terms o goods necessary or survival, since these, ar rom becoming scarce, have become more and more abundant, but rather in terms o survival itsel, which is ever the enemy o real lie. Modern comorts seemed at irst to promise everyone a lie richer even than the dolce vita o the eudal aristocracy. But in act they turned out to be mere oshoots o capitalist productivity, oshoots doomed to premature old age as soon as the distribution system transormed them into nothing but objects o passive consumption. Working to survive, surviving by consuming and or the sake o consuming: the hellish cycle is complete. According to the logic o the-economyrules, survival is both necessary and suicient. This is the basic reality o the bourgeois era. But it is also true that a historical period based on such an antihuman reality must needs be a period o transition, an intermediate stage between the lie genuinely lived, i less than transparently, by the eudal masters and the lie that will be constructed rationally and passionately by masters without slaves.

In an economy driven by the production requirements o ree-trade capitalism, wealth alone coners power and honor. As master o the means o production and o labor power, wealth ensures by extension, thanks to the development o productive orces and o consumer goods, that its owners enjoy a wealth o choice among the ruits o never-ending progress. But to the extent that this orm o capitalism is transormed into an antithetical orm, namely a state-planned economy, the prestige o the capitalist playing the market with his millions ades away, and with it the caricature o the pot-bellied, cigar-puing merchant o human lesh. Today's managers draw their power rom their organizing skills—even i computer technology already holds them up to ridicule by providing a model that they can never emulate. They are rich in their own right, certainly, but can they vaunt their wealth by having it signiy the potential choices available to them? Can they build a Xanadu, maintain a harem, or cultivate filles-fleurs? Alas, no—or how could money retain its symbolic orce when it is continually solicited and hampered by the imperatives o consumption? Under the dictatorship o consumption money melts away like snow in the sunshine, its signiicance passing to objects with more representational value—more tangible objects better adapted to the welare state and its spectacle. The unction o money has surely already been sidelined by the market in consumer goods, which, duly wrapped in ideology, have become the true signs o power. Beore long, money's only remaining justiication will be the quantity o objects and useless gadgets it enables one to acquire and wear out at an ever-accelerating pace; only the quantity and the pace matter, or mass distribution and standardization automatically wipe out quality and rarity. The ability to consume aster and aster, to change your car, your drink, your house, your TV, or your girlriend ever more requently, is now the only index o how much power you can lay claim to in the social hierarchy. From the preeminence o blood to the power o money, rom the preeminence o money to the power o novelties, Christian and socialist civilization has now attained its highest stage: a civilization o the prosaic and the trivial.

Purchasing power is a license to purchase power. The old proletariat sold its labor power in order to subsist; what little leisure time remained proletarians spent as best they could in conversation, arguments, tavern games, country matters, going on the tramp, estivity, and riot. The new proletariat sells its labor power in order to consume. When they are not too busy working themselves to death in hopes o a promotion, workers are invited to buy objects—a car, a suit and tie, some culture—that will signal their social rank. We have reached the point where the ideology o consumption becomes the consumption o ideology.

The system o commercial exchange has now come to govern all o people's everyday relations with themselves and with their ellows. Every aspect o public and private lie is dominated by the quantitative.

The calculation o a human's capacity to produce or make others produce, to consume or make others consume, is the perect concrete expression o the idea so dear to the philosophers (and so revealing as to their unction) o the measure of man. Even the simple pleasure o a drive in the country is widely assessed in terms o miles on the clock, speeds reached and petrol consumed. Given the rate at which economic imperatives gobble up eelings, desires, and needs, and pay cash to corrupt them, people will soon be let with nothing but the memory o having once existed. History, in which we shall live retrospectively, will be our sole consolation or our condition o survival. How can real joy exist in a space-time that is measurable and continually measured? Not so much as a hearty laugh. At best, the dull satisaction o the person-who's-got-his-money's-worth, and who exists by that standard. Only objects can be measured, which is why all exchange reiies.

The great ideologies quickly abandon aith or numbers. What is the nation? Today it amounts to a ew thousand war veterans. And what is what [Karl] Marx and [Friedrich] Engels used to call "our party"? A ew million voters and a ew thousand bill-posters: a mass party.

In act ideology's essence is drawn rom quantity: ideas reproduced again and again in time (Pavlovian conditioning) and in space (once consumers take up the rerain). Ideology, the news media, and culture—all tend gradually to shed their content and become pure quantity. The less importance a news item has, the more it is repeated, and the more it distracts people rom their real problems. We are a long way rom the Big Lie o which [Joseph] Goebbels said that it was the easiest to swallow. Ideological hyperbole evinces equal conviction to pitch a hundred books, a hundred washing powders, a hundred political ideas, each o which it promotes in turn as ar and away the best. Even in the ideological realm quantity is destroyed by quantity itsel: conditioning is inevitably eroded by its sel-contradictions.

Could this possibly open an avenue back to power o the qualitative, a power that can move mountains? Far rom it: sel-contradictory conditioning is prone to produce trauma, inhibition, or a radical reusal to be brainwashed urther. True, ideology can parry this by leaving conditioned individuals choices between lies, by raising spurious questions, alse dilemmas. But such eeble distractions count or precious little in view o the survival sickness to which consumer society exposes its members.

At any instant boredom can breed an unanswerable rejection o uni ormity. Recent events in Stockholm, Amsterdam, and Watts have shown how the merest pretext can precipitate salutary uprisings. What an immense quantity o ot-repeated lies can be swept away by a single burst o revolutionary poetry! From Villa to Lumumba, rom Stockholm to Watts, qualitative agitation—agitation that radicalizes the masses because its source is the radicalism o the masses—eectively pushes back the rontiers o submission and brutishness.

No one, no matter how alienated, is without (or unaware o) an irreducible core o creativity, a camera obscura sae rom intrusion by lies and constraints. I ever social organization extends its control to this stronghold o humanity, its dominion will no longer be exercised over anything save robots, or corpses. And, in a sense, that is why consciousness o creative energy increases, paradoxically enough, as a unction o consumer society's eorts to co-opt it.

Argus is blind to the danger right in ront o him. Where quantity reigns, quality has no recognized legal existence; but this is the very thing that saeguards and nourishes it. I have already noted that the dissatisaction bred by the manic pursuit o quantity calls orth a radical desire or the qualitative. The more oppression is justiied in terms o the reedom to consume, the more the malaise arising rom this contradiction exacerbates the thirst or total reedom. The crisis o production-based capitalism pointed up the element o repressed creativity in the energy expended by the worker. The alienation o creativity through orced labor, thanks to the exploitation o the producers, was denounced once and or all by Marx. Whatever the capitalist system and its successors (even antagonistic ones) have lost on the production ront they try to make up or in the sphere o consumption. The plan is that, as they gradually ree themselves rom their duties as producers, human beings should be trapped by newer obligations as consumers. By opening up the empty lot o leisure time to a creativity liberated at long last by shorter working hours, the well-intentioned apostles o humanism have merely mustered an army it or drilling on the training grounds o the consumer economy. Now that the alienation o the consumer is being laid bare by consumption's own internal dialectic, one may wonder what kind o prison awaits the highly subversive orces o individual creativity. As I pointed out earlier, the rulers' last chance here is to turn us all into organizers o our own passivity.

With touching candor, De Witt Peters suggests that handing out paints, brushes, and canvas to everyone who requested them would produce very interesting results. It is true that i this policy were applied in a variety o well-deined and well-policed spheres, such as the theater, the plastic arts, music, writing, etc., and in a general way to any such sphere susceptible o total isolation rom all others, then the system might have a hope o endowing people with the consciousness o the artist, which is to say the consciousness o someone who proesses to exhibit their creativity in the museums and shop windows o culture. The popularity o such a culture would be a clear sign o Power's success. Fortunately, the chances o people being successully "culturized" in this way are now slight. Do the cyberneticians and their ilk really imagine that people can be talked into "ree experimentation" within bounds laid down by authoritarian decree? Or that prisoners at last aware o their creative capacity might daub their cells with "original graiti" and leave it at that? What would prevent them rom extending their newound penchant or experiment to weapons, desires, dreams, and all manner o means o sel-ulillment? The crowd, ater all, is already ull o agitators. No: the last possible way o co-opting creativity—the organization o artistic passivity—is, happily, doomed to ailure.

Spontaneity is the mode o being o individual creativity, its original, immaculate orm, neither polluted at the source nor threatened by co-optation. Whereas creativity is the most equitably distributed thing imaginable, spontaneity seems to be the privileged possession o those whom long resistance to Power has endowed with a consciousness o their own value as individuals. In revolutionary moments this means the majority; at other times, when the revolution must be prepared day by day, it means more people than one might suppose. Wherever the light o creativity continues to shine, spontaneity has a chance.

"The new artist protests," wrote [Tristan] Tzara in 1918. "He no longer paints . . . but creates directly." Immediacy is certainly the most succinct, but also the most radical demand that must characterize the new artists o today, who are destined to be constructors o situations to be lived directly. I say succinct because it is important ater all not to be misled by the word spontaneity. The spontaneous can never spring rom internalized constraints, even subconscious ones; nor can it abide alienating abstraction or spectacular co-optation: clearly it is a conquest, not a given. The reconstruction o the individual presupposes the reconstruction o the unconscious (consider the construction o dreams).

What spontaneous creativity has lacked up to now is the clear consciousness o its own poetry. Common sense has always treated spontaneity as a primary state, an initial stage in need o theoretical adjustment, o transposition into abstract terms. This view isolates spontaneity, treats it as a thing-initsel—and thus recognizes it only in the travestied orms which it acquires within the spectacle (e.g., action painting). In point o act spontaneous creativity carries the seeds o its eective development within itsel. It is possessed o its own poetry.

I have already said that creativity, though equitably distributed to all, inds direct, spontaneous expression only on speciic occasions. These are prerevolutionary moments, the source o the poetry that changes lie and transorms the world.

Those who make a proession o creating, and those whose proession prevents them rom creating, both artists and workers, are being pushed into the same nihilism by the process o proletarianization. This process, which is accompanied by resistance to it—resistance, that is, to co-opted orms o creativity—occurs amid such a plethora o cultural goods—records, ilms, paperback books—that once these commodities have been reed rom the laws o consumption they will pass immediately into the service o true creativity. The sabotage o the mechanisms o economic and cultural consumption is epitomized by young people who steal the books in which they expect to ind conirmation o their radicalism.

What is poetry? It is the organization o creative spontaneity, the deployment o the qualitative in accordance with its coherent inner logic. Poetry is what the Greeks call poiein, or "making," but making restored to the purity o its original impulse—restored, in a word, to the totality.

Without the qualitative, no poetry is possible. The void let by poetry is illed by poetry's opposites: inormation, transitional programs, specialization, reormism—all the motley guises o the ragmentary. But the presence o the qualitative does not o itsel ensure the progression o poetry. The richest complex o signs and possibilities may well lapse into conusion, all apart or lack o consistency, or crumble by reason o crossed purposes. The yardstick o eectiveness must always remain supreme. Poetry is thus also radical theory completely integrated into action, the mortar binding tactics and revolutionary strategy, and the high point o the great game o everyday lie.

What is poetry? In 1895, during an ill-advised and seemingly doomed French rail strike, a militant o the National Railwaymen's Union stood up and suggested an ingenious and cheap way o advancing the strikers' cause: "It takes two pennyworth o a particular substance used in the right way to immobilize a locomotive." It was not very long beore the government and the bosses caved in. Poetry in this case was clearly the act that brought a new reality into being, that reversed the perspective. The materia prima is within everyone's reach. Poets are those who know how to use it to best eect. Furthermore, two pennyworth o some chemical is as nothing compared with the abundance o peerless ready energy aorded by everyday lie itsel: the energy o the will to live, the power o desire unleashed, the passion o love, the love o passion, the orce o ear and anxiety, the rising tide o hate, and the repercussions o wild destructiveness. Who knows what poetic upheavals may conidently be expected to stem rom such universally experienced eelings as those associated with death, old age, and sickness? This still marginal consciousness will surely be the starting point o the long revolution o everyday lie, the only true poetry made by all and not by one alone.

"What is poetry?" ask the aesthetes. So, or their beneit, let us state the obvious: rarely does poetry today involve poems. Most works o art are betrayals o poetry. How could it be otherwise, when poetry and power cannot be reconciled? At best the artist's creativity builds a prison or itsel, cloistering itsel, awaiting its moment, within an oeuvre that has not yet said its last word; but, however high its author's hopes, that last word—supposed to herald perect communication—can never be pronounced so long as the revolt o creativity has not yet brought art to its ulillment.

The Arican work o art—poem, music, sculpture, or mask—is not considered complete until it has become creative speech, an active word: it must function. This holds true well beyond Arican art. There is no art in the world which does not seek to function; and to unction—later co-optation notwithstanding—in accordance with the will that generated it, the will to live continually in the euphoria o the moment o creation. Why is it that the greatest works never seem to be inished? The answer is that great art cries out in every possible way or ulillment, or the right to enter the world o lived experience. The degeneration o present-day art is a bow perectly readied or such an arrow.

Nothing can save past culture rom the cult o the past except those pictures, writings, musical or built architecture, and so on, whose qualitative dimension reaches us independently o their orms (contaminated by the decay now aecting all artistic orms).

As we know, consumer society reduces art to a range o consumable products. This vulgarizing tendency accelerates degeneration but by the same token improves the prospects o supersession. That communication so urgently sought by the artist is jammed and banned even in the simplest relationships o everyday lie. So true is this that the search or new orms o communication, ar rom being the preserve o painters and poets, is now part o a collective eort. This is the end o the old specialization o art. There are no more artists because everyone is an artist. The work o art o the uture will be the construction o a passionate lie.

Poetry is always somewhere. I it leaves the realm o the arts, it is all the easier to see that it belongs irst and oremost in action, in a way o living and in the search or a way o living. Everywhere repressed, this poetry springs up everywhere. Brutally put down, it is reborn in violence. It consecrates riots, embraces rebellions, and animates all great revolutionary carnivals until the bureaucrats place it under house arrest in their hagiographical culture.

Lived poetry has eectively shown throughout history, even in partial revolts, even in crime—which [Ernest] Coeurderoy so aptly dubbed the "revolt o one"—that it is the protector par excellence o everything irreducible in mankind, meaning creative spontaneity. The will to unite the individual and the social, not on the basis o illusory community but on that o subjectivity, is what makes the new poetry into a weapon that everyone must learn to handle by themselves. Poetic experience is now at a premium. The organization o spontaneity will be the work o spontaneity itsel.

### NOTES

This piece is excerpted rom Raoul Vaneigem, The Revolution of Everyday Life, trans. Donald Nicholson-Smith (Oakland, CA: PM Press, 2012). Reprinted by permission o PM Press (http://www.pmpress.org).

1. Tristan Tzara, "Dada Maniesto," March 23, 1918, available at http://391.org/maniestos/1918-dada-maniesto-tristan-tzara.html#. Wa2M6siGOUk.

# 6.2 Info-labor/Precarization

## F "B" B

## h PYChC CAP f h CmY

The digital nervous system incorporates itsel progressively in the organic nervous system, in the circuit o human communication, and recodiies it according to its operational lines and according to its own speed. But in order to ulill this transormation, the body-mind must pass through an inernal mutation, that we see developing in the history o the world. To understand and to analyze this process, neither the conceptual instruments o political economy nor the instruments o technological analysis are su icient. The process o production becomes semiotic and the ormation o the digital nervous system co-involves and enervates the mind, the social psyche, desires and hopes, ears and imaginings. Thereore i we want to analyze these productive transormations, we must concern ourselves with semiotic production, with linguistic and cognitive mutations. And mutation passes through the range o pathologies.

Neoliberal culture has injected into the social brain a constant stimulus toward competition, and the technical system o the digital network has rendered possible an intensiication o inormatic stimuli, transmitted rom the social brain to individual brains. This acceleration o stimuli is a pathogenic actor that has wide-ranging eects in society. Economic competition and digital intensiication o inormatic stimuli, combined together, induce a state o permanent electrocution that lows into a widespread pathology which maniests itsel either in the panic syndrome or in attention disorders.

Panic is an ever more widespread syndrome. Until a ew years ago, psychiatrists hardly recognized this symptom that belonged rather to the romantic literary imagination, and could approach the eeling o being overwhelmed by the ininite richness o the orms o nature by unlimited cosmic power. Today, instead, panic is ever more requently denounced as a painul and worrying symptom, the physical sensation o no longer succeeding in governing one's own body, an acceleration o the heart rate, a shortness o breath that can lead to ainting and paralysis. Even i to my knowledge exhaustive research does not exist in this area, the hypothesis can be proposed that the mediatization o communication and the consequent rareaction o physical contact can provoke pathologies in the aective and emotional sphere. For the irst time in human history, there is a generation that has learnt more words and heard more stories rom the televisual machine than rom its mother. Attention disturbances are more and more widespread. Millions o North American and European children are treated or a disturbance that maniests itsel as the incapacity to maintain concentrated attention on an object or more than a ew seconds. The constant excitation o the mind on the part o neurostimulant luxes probably leads to a pathological saturation. I we want to understand the contemporary economy we must concern ourselves with the psychopathology o relations. And i we want to understand contemporary psychochemistry we must take into account the act that the mind is invested by semiotic luxes that ollow an extra-semiotic principle: the principle o economic competition, the principle o maximum development. From the time when capitalism connected to the brain, the latter incorporated a pathological agent, a psychotic meme that will accelerate pulsations even to tremors, even to collapse.

In the 1990s, Prozac culture was intermingled with the new economy. Hundreds o thousands o operators, directors, and managers o the occidental economy took innumerable decisions in a state o chemical euphoria and psychopharmacological lightheadedness. But in the long term the organism collapsed, unable to support indeinitely the chemical euphoria that had sustained competitive enthusiasm and productivist anaticism. Collective attention was supersaturated and this was provoking a collapse o a social and economic kind. As happens in a manic depressive organism, as happens with a patient aected by bipolar disorder, ater the inancial euphoria o the 1990s, there ollowed a depression. It is thereore a case o clinical depression that strikes motivation, initiative, sel-esteem, desire, and sex appeal at the roots. To understand the crisis o the new economy it is necessary to begin rom the psychic experience o the virtual class; it is necessary to relect on the psychic and emotional state o the millions o cognitive workers who animated the scene o business, culture, and the imaginary during the decade o the 1990s. The individual psychic depression o a single cognitive worker is not a consequence o the economic crisis but its cause. It would be simple to consider depression as a consequence o a bad business cycle. Ater having worked or so many years happily and proitably, the value o shares has plummeted and our brainworker is overcome by an ugly depression. It does not happen in this way. Depression descends on the cognitive worker because his or her own emotional, physical, intellectual system cannot indeinitely support the hyperactivity provoked by the market and by pharmaceuticals. As a consequence, things are set to go badly in the market. What is the market? The market is the place in which signs and nascent meanings, desires and projections meet. I we want to speak o demand and supply, we must reason in terms o luxes o desire and semiotic attractors that ormerly had appeal and today have lost it.

In the Net economy, lexibility has evolved into a orm o ractalization o work. Fractalization means the modular and recombinant ragmentation o the time o activity. The worker no longer exists as a person. He or she is only an interchangeable producer o microragments o recombinant semiosis that enter into the continuous lux o the Net. Capital no longer pays or the availability o a worker to be exploited or a long period o time; it no longer pays a salary that covers the entire range o economic needs o a person who works. The worker (a machine endowed with a brain that can be used or ragments o time) becomes paid or his or her occasional, temporary services. Work time is ragmented and cellularized. Cells o time are or sale on the Net and businesses can buy as much as they want without being obligated in any way in the social protection o the worker.

The intense and prolonged investment o mental and libidinal energies in the labor process has created the conditions or a psychic collapse that is trans erred into the economic ield with the recession and the all in demand and into the political ield in the orm o military aggressivity. The use o the word collapse is not as a metaphor but as a clinical description o what is happening in the occidental mind. The word collapse expresses a real and exact pathological phenomenon that invests the psychosocial organism. That which we have seen in the period ollowing the irst signs o economic decline, in the irst months o the new century, is a psychopathic phenomenon o overexcitation, trembling, panic, and inally o a depressive all. The phenomena o economic depression have always contained elements o the crisis o the psychosocial equilibrium, but when at last the process o production has involved the brain in a massive way, psychopathology has become the crucial aspect o economic cycles.

The available attention time or the workers involved in the inormatic cycle is constantly being reduced: they are involved in a growing number o mental tasks that occupy every ragment o their attention time. For them there is no longer the time to dedicate to love, to tenderness, to aection. They take Viagra because they don't have time or sexual preliminaries. They take cocaine to be continuously alert and reactive. They take Prozac to cancel out the awareness o the senselessness that unexpectedly empties their lie o any interest. Cellularization has brought about a type o permanent occupation o living time. The eect is a mutation o social relations in a psychopathic direction. The signs are evident: millions o packets o psychopharmaceuticals sold, an epidemic o attention disturbances spreading among children and adolescents, the becoming normal o the diusion o drugs like Ritalin in schools, and what seems to be the spreading o an epidemic o panic in the abric o everyday lie.

## h f-Phr AD h CA mD

The mediascape is the universe o transmitters that send to our brain signals according to the most varied ormats. The ino-sphere is the interace between the media system and the mind that receives the signals, the mental ecosphere, that immaterial sphere in which semiotic luxes interact with the reception antennae o the minds scattered on the planet. The mind is the universe o receivers that are not naturally limited to receiving but process, create, and in their turn put in motion new processes o transmission and provoke the continuous evolution o the mediascape.

The evolution o the ino-sphere's activation o always more complex networks o inormation distribution has produced a leap in the power, speed, and the very ormat o the ino-sphere. There is no corresponding leap in the power and ormat o reception.

The universe o receivers, human brains o real people made o lesh, ragile and sensual organs, is not ormatted according to the same standard as the system o digital transmitters. The unctional paradigm o the universe o transmitters does not correspond to the unctional paradigm o the universe o receivers. This asymmetry is maniested by various pathological eects: permanent electrocution, panic, overexcitation, hypermobility, attention disturbances, dyslexia, inormation overload, and saturation o reception circuits.

At the origin o this saturation, there is a real and proper deormity o ormats. The ormat o the universe o transmitters has evolved, multiplying its powers, while the ormat o the universe o receivers has not been able to evolve in as rapid a manner, or the simple reason that it is based on an organic support (the human brain-body) that has evolutionary times completely dierent rom the evolutionary times o machines.

That which is being determined could be deined as a paradigmatic discrepancy, a schism between the paradigm that models the universe o transmitters and the paradigm that models the universe o receivers. In a situation like this, communication becomes an asymmetrical disturbed process. We could speak in this regard o a discrepancy between cyberspace in unlimited and constant expansion and cybertime. Cyberspace is a network that includes mechanical and organic components whose processing power can be accelerated without limits, while cybertime is an essentially lived reality, linked to an organic support (the human body and brain) whose processing time cannot be accelerated beyond relatively rigid natural limits.

Since the time when, in 1977, he wrote the book Speed and Politics, Paul Virilio has maintained that speed is the decisive actor in modern history. It is thanks to speed, Virilio claims, that wars are won, both military and commercial ones. In many o his writings, Virilio shows that the speed o movements, o transportation, o motorization has allowed armies to win wars in the course o the last century. Since then, it has been possible to substitute objects, goods, and people or signs. By virtual, electronically transerable phantasms, the barriers o speed have been broken and the most impressive process o acceleration that human history has ever known has erupted. In a certain sense we can say that space no longer exists, given that inormation can cross it instantly and events can be transerred in real time rom one place to another on the planet, becoming virtually shared events. But what are the consequences o this acceleration on the human mind, on the human body? To understand it we must make reerence to the capacity o conscious processing, to the capacity or aective assimilation o signs and events on the part o the conscious and sensitive organism.

The acceleration o inormation exchange has produced and is producing an eect o a pathological type on the individual human mind and even more on the collective mind. Individuals are not in a position to consciously process the immense and always growing mass o inormation that enters their computers, their cell phones, their television screens, their electronic diaries, and their heads. However, it seems indispensable to ollow, recognize, evaluate, process all this inormation i you want to be eicient, competitive, victorious. The practice o multitasking, the opening o a window o hypertextual attention, the passage rom one context to another or the complex evaluation o processes, tends to deorm the sequential modality o mental processing. According to Christian Marazzi, who has concerned himsel in various books with the relations between economics, language, and aectivity, the latest generation o economic operators is aected by a real and proper orm o dyslexia, incapable o reading a page rom the beginning to the end according to sequential procedures, incapable o maintaining concentrated attention on the same object or a long time. And dyslexia spreads to cognitive and social behaviors, leading to rendering the pursuit o linear strategies nearly impossible.

Some, like [Thomas H.] Davenport and [John C.] Beck, speak o an attention economy. But when a cognitive aculty enters into and becomes part o economic discourse, this means that it has become a scarce resource. The necessary time or paying attention to the luxes o inormation to which we are exposed and which must be evaluated in order to be able to make decisions is lacking. The consequence is in ront o our eyes: political and economic decisions no longer respond to a long-term strategic rationality and simply ollow immediate interests. On the other hand, we are always less available or giving our attention to others gratuitously. We no longer have the attention time or love, tenderness, nature, pleasure, and compassion. Our attention is ever more besieged and thereore we assign it only to our careers, to competition, and to economic decisions. And in any case our temporality cannot ollow the insane speed o the hypercomplex digital machine. Human beings tend to become the ruthless executors o decisions taken without attention.

The universe o transmitters, or cyberspace, now proceeds at a superhuman velocity and becomes untranslatable or the universe o receivers, or cybertime, that cannot go aster than what is allowed by the physical material rom which our brain is made, the slowness o our body, the need or caresses and aection. Thus opens a pathological gap and mental illness spreads as testiied by the statistics and above all our everyday experience. And just as pathology spreads, so too do drugs. The lourishing industry o psychopharmaceuticals beats records every year; the number o packets o Ritalin, Prozac, Zolot, and other psychotropics sold in the pharmacies continually increases, while dissociation, suering, desperation, terror, the desire not to exist, to not have to ight continuously, to disappear grows alongside the will to kill and to kill onesel.

When, toward the end o the 1970s, an acceleration o the productive and communicative rhythms in occidental metropolitan centers was imposed, a gigantic epidemic o drug addiction made its appearance. The world was leaving its human epoch to enter the era o machinic post-human acceleration: many sensitive organisms o the human variety began to snort cocaine, a substance that permits the acceleration o the existential rhythm leading to transorming onesel into a machine. Many other sensitive organisms o the human kind injected heroin in their veins, a substance that deactivates the relation with the speed o the surrounding atmosphere. The epidemic o powders during the 1970s and the 1980s produced an existential and cultural devastation with which we still haven't come to terms. Then illegal drugs were replaced by those legal substances which the pharmaceutical industry in a white coat made available or its victims, and this was the epoch o antidepressants, o euphorics, and o mood regulators.

Today psychopathy reveals itsel ever more clearly as a social epidemic and, more precisely, a socio-communicational one. I you want to survive you have to be competitive, and i you want to be competitive you must be connected, receive, and process continuously an immense and growing mass o data. This provokes a constant attentive stress, a reduction o the time available or aectivity. These two tendencies, inseparably linked, provoke an eect o devastation on the individual psyche: depression, panic, anxiety, the sense o solitude and existential misery. But these individual symptoms cannot be indeinitely isolated, as psychopathology has done up until now and as economic power wishes to do. It is not possible to say: "You are exhausted, go and take a vacation at Club Med, take a pill, make a cure, get the hell away rom it all, recover in the psychiatric hospital, kill yoursel." It is no longer possible, or the simple reason that it is no longer a matter o a small minority o crazies or a marginal amount o depressives. It concerns a growing mass o existential misery that is tending always more to explode in the center o the social system. Besides, it is necessary to consider a decisive act: at the time when capital needed to suck in physical energy rom its exploited and rom its slaves, psychopathology could be relatively marginalized. Your psychic suering didn't matter much to capital when you only had to insert screws and handle a lathe. You could be as sad as a solitary ly in a bottle, but your productivity was hardly aected because your muscles could still unction. Today capital needs mental energies, psychic energies. And these are exactly the capacities that are ucking up. It's because o this that psychopathology is exploding in the center o the social scene. The economic crisis depends or the most part on a circulation o sadness, depression, panic, and demotivation. The crisis o the new economy was provoked in a large part by a crisis o motivations, by a all o the artiicial euphoria o the 1990s. This has led to eects o disinvestment and in part even to a reduction o consumption. In general, unhappiness unctions as a stimulus to consume: buying is a suspension o anxiety, an antidote to loneliness, but only up to a certain point. Beyond this certain point, suering becomes a demotivating actor or purchasing. There is thereore an elaboration o conlicting strategies. The masters o the world certainly do not want humanity to be able to be happy, because a happy humanity would not let itsel be caught up in productivity, in the discipline over work or in hypermarkets. However, they try out useul techniques to make unhappiness moderate and tolerable, or postponing or preventing a suicidal explosion, or inducing consumption.

What strategies will the collective organism ollow in order to escape this abric o unhappiness? Is a strategy o deceleration, o the reduction o complexity possible and able to be hypothesized? I don't believe so. In human society, potentialities cannot be deinitively canceled out, even when they are revealed to be lethal or the individual and probably even or the species. These potentials become regulated and kept under control or as long as possible, but in the end are inevitably used as happened (and will happen again) with the atomic bomb. A strategy o the upgrading o the human organism is possible—o the mechanical adjustment o the human body and brain to a hyperast ino-sphere. This is the strategy that is used to deine the posthuman. Finally a strategy o subtraction is possible, o distancing rom the vortex, but this is a type o strategy that only small communities can ollow, constituting spheres o existential, economic, and inormatic autonomy with respect to the economic world.

## PAC WAr AD m-CAPA

Globalization stands reramed in the dark light o the global war. This means we need to reconceptualize the change that is taking place in the social, economic, and anthropological orm o globalization. During the past two centuries, global control was the general techno-utopia o capitalist society and modern culture. Now, the time o global control is over. We are completely out o this ramework today. The new governing ramework o capitalism is global panic. I we want to understand what panic means we have to talk about the attention economy and about "digital labor." This is where the source o contemporary panic lies: in the organization o time in the digital sphere, in the relationship between cyberspace and cybertime.

What is panic? We are told that psychiatrists have recently discovered and named a new kind o disorder—they call it Panic Syndrome. It seems that it's something quite recent in the psychological sel-perception o human beings. But what does panic mean?

Once, panic used to be a nice word, and this is the sense in which the Swiss-American psychoanalyst James Hillman remembers it in his book on Pan. Pan used to be the god o nature, the god o totality. In Greek mythology, Pan was the symbol o the relationship between man and nature.

Nature is the overwhelming low o reality, things, and inormation that we are surrounded by. Modern culture is based on the idea o human domination, o the domestication o nature. So the original panic eeling, which was something good or the ancient world, is becoming increasingly terriying and destructive. Today, panic has become a orm o psychopathology. We can speak o panic when we see a conscious organism (individual or social) being overwhelmed by the speed o processes he/she is involved in, and has no time to process the inormation input. In these cases the organism, all o a sudden, is no more able to process the sheer amount o inormation coming into its cognitive ield or even that which is being generated by the organism itsel.

Technological transormations have displaced the ocus rom the sphere o the production o material goods toward the sphere o semiotic goods: the ino-sphere. With this, semio-capital becomes the general orm o the economy. The accelerated creation o surplus value depends on the acceleration o the ino-sphere. The digitalization o the ino-sphere opens the road to this kind o acceleration. Signs are produced and circulated at a growing speed but the human terminal o the system (the embodied mind) is put under growing pressure, and inally it cracks. I think that the current economic crisis has something to do with this imbalance in the ield o semio-production and in the ield o semio-demand. This imbalance in the relationship between the supply o semiotic goods and the socially available time o attention is the core o the economic crisis as well as the core o the intellectual and the political crises that we are living through now.

We can describe this situation in terms o the relationship between cyberspace and cybertime. Cyberspace is the ininite productivity o collective intelligence in a networked dimension. The potency o the general intellect is enormously enhanced when a huge number o points enter into connections with each other thanks to the telematic network. Consequently, ino-production is able to create an ininite supply o mental and intellectual goods. But while cyberspace is conceptually ininite, cybertime is not ininite at all. I call cybertime the ability o the conscious organism to actually process (cyberspatial) inormation. This ability cannot be indeinitely expanded, because it has limits that are physical, emotional, aective. The contradiction between ininite expansion o cyberspace and limited capability o processing o cybertime is the origin o contemporary chaos.

[Gilles] Deleuze and [Félix] Guattari talk about chaos in What Is Philosophy? They say that chaos occurs when the world goes too ast or your brain. This is chaos.

We could recall that Karl Marx had once expressed the concept o an overproduction crisis. You have an overproduction crisis when machinery and the labor o workers produce an amount o goods that the market cannot absorb. During the history o the industrial system, the overproduction crisis was recurrent, and capitalism was pushed to destroy goods, destroy productive capacity, and also destroy human lives, in order to overcome this kind o economic crisis.

What is going to happen now? Should we see a relationship between this big imbalance and the war that is raging and obscuring the horizon o the world? Let's go back to the concept o panic.

Semio-capital is in a crisis o overproduction, but the orm o this crisis is not only economic but also psychopathic. Semio-capital, in act, is not about the production o material goods but about the production o psychic stimulation. The mental environment is saturated by signs that create a sort o continuous excitation, a permanent electrocution, which leads the individual mind as well as the collective mind to a state o collapse.

The problem o panic is generally connected with the management o time. But we can also see a spatial side to panic. During the past centuries, the building o the modern urban environment used to be dependent on the rationalist plan o the political city. The economic dictatorship o the last ew decades has accelerated urban expansion. The interaction between cyberspatial sprawl and urban physical environment has destroyed the rationalist organization o space.

In the intersection o inormation and urban space we see the prolieration o a chaotic sprawl ollowing no rule, no plan, dictated by the sole logic o economic interest. Urban panic is caused by the perception o this sprawl and this prolieration o metropolitan experience; the prolieration o spatial lines o light. The metropolis is a surace o complexity in the territorial domain. The social organism is unable to process the overwhelmingly complex experience o metropolitan chaos. The prolieration o lines o communication has created a new kind o chaotic perception.

In their book The Attention Economy, Davenport and Beck say that the central problem o the cognitive worker, and generally o people who are living in hypersaturated inormational environments, is this: we have no more time or attention, we are no more able to understand and process inormation input because our time is saturated by a low o hyperinormation. We don't have time or attention in the workplace. We are orced to process ar too large amounts o inormation and our body-mind is completely taken by this. And urther, we have no time or aection, or communication, or erotic relationships. We have no more time or that spatial kind o attention that means attention to the body—to our body, to the body o the other. So, more and more, we eel that we have run out o time; that we must accelerate. And we eel simultaneously that acceleration leads to a loss o lie, o pleasure and o understanding.

## CmP

The concept o competition has replaced that o competence.

Competence is the intellectual skill that enabled the bourgeoisie to carry out its planning, administrative, and organizational unction, and justiied its right to property.

Ever since the technologies o intelligence made it possible to standardize the processes o planning, coordination, and administration that once coalesced with the role o ownership, intellectual unctions have turned into the unctions o subordinate labor.

The competent bourgeoisie was replaced by a class that turned competition into the only rule and competence. However, when speaking o competition, is it not obvious that the most competitive is the one who can eliminate the adversaries? And when it comes to eliminating adversaries things get serious.

As property came to coincide with a dusty cloud o ractions o investment rather than with the person, competition replaced competence. Many competences are still necessary to production, but they are now detached rom the role o the enterprise. Any intellectual competence that is not related to speculation is made precarious, devalued, and low waged.

Crime is no longer a marginal unction o the capitalist system but the decisive winning actor or deregulated competition. Torture, homicide, child exploitation, the drive to prostitution, and the production o instruments o mass destruction have become irreplaceable techniques o economic competition. Crime is best suited to the principle o competition.

# 6.3 Imaginal Machines

### STEVPHEN SHUKAITIS

There has long existed both an intense and troubled connection between avant-garde arts and autonomous politics. That a composition is aesthetically innovative does not necessarily mean that it's particularly progressive politically, either in its content or the relations animated through its creation and circulation. The Futurists do not just represent the point where, as Stewart Home explores, utopian currents based around the integration of all aspects of life, which were viewed primarily as religious during medieval times, moved over to be considered part of the artistic sphere. The Futurists are perhaps the clearest indication that interesting aesthetics do not necessarily correspond to progressive politics, as their intense fascination with war, speed, patriotism, and eventual movement toward fascism display. There are countless more examples less dramatic but all the more complex, ranging from surrealism's turbulent relation to the institutional communist movement (and eventual severing and usage by U. S. state propaganda efforts and fronts) to debates about the role of street art in gentrification.

One recurring challenge for political art is to circumvent the assumption, implicitly contained within a didactic composition, that the work's arguments can take place in an already existing public sphere—the common ground and frame of reference—that preexists the particular expression. Unaware of this challenge, much political artwork strives to create interesting and compelling arguments, flourishes of speech, in hopes that the message will reach the listener with little interference. In order for political speech to cause affective resonance, conditions need to exist for the constituted audience to be able to identify with those who are expressing them, to possess a capacity to affect and be affected. This process of affective composition so often begins from minor moments and interactions: yet through them spaces of commonality, where new relations and interactions are possible, emerge. [Gilles] Deleuze and [Félix] Guattari's observation that "the people are missing" is not a lament but a realization that the task of politics is precisely the composition of common space through processes of intensive engagement not bound by the closure of already understood identities and positions.2 This is precisely the point explored by Randy Martin in his work on the role o theater in the producing and orming o publics, acting as a means or the production o socialist ensembles: "publics must continue to be generated i a revolutionary project is to maintain its resonance." For Martin autonomy is not something granted but rather a critical presence existing through and despite networks o governance. The continual generation o new publics, o new orms o the resonance o ideas and relations, is the process o aective composition, whether through the orming o publics through theater or any other o the possible means.

But what is meant by aective composition? At the risk o launching yet another trendy neologism, the concept o aective composition is ormed by the bringing together o notions o aect with the autonomist notion o class composition. The concept o aect was developed in a submerged history o philosophy, stretching rom Spinoza to Deleuze and Guattari (and having been developed urther by igures such as Antonio Negri and Genevieve Lloyd), to indicate an increase in capacity to aect or to be aected by the world. For Deleuze and Guattari, artistic creation is the domain o aective resonance, where imagination shits through the interacting bodies. Composition is used here, borrowing rom the autonomist Marxist notion o class composition, indicating the autonomous and collective capacities to change the world created through social resistance. As orms o collective capacity and sel-organization are increased, composed by the circulation o struggles and ideas, the workings o the state and capitalism attempt to ind ways to disperse them or to integrate these social energies into their own workings. Thus there are ormed cycles o the composition, decomposition, and recomposition o struggles. A key insight o autonomist thought was the argument that the nature o struggles and the orms o social cooperation created within in them determine the direction o capitalist development, rather than the autonomous sel-directed power o capital. Considering aective composition through orms o street art and perormance is to look at the ways that the capacities they create contribute to the development o aective capacities and orms o sel-organization. It is the ways in which street arts can take place in what the Inernal Noise Brigade mission statement describes as "acilit[ating] the sel-actualization o the mob." This sel-actualization is not something which ever reaches a inal or inished point, but continually doubles over and immanently above itsel, turning cycles o struggles into spirals o movement composition.

The aective composition o relations and intensities in aesthetic politics is a pressing question because o ways that the possibilities or the existence o public and common space have changed over recent years. The increasingly drastic commercialization o public space, corporate domination o media outlets, and predominance o earmongering in all areas o lie has created a condition where there are immense lows o inormation and data available or discussion but precious little public sphere in which this data can resonate. Paolo Virno argues that where orms o collective intelligence do not ind expression in a public sphere where common aairs can be attended to, it produces terriying eects and prolierations o unchecked and groundless hierarchies. These are areas o "publicness without a public sphere." There are lows o inormation and images constantly surrounding and immersing us that allow or new possibilities or communication and the ormation o subjectivities, but which can also be quite overwhelming and go in directions that are not necessarily liberatory. Chat rooms and blogs meld seamlessly with the commercial landscapes o gentriied cities and the twenty-ourhour-a-day low o "news" that may excite the libido or intone the constant reminder o "be araid" but do not constitute a common place o collective engagement. More than anything they tend to proactively prevent the emergence o shared space in ways that have not been overcoded by the workings o state or capital.

One cannot assume that there is an already existing public sphere, an existing arrangements o bodies, ready to receive inormation conveyed through an artistic composition. Relying on the expected aesthetics o propaganda means circumscribing possible patterns o resonance more limited than might be wished. Political art derives its politics not just by its content but also by the ways in which it is designed to work with or against the predetermined orms o circulation o ideas, images, and relations. In other words, to appreciate that orms o street art do not derive their subversiveness simply rom the act that they occur on the street (which can also include a whole range o viral marketing and quotidian orms o spectacular recuperation), but rather rom unolding the relations that avoid the overcoding operations o the art institution and commodity production. It is this ocus on patterns o circulation and relations as a politico-aesthetic activity, what George Katsiaicas describes as "engaging aesthetic rationality in the process o political transormation, o turning politics into art, everyday lie into an aesthetically governed domain," that comprises the process o aective composition.

One approach to understanding relationships and the construction o community in an artistic ramework recently has been Nicolas Bourriaud's notion o "relational aesthetics," which he developed during the mid-1990s coming out o his curatorial work. Relational aesthetics, which Bourriaud rames as part o a materialist tradition, is argued to represent a theory not o art but o orm—namely, one where "intersubjectivity does not only represent the social setting or the reception o art . . . but also becomes the quintessence o artistic practice." Collective understandings, experiences, and interactions then are not something that is added on the work aterward but rather compose the starting point and substance o the work itsel. While any artwork can be thought o as a relational object through the kinds o interactions it animates (otentimes a gentle stare with a determined air o trying to appear clever), the dierence here is that these relations are the core o the work itsel. Bourriaud argues that today the designation o art seems to be little more than a "semantic letover" which should be replaced by a deinition like "art is an activity consisting in producing relationships with the world with the help o signs, orms, actions, and objects."

From this Bourriaud tries to recast the critical unction o aesthetic intervention, arguing that rather than being based on orming imaginary and utopian realities, artistic intervention is aimed at orming living models o action and being within the existing world. The artistic composition exists then as "a social interstice within these experiments and these new 'lie possibilities' appear as possible." A piece's aura no longer lies in another projected world, or in the orm itsel, but rather in the temporary collectives and communities that coalesce or the purposes o producing the exhibition or the space itsel (although or Bourriaud clearly this is all about the exhibition rather than any diuse process o creation going beyond the gallery walls). For Bourriaud the subversive and critical unction o artistic creation is the invention o individual and collective vanishing lines, lines o light, in the creation o temporary nomadic constructions.

At irst this seems reasonable enough, and relatively close to what I'm describing as the process o aective composition, the movement rom artistic creation based around objects to the creation o relations and modulations o aect. But slowly the situation becomes more problematic. For instance, there's no necessary dichotomy between orming imaginary and utopian realities and micropractices o intervention. Utopian dreams and models, rather than being stated and ixed models to impose, have oten acted as inspirations or inding micropolitical modes o intervention, which is to say that they have oten been connected rather than mutually exclusive. Likewise one can build an overall vision drawing rom and extending micropractices. But what might at irst appear to be a relatively minor dierence not worth quibbling about that much becomes more pronounced, especially when Bourriaud makes the argument that today "social utopias and revolutionary hopes have given way to everyday micro-utopias and imitative strategies, [and] any stance that is directly 'critical' o society is utile." Rather than there being just a distinction between imaginary and utopian projects, here it is argued that these projects insoar as they are directly critical o the social world (the state, capitalism, war, exploitation) are utile. In other words, dear artist, give up any hopes o having anything to say about social conditions on any large scale and make yoursel content with micro-interventions and practices. This sounds quite similar to approaches ascribed to politics and thought coming in the wake o 1968 and poststructuralism and so-called identity politics. In other words, a shit in politics, rom grand to minor, in a way that micropractices and interventions are divorced rom large questions and social structures.

This does not mean that all practices and questions have to be directly oriented to addressing large-scale social and historical projects, as might be argued by the looming clichéd image o the overly zealous Trotskyite who insists that everything must be about directly contesting capitalism rom the perspective o the working class or it is "objectively reactionary." There are many ways to congeal and conjoin minor articulations inormed by and relating to large social questions that are undamentally concerned with an overall social critique, without having that critique determine and overly con ine these practices.

But let us return or a second to a particular kind o moment o the breaking down o barriers through aective composition, namely through orming an aective space through and around the perormance o radical marching bands. A moment where the passivity o the crowd perhaps is broken and the nature o the space is transormed. Bodies milling about, held awkwardly at a distance, a space maintained and looks a little chilly. Not rom malice or mistrust but rom not knowing. But in that instant borders all. The irst hit o the drum is the irst crack in the wall o the objectiying, separating gaze, the space created by the passive stare o an audience toward a perormance, an exhibition: a spectacle. As the melody pulses through the crowd we revel in the timbre o the horns. Arms, words, memory, and noise tenuously connect through time and desire. Ideas, memories, histories, cultures, and stories are crossbred. Rage blends with joy; dislocation is replaced by emerging, momentary worlds.

It is in this sense that radical marching bands are o the most interest: in the ways they undercut the usual space (and sometimes relations) o perormance and create mobile and aective spaces in the streets where it becomes possible or other orms o relations to emerge. Projects such as the Hungry March Band, the Inernal Noise Brigade, and Rhythms o Resistance, closely connected with the late 1990s upswing in streets protests and parties such as Reclaim the Streets, brought carnivalesque energies and excitement into the all too oten ritualistic and stale mode o political protest. One could argue, perhaps paradoxically, that while in the upswing o organizing and summit protests that occurred in the post-'99 realization there already were existing movements against the more egregious excesses o the state and capitalism; there was somewhat o a shit away rom diuse orms o cultural politics to more spectacular media-riendly orms such as mass mobilizations and lockdowns. Radical marching bands and other orms o tactical rivolity were important in keeping open space or the emergence o intensive and aective relations within such spaces, relations which hopeully would ind their ways out on to all o the abric o daily lie.

Hakim Bey describes how marching bands were invented by Turkish Janissaries, members o the Ottoman Imperial Guard, who belonged to a heterodox Bektashi Sui order. The marching band, developed or use in military campaigns, unctioned as a orm o psychological warare through music that induced sheer terror in their opponents. Their eect on European armies, who had never experienced anything like that previously, must have been complete ear, most likely resulting in increased morale among the Turkish troops. Marching bands were adopted by European states or use in military campaigns and increasingly in symbolic and ceremonial unctions as orms o ampliied communications technologies became developed. Forms o marching band music moved with the migration o Roma people rom the Ottoman Empire to the southern United States, who brought along with them brass instruments that had a proound eect on the development o music in the area.

Not surprisingly, then, the repertoire o many marching bands is also a veritable melting pot o styles, cultures, and background, bringing together anything rom jazz and big band tunes to klezmer, Moroccan music and Indian wedding tunes to calypso, salsa, reggae, and Sun Ra. There are also large degrees o inspiration rom projects that have merged together the energy o punk rock and street perormance, such as Crash Worship and ¡Tch Kung! (who had members that went on to orm marching bands). There are large degrees o crossover and mixing between political marching bands and other orms o street and perormance art and theater (such as Vermont's Bread & Puppet Theater, which provided a key source o inspiration or many marching bands) as well as underground circus and vaudeville (such as the Bindlesti Family Cirkus and Circus Contraption).

One o the best examples I can think o or how a marching band altered the composition o a situation occurred at the Foo Festival in Providence, Rhode Island, in July 2006. The event, organized by people rom AS220, a local arts space, illed the greater part o a city block while literally thousands o people milled about attending various talks and workshops, casually munching on ood, browsing through the wares o booksellers, and watching bands and musicians perorm on a stage located near one end o the estival. At several points during the day the What Cheer? Brigade, a local marching band, would materialize replete with propulsive drumming and piercing horns, resplendent in motley attire that one would be hard pressed to call uniorms. Their appearance changed the nature o the situation because as they would enter the space people would begin to dance and rolic around with them as they moved through the space, rather than staying ixed on the stage as a ocal point, one that clearly marked the dierence between those who were perorming and observing. This increase in the generalized level o conviviality aected not only those directly involved in the dancing but seemed to move beyond itsel as those around it somehow ound new reasons to converse and interact with people they hadn't spoken with beore.

The marching band may most commonly be experienced as an appendage to the state orm, as a space deined by tightly scripted and controlled lines and the military insignia. They are encountered at the military or civic parade, or perhaps as a motivational soundtrack to a sports competition. And it is perhaps this association that makes their playul détournement and reappropriation to serve other ends all the more delicious. March music might usually typically have resonance with the workings o the war machine, but as Deleuze and Guattari would remind us, this war machine can never totally be integrated into the workings o governance: there is always something that escapes. It is a process that exceeds that subject and existing communicative structures yet paradoxically one that creates a space where the possibility o transversal commonality exists. And the war machine, understood as a space o exteriority to the state, can also be understood as a trans ormation machine, as the nomadic lows and machinations that constitute spaces o possibility.

Stencil art and graiti as well as street perormances play an important role in breaking down the orms o relations created by artistic activity as separated or removed rom daily lie because they can be inscribed within the lows o people's everyday lives, in the streets, and in subways. But this does not inherently mean that such activities contain the possibility or reorienting people's expectations or will result in certain responses. And indeed, it is possible that what was once an innovative creative activity can become standardized and expected in such ways that the aectivity it initially generated is longer as intensive or eective in its workings. And i Banksy, or someone as marketable as him, should come to your town, it can drive up the real estate values as well. Even the most apparently subversive imagery can be reincorporated and recuperated back into the workings o the spectacle.

This constituent and aective space or creating new relations is not one that can be created and continue to exist without intererence or diiculty. Temporary autonomous zones are temporary or a reason—namely, the realization that attempts to create such spaces will inevitably ace repression and recuperation. Thus, it is oten not tactically sensible to create a space and maintain it (investing time, energies, and cost) against all odds. These moments and spaces are described quite well by the Leeds May Day Group as "moments o excess." One can see how with phenomena as diverse as the rise o punk and social centers to culture jamming and Critical Mass, through dierent mixes o co-optation and legal action, a space that once vibrant and ull o possibilities comes to be a bit lackluster. But the compositional capacities o these ruptures are not unlimited, or they too through repetition become ritualized and all back into solidiied patterns o circulation. The question becomes one o keeping open the aective capacities o the created space: to ind ways to avoid the traps o spectacular recuperation and the solidiication o constituent moments and possibilities into ixed and constituted orms that have lost their vitality.

It's in this way that the concept o the art strike, as originally proposed by Gustav Metzger, and then urther developed by Stewart Home and the Praxis Group in 1990–1993, becomes useul or the composition o struggles. At a juncture where capitalism is increasingly reliant on the production o new images, relations, and aects or its continued existence, [it] struggles to ind ways to intervene in these somewhat more ephemeral realms. This is why Home argues that the importance o the art strike lies less in its easibility or its concrete success, but rather in the possibility it opens up or extending and intensiying class war: "By extending and redeining traditional conceptions o the strike, the organization o the art strike intends to both increase its value both as a weapon o struggle and a means o disseminating proletarian propaganda." The withdrawal o artistic labor needs to be collective in a signiicant way to have any eect, or to only have one artist striking against the institutional machinery (as with Metzger's irst strike), or a handul (as with the Praxis Group), while quite conceptually interesting, has little in the way o eects. The withdrawal o artistic labor can only be aided by disruption o artistic production and communication by inducing o conusion and distortion o communication guerillas in the mystiications o the post-Fordist world. As the autonome a..r.i.k.a. gruppe argues, "When inormation becomes a commodity and Cultural Capital a most important asset, the distortion and devaluation o both is a direct attack against the capitalist system. To say it in a swanky way: This is Class War." This is much the tactic explored in the discussion o the IWW Starbucks campaign: insoar as the production and ambiences is central to Starbucks, monkey wrenching their image production process can be understood as industrial action. Or, as Patrick Reinsborough describes it, "direct action at the point o assumption."

This would mean to work with a sense o aesthetic politics and interventions that are not necessarily or totally based on the elements contained within the work itsel, but on understanding the possibilities created or aective relations, spaces, and interactions and their intensiication and deepening by the process o artistic creation. And to continually modiy the composition o these situations, subtracting them rom circulation and visibility, and turning toward disruption, conusion and illegibility, in the ace o recuperation. This is to understand artistic creation as what George Kubler describes as the shaping o time: art as a succession o works and productions distributed through time that embody the development o orms o collective time and relations. That is, a process that is not necessarily predicated on the creation o meaning, but as an intervention or opening into a system o relations, connecting innovations that are passed along and mutated through the modulation o the relations in which they exist, on a terrain and topology o time "where relationships rather than magnitudes are the subject o study." It is in these chains o relations that radical innovations in work, orm, idea, and practice are passed along, mutated, and linked in a succession o works embodying orms o collective time and relations.
